Dimension reduction, exact recovery, and error estimates for sparse reconstruction in phase space
M. Holler, A. Schlüter, B. Wirth

Abstract
An important theme in modern inverse problems is the reconstruction of time-dependent data from only finitely many measurements. To obtain satisfactory reconstruction results in this setting it is essential to strongly exploit temporal consistency between the different measurement times. The strongest consistency can be achieved by reconstructing data directly in phase space , the space of positions and velocities. However, this space is usually too high-dimensional for feasible computations. We introduce a novel dimension reduction technique, based on projections of phase space onto lower-dimensional subspaces, which provably circumvents this curse of dimensionality: Indeed, in the exemplary framework of superresolution we prove that known exact reconstruction results stay true after dimension reduction, and we additionally prove new error estimates of reconstructions from noisy data in optimal transport metrics which are of the same quality as one would obtain in the non-dimension-reduced case.



2020 MSC
65J22 , 44A12 , 49Q22 , 90C25 , 28A33

1
Introduction

In several practically relevant inverse problems, the objects to be reconstructed are moving,
and one is interested in information on that motion in addition to the shape and distribution of the objects themselves.
For instance, one might try to reconstruct the distribution of radioactively labelled blood cells and their velocities in a patient from positron emission tomography measurements.
Simultaneous reconstruction of objects and motions is typically a highly nonlinear task since the forward operator involves the composition of the objects with the motion.
Sometimes, though, nonlinear problems can be transformed into convex optimization problems via so-called functional lifting.
The underlying idea is to replace a
Y
-valued variable with a (probability) measure on
Y
.
In imaging, this was first applied to the Mumford–Shah functional
[
2
]
, but many other models followed.
In the context of dynamic inverse problems the approach of functional lifting was pursued by Alberti et al.
[
3
]

for the reconstruction of linear particle trajectories from indirect observations.
The resulting convex program is very high-dimensional, though.
In this work we propose a technique to reduce the number of these dimensions without sacrificing too much of the reconstruction properties.
Essentially, using different variants of the Radon transform,
we replace the original reconstruction problem by a collection of reconstruction problems with only one spatial dimension.
Here we analyse this technique completely for the reconstruction of linear particle trajectories,
including results on exact reconstruction without noise and error estimates for reconstruction from noisy data,
but the technique is more general and may in principle be applied to other settings as well.

1.1
Functional lifting and novel dimension reduction for reconstruction of linear particle trajectories

We first briefly introduce a classical method for reconstruction of stationary particles as analysed by Candès and Fernandez-Granda in
[
15
]

and then the method for moving particles proposed by Alberti et al. in
[
3
]
,
to which we finally apply our dimension reduction technique.
The presentation in this section is expository; all involved spaces and operators will be introduced more rigorously at a later point.

Stationary particles of mass
m_{i}>0
at location
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be described
as a linear combination
u=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
of Dirac measures in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
If an observation
f
of this particle configuration is obtained with a
forward
or
observation operator

\mathrm{Ob}
,
then one may try to reconstruct
u
from the observation by solving

\min_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\mathrm{Ob}u=f.

(1)

(We denote by
\mathcal{M}(X)
and
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)
the signed and the nonnegative Radon measures on some domain
X
and by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}
the total variation norm on Radon measures).
In
[
15
]
Candès and Fernandez-Granda consider the exemplary case where
u
is supported in the unit square and
\mathrm{Ob}
yields a finite number of Fourier coefficients.
In our setting, this would mean to take
\mathrm{Ob}u=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}u
, where
(\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}u)_{\xi}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi x\cdot\xi}\>\mathrm{d}u(x)
for all frequencies
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
\lVert\xi\rVert_{\infty}\leq\Phi
, but we will consider a general forward operator in our model.
Under a bound on the minimum particle distance Candès and Fernandez-Granda prove that the solution of (
1
) yields exact reconstruction of the ground truth
(in fact they even allow complex-valued masses).
In case of noisy observations
f
they replace the constraint
\mathrm{Ob}u=f
with a fidelity term and obtain error estimates for the reconstruction
[
14
]
.

In
[
3
]
Alberti et al. generalize the above reconstruction problem to the case of
moving
particles with observations at
multiple
points in time.
The motivation is twofold.
First, one hopes to gain motion information in addition to the mere particle reconstruction at the different observation times.
Second, the observations at multiple times may help to improve the reconstruction quality at every single point in time.
Alberti et al. describe a dynamic particle configuration as a linear combination
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}
of Dirac measures in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
where this time
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the initial particle position and
v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the particle velocity.
They, too, allow complex-valued masses, but our reduction technique relies to some extent on the nonnegativity of the measure
(their numerical optimization only applies to nonnegative measures as well).
In the following, we will use the notation
{g}_{\#}\nu
to denote the pushforward measure of
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
under a measurable map
g\colon X\to Y
, that is the measure on
Y
defined by
{g}_{\#}\nu(A)=\nu(g^{-1}(A))
for all measurable sets
A
.
The spatial configuration at time
t
is then obtained by a
“move”-operator
, the pushforward of
\lambda
under
(x,v)\mapsto x+tv
,

\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda={[(x,v)\mapsto x+tv]}_{\#}\lambda,

which, when applied to a discrete measure
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}
, simply yields

\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}+tv_{i}}.

Similarly to the stationary case, if at a finite number of times
t\in{\mathcal{T}}\subset\mathbb{R}
observations
f_{t}
of this spatial configuration are taken with a forward operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
,
then the authors reconstruct
\lambda
by solving

\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda=f_{t}\quad\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}}.

(2)

Based on the theory by Candès and Fernandez-Granda,
Alberti et al. show exact reconstruction of particle trajectories in the noise-free case
and in the discretized setting stability of their reconstruction with respect to noise (again replacing the equality constraint by a fidelity term).

The sought Radon measure
\lambda
lives in a high-dimensional space and thus can typically not be discretized to solve the optimization problem.
As a remedy, the numerical optimization in
[
3
]
is performed by iteratively placing Dirac measures into
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
via the Frank–Wolfe algorithm.
However, it remains challenging to generalize this algorithm to non-Dirac measures (see for instance
[
12
]
for a recent approach
that works with Dirac measures moving along absolutely continuous curves
using optimal transport regularization).
For this reason we suggest the following dimension reduction.
For a unit vector
\theta\in S^{d-1}
we introduce the
Radon transform
and the
joint Radon transform
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu={[x\mapsto\theta\cdot x]}_{\#}\nu,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\lambda={[(x,v)\mapsto(\theta\cdot x,\theta\cdot v)]}_{\#}\lambda.

Intuitively, the joint Radon transform projects the system onto the span of
\theta

so that one only observes the one-dimensional positions
\theta\cdot x
and velocities
\theta\cdot v
of any moving object.
Our new, dimension-reduced variables will then be the
snapshots

u_{t}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the system at a collection
\Sigma\supset{\mathcal{T}}
of times,

u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda,\quad t\in\Sigma,

and the collection of one-dimensional
position-velocity projections

\gamma_{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for a set
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
of directions,

\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\lambda,\quad\theta\in\Theta.

To ensure that the snapshots and position-velocity projections are compatible with each other we make use of the identity
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}
,
thus
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}
for all
t\in\Sigma
,
\theta\in\Theta
.
Collecting the position-velocity projections
\gamma_{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
\theta\in\Theta
in a variable
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
and using further that the nonnegativity of
\lambda
ensures
\|\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
, as a dimension-reduced version of (
2
) we suggest to solve

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2})\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\begin{cases}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}&\text{for all }t\in\Sigma,\theta\in\Theta,\\
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f_{t}&\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}},\end{cases}

(3)

where the slice
\gamma_{\theta}
is defined by the relation
\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\otimes\gamma_{\theta}
, with
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
a probability measure on
\Theta
.
Above,
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)^{I}
denotes the (possibly uncountably infinite) Cartesian product of spaces of nonnegative Radon measures for an index set
I
.
In the case of noisy data
f_{t}
, the constraint
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f_{t}
is again replaced with a fidelity term.
Note that
\gamma
and
u
can be interpreted as measures on the
(d+1)
-dimensional manifolds
\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\subset S^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
, respectively,
which is indeed a reduction compared to the
2d
-dimensional domain of the original variable
\lambda
.

Besides ensuring well-posedness of the dimension-reduced problem, in this work we will prove 1) exact reconstruction of particles in the noise-free case and 2) stable reconstruction of particles from noisy data with convergence rates.
The conditions for exact and for stable reconstruction are essentially the same as for the high-dimensional model (
2
),
so the dimension reduction did not cause any deterioration of the reconstruction properties.
In addition to these main results, we also prove equivalence of different variants of the reduced problem (
3
)
in which the snapshots and position-velocity projections are taken from different spaces.
The theoretical results are supported by numerical experiments.

1.2
Main results

To model a more realistic setting and to avoid technicalities associated with Radon measures on unbounded or open domains, from now on we restrict ourselves to compact domains
(though in principle one may also work with infinite domains for position, velocity, and time).
In detail, the moving particles are assumed to stay within the compact region
\Omega
,
and the sets
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}
of times and
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
of directions considered in our dimension-reduced model are also assumed compact.
Exemplary choices are
\Sigma=[-1,1]
and
\Theta=S^{d-1}
or finite sets.
For later convenience we equip both
\Sigma
and
\Theta
with probability measures
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
.
Finally, we choose a compact
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
to represent all allowed pairs of (one-dimensional) projected positions and velocities.

For simplicity we assume the forward operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
to map
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
into a Hilbert space
H
(typically finite-dimensional),
and in case of noisy data we employ the quadratic penalty term

\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}

with weight parameter
\alpha\geq 0
(our analysis will not depend on that particular choice).
For notational convenience we explicitly allow the value
\alpha=0
, in which case the fidelity term shall be interpreted as the constraint
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
.

The resulting dimension-reduced problem, including the case of noisy data, is given as

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\!\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\!+\!\frac{1}{2\alpha}\!\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}\!-\!f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\text{ s.\,t.\ }\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}\text{ for all }t\!\in\!\Sigma,\,\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\text{-a.a.\ }\theta\!\in\!\Theta.

(4)

After establishing well-posedness of the above model, our first, main results concern its reconstruction properties for
\alpha=0
.
Since the work of de Castro and Gamboa
[
16
]
and Candès and Fernandez-Granda
[
15
]
it is known
that a measure of the form
u_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}

(i. e. a finite collection of particles with masses
m_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and locations
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
can be exactly reconstructed via (
1
) even from a finite-dimensional observation
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}

as long as the particle configuration satisfies certain spatial regularity conditions depending on the forward operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}

(for instance, if
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
yields a truncated Fourier series, the particles have to satisfy a minimum distance condition).
Additionally, in case of noisy observations the reconstruction error can be quantified in terms of the noise strength
[
14
]
.
The interesting insight of Alberti et al. in
[
3
]
essentially was
that these reconstruction properties for static particle configurations
u_{t}
can be transferred
to the reconstruction of dynamic particle configurations
\lambda
via (
2
).
In detail, if
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}
represents the ground truth dynamic particle configuration
and if the snapshot
u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{d}\lambda
can be exactly reconstructed from the noise-free observation
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}

for times
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
from a small set
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
of observation times,
then model (
2
) is guaranteed to reconstruct the correct
\lambda
exactly as long as
\lambda
satisfies a certain dynamic regularity.
This means that not only the particle positions
x_{i}
and masses
m_{i}
are correctly recovered, but in addition also their velocities
v_{i}
.
The required dynamic regularity is that the snapshots
u_{t}
for
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
neither contain coincidences nor ghost particles.

Definition 1

(Coincidence and ghost particle)
.

Let
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a particle configuration
with
m_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
x_{i},v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, and let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{R}
be a set of times.

1.

Configuration
\lambda
has a
coincidence
at some time
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
if the locations of two distinct particles
i\neq j
coincide at time
t
, that is,
x_{i}+tv_{i}=x_{j}+tv_{j}
.

2.

Configuration
\lambda
admits a
ghost particle
with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

if there exists
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
outside the support of
\lambda

such that at each time
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
the ghost particle location coincides with a particle location, that is,
x+tv=x_{i}+tv_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
depending on
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.

Our contribution is to show that this behaviour persists after the dimension reduction.
In fact, we prove that in absence of coincidences or ghost particles
the model (
4
) with
\alpha=0
yields exact reconstruction of the ground truth
\gamma
and
u
in case of noise-free data
and that in case of noisy observations the reconstruction error can be quantified in terms of the noise strength.
To state the results, let the ground truth configuration and corresponding noise-free observations be labelled with
\dagger
,

\lambda^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad u_{t}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{d}\lambda^{\dagger},\quad\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\lambda^{\dagger},\quad f_{t}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}^{\dagger}.

Further, we will say that the snapshot
u_{t}^{\dagger}
can be
reconstructed exactly
from the observation
f_{t}^{\dagger}
with the observation operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}

if it is the unique solution to the static problem at the time instant
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
,

\min_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)}\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u=f^{\dagger}_{t}.

\mathrm{P}_{\text{stat}}^{t}(f^{\dagger}_{t})

To obtain dynamic exact reconstruction or a bound on the reconstruction error, the following assumption will be relevant.

Assumption 2

(Ground truth regularity)
.

Assume that there is
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
with the following properties:

1.

For all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, snapshot
u_{t}^{\dagger}
can be reconstructed exactly from
f_{t}^{\dagger}
with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
.

2.

Configuration
\lambda^{\dagger}
has no coincidences in
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.

3.

Configuration
\lambda^{\dagger}
admits no ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.

2
is the same as in
[
3
]
for model (
2
), see
Section

1.3
below for a detailed comparison.

Depending on the choice of the measure
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
, we obtain exact reconstruction results under this assumption or an even weaker version of it.

The first result in that direction relies on
2
and is applicable in particular the case that
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
is the (rescaled)
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure restricted to a relatively open subset of
S^{d-1}
.

Theorem 3

(Exact reconstruction for noise-free data – Hausdorff measure)
.

Take
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
to have a nonzero lower semi-continuous density with respect to the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
S^{d-1}
.

If
2
holds, the solution
(u,\gamma)
of (
4
) for
f=f^{\dagger}
and
\alpha=0
satisfies

1.

u_{t}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
for all
t\in\Sigma
, and

2.

\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta
.

In a second branch of results, we consider the case that
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
is a counting measure. Here we obtain exact reconstruction by only requiring that a certain number of snapshots
u_{t}^{\dagger}
can be exactly reconstructed from observations with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
(without any assumptions on ghost particles or coincidences); see
Section

3.3
.

While those results are mostly deterministic, one particular case that we highlight here shows that
most
point configuration can be reconstructed already if only three snapshots
u_{t}^{\dagger}
can be reconstructed exactly from observations with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
.

Theorem 4

(Exact reconstruction for noise-free data – Counting measure)
.

Let the location vector of
\lambda^{\dagger}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
be distributed
according to a probability distribution which is absolutely continuous with respect to the
2Nd
-dimensional Lebesgue measure, and choose
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
to be the counting measure on
d+1
directions in
\Theta
with any
d
of them being linearly independent.

If there are three times
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
at which snapshot
u_{t}^{\dagger}
can be reconstructed exactly from observations with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
, then almost surely the solution
(u,\gamma)
of (
4
) for
f=f^{\dagger}
and
\alpha=0
satisfies

1.

u_{t}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
for all
t\in\Sigma
, and

2.

\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta
.

Our final main result concerns convergence rates in case of noisy data.
The noise strength
\delta>0
of noisy observations
f^{\delta}
is for simplicity defined as the size of the data term at the ground truth,

\delta=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|f_{t}^{\delta}-f_{t}^{\dagger}\|_{H}^{2}.

The reconstruction error will be estimated using the unbalanced optimal transport cost

W_{2,R}^{2}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\inf\left\{W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\nu_{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}R^{2}\|\nu_{1}-\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}}\,\middle|\,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right\},

where
R>0
is a parameter
indicating how far mass may be transported (any mass requiring a farther transport is simply removed, paying
R^{2}
times the removed amount of mass) and

W_{2}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\inf\left\{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\dist(x,y)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\pi(x,y)\right)^{1/2}\,\middle|\,\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}),\right.\\
\left.\vphantom{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\right)^{1/p}}{[(x,y)\mapsto x]}_{\#}\pi=\nu_{1},{[(x,y)\mapsto y]}_{\#}\pi=\nu_{2}\right\}

denotes the Wasserstein-2 distance between
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The error estimates naturally require a quantitative version of
2
(
1
) that additionally encodes the stability of the reconstruction.
This is done via a particular type of source condition, which essentially states that dual variables must exist
which take any prescribed combination of the values
\{-1,1\}
on the support of the snapshot
u_{t}^{\dagger}
and behave like paraboloids in the vicinity.
Denoting the subdifferential of the total variation norm by
\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and the (pre-)dual observation operator by
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}:H\rightarrow C(\Omega)
,
the source condition reads as follows.

Definition 5

(Stably reconstructible measure)
.

We call a measure
\nu^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)

stably reconstructible
(from measurements via
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
),
if one can find
\kappa,\mu,R>0
such that for any
N
-tuple
s\in\{-1,1\}^{N}
and associated measure
\nu^{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega)
there exist dual variables
v^{\dagger},v^{s}
with

-\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}v^{\dagger}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}(\nu^{\dagger})
,
-\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}v^{s}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}(\nu^{s})
and

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}v^{\dagger}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist(x,\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})\}^{2}

\displaystyle\text{for all }x\in\Omega,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}v^{s}(x_{i})\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}v^{s}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mu\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle\text{for all }x\in B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}),\,i=1,\ldots,N.

Stable reconstructibility turns out to imply exact reconstructibility.
The main result of Candès and Fernandez-Granda in
[
14
,
15
]
essentially concerns this source condition:
if the forward operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
represents a truncated Fourier series,
then
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is stably reconstructible as soon as the minimum distance between its particles is (up to an explicit constant) no smaller than the inverse truncation frequency.
We prove the following reconstruction error estimates for noisy observations.

Theorem 6

(Reconstruction error estimates)
.

Let
(u,\gamma)
be the solution of (
4
) for
f=f^{\delta}
and
\alpha=\sqrt{\delta}
, and let
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is stably reconstructible for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Further,

1.

let
\overline{\Theta}\subset\Theta
compact such that
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
contains no coincidence or ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
for any
\theta\in\overline{\Theta}
, and

2.

let
\overline{\Sigma}\subset\Sigma
compact such that for all
t\in\overline{\Sigma}

all particles have positive mutual distance in the projections
(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t})_{\theta\in\overline{\Theta}}

and the support
\supp u^{\dagger}_{t}
can be uniquely reconstructed from
(\supp(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}))_{\theta\in\overline{\Theta}}
.

Then there exist constants
R,C>0
(depending on
\lambda^{\dagger}
,
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
,
\overline{\Theta}
and
\overline{\Sigma}
) such that

1.

we have
W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u_{t}^{\dagger})\leq C\sqrt{\delta}
for all
t\in\overline{\Sigma}
,

2.

we have
W_{2,R}^{2}(\gamma_{\theta},\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})\leq C\sqrt{\delta}
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\overline{\Theta}

For any given finite particle configuration
\lambda^{\dagger}
satisfying
2

the set of
\theta\in S^{d-1}
such that
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
contains a coincidence or ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

has vanishing
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
,
thus only a
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-nullset of directions is excluded in
6
.
Similarly, already if
\overline{\Theta}
contains just
d+1
generic directions,
the set of times
t\in\mathbb{R}
violating the conditions on
\overline{\Sigma}
is also a Lebesgue-nullset
so that again just a nullset of times is excluded in
6
.

1.3
Comparison to the state of the art

As mentioned before, in contrast to
[
3
]
we require nonnegativity of the measures to be reconstructed,
which is the cost to pay for achieving a dimension reduction (the technical reason will be pinpointed later in
71
).
From an application viewpoint, the restriction to nonnegative measures is still of significant relevance,
for instance, if the measures describe the mass distribution of fluorescent molecules in fluorescence microscopy
or if, as in
[
3
]
, they describe the distribution of ultrasonic reflectors in ultrafast ultrasonography.
This restriction is also considered elsewhere in the literature,
for instance, exact recovery and error estimates for the reconstruction of nonnegative measures are analysed in
[
16
,
17
]
.
In general it seems easier to satisfy source conditions for nonnegative measures
so that
2
(
1
) and stable reconstructibility in the sense of
5
are more easily satisfied,
see
[
17
]
.

Except for positivity,
2
is essentially the same as what is required in
[
3
]
for model (
2
): The main result of
[
3
]
also needs to exclude the existence of ghost particles, and in addition it requires the existence of static dual certificates, which implies properties
1
and
2
of
2
.

Exact reconstruction is shown in both
[
3
]
and our
3
and
4
.
In contrast, our convergence result in
6
extends the work of
[
3
]
in several ways: Besides being formulated for any dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
(instead of
d=1
), it considers the infinite-dimensional setting and an unmodified version of (
4
). In comparison,
[
3
]
shows convergence only in a discretized setting (with the involved constants being inversely proportional to the resolution of the discretization so that they blow up in the continuum limit) and for a specific type of noise model.

It is also worth noting that, while truncated Fourier measurements
\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
are most commonly used for the operator
\mathrm{Ob}
as in
[
14
,
15
,
17
,
3
]
, they represent just one possible example (admittedly, an important one for which dual variables for source conditions can most readily be constructed).
There exist alternative observation operators allowing
2
(
1
) and stable reconstructibility to be satisfied so that our theory applies:
De Castro and Gamboa list several examples of so-called generalized moments with corresponding properties for which exact reconstruction holds
[
16
]
.
In particular, our theory does not rely on the specific dual variables constructed in
[
3
]
(of which we perform a dimension reduction),
but it only relies on their properties, which also can be attained for observation operators
\mathrm{Ob}
other than truncated Fourier measurements.

1.4
Paper outline

The model and corresponding operators will be introduced in detail in
Section

2
. Further, in
A.1
, different alternative formulations will be introduced and their equivalence will be proven.

Section

3
contains the proofs of
3
and
4
and some variants as well as a discussion of how big a direction set
\Theta
is useful,
which may be interesting from the viewpoint of numerical effort.
The error estimation results from
6
and its variants for finite
\Theta
are derived in
Section

4

based on a general strategy of how to obtain unbalanced optimal transport error estimates for inverse problems of measures via convex duality techniques.
The model is implemented and illustrated numerically in
Section

5
.

1.5
Notation and preliminaries

In this paragraph we recall the (standard) notion of Radon measures and collect some properties of measures
before finally providing a list of symbols used throughout the article.
While different results in this section on measures on a space
X
require different assumptions on
X
, we note that in our application
X
will always be either a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or of the sphere
S^{d-1}
with the induced norm or metric, respectively. Consequently,
X
will always be a separable, complete,
\sigma
-compact metric space such that all of the below results are applicable to
X
.

Definition 7

(Borel and Radon measure, total variation)
.

Let
X
be a topological space. A
real Borel measure
is a countably-additive set function
\nu:\mathcal{B}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, with
\mathcal{B}(X)
being the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
X
. The
total variation
of a real Borel measure
\nu:\mathcal{B}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the nonnegative Borel measure defined as

|\nu|(E)=\sup\left\{\sum_{i}|\nu(E_{i})|\,\middle|\,E_{1},E_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{B}(X)\text{ are pairwise disjoint},\,E=\bigcup_{i}E_{i}\right\}.

A
finite Radon measure
is a real Borel measure whose total variation is a regular measure. We denote by
\mathcal{M}(X)
the space of finite Radon measures on
X
and by
\mathcal{M}_{+}(X)\subset\mathcal{M}(X)
the set of nonnegative finite Radon measures.

Note that
\mathcal{M}(X)
equipped with the norm
\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}}=|\nu|(X)
is a normed vector space.

Theorem 8

(Duality,
[
22
, Thm. 6.19]
)
.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space. Then, the space
\mathcal{M}(X)
can be identified with the dual space of
C_{0}(X)
, the latter being the completion of the space of compactly supported continuous functions with respect to the supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. The duality pairing is given as

\langle\nu,\phi\rangle=\int_{X}\phi(x)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x),

and we have

\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\sup_{\phi\in C_{0}(X),\|\phi\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\int_{\Omega}\phi(x)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x).

In particular,
\mathcal{M}(X)
is a Banach space.

In accordance with the duality
\mathcal{M}(X)=C_{0}(X)^{*}
we will denote the weak-
\ast
convergence of a sequence
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{M}(X)
to some
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
by
\nu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\nu
as
n\to\infty
.
In addition, we will also need the notion of
narrow convergence
: We say that
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots
converges to
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
with respect to the narrow topology and write
\nu_{n}\overset{\textbf{n}}{\rightharpoonup}\nu
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{X}\varphi(x)\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{n}(x)=\int_{X}\varphi(x)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

for all
\varphi\in C^{b}(X)
, the latter denoting the Banach space of bounded, continuous functions on
X
equipped with the supremum norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. Obviously, narrow convergence is a stronger property than weak-
\ast
convergence. We will, however, also require equivalence of the two notions for certain compactly supported sequences of measures. To this end, we first define the support of Radon measures and provide some basic properties.

Definition 9

(Support of a measure)
.

The
support
of a real Borel measure
\nu:\mathcal{B}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on a topological space
X
is defined as

\supp(\nu)=\left\{x\in X\,\middle|\,|\nu|(B)>0\text{ for each neighbourhood }B\text{ of }x\right\}.

Proposition 10

(Support properties)
.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
. Then the following holds.

•

\supp(\nu)
is closed, and
\nu
is concentrated on
\supp(\nu)
, that is,
\nu(E)=\nu(E\cap\supp(\nu))
for all
E\in\mathcal{B}(X)
.

•

If
\nu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\nu
in
\mathcal{M}(X)
as
n\to\infty
and if
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset S
for all
n
with
S\subset X
closed, then
\supp(\nu)\subset S
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\supp(\nu)
is the complement of the union of all open sets
V
with
|\nu|(V)=0
, thus it is closed. Also,
|\nu|(V)=0
for each open
V\subset X\setminus\supp(\nu)
, which implies
|\nu|(V)=|\nu|(V\setminus\supp(\nu))+|\nu|(V\cap\supp(\nu))=|\nu|(V\cap\supp(\nu))
for any
V
open. Using the latter, it is immediate to show that for any
E\in\mathcal{B}(X)
with
E\subset X\setminus\supp(\nu)
,

0\leq\inf\{|\nu|(V)\,\left|\right.\,E\subset V,\,V\text{ open }\}\leq\inf\{|\nu|(V)\,\left|\right.\,E\subset V,\,V\text{ open},\,V\subset X\setminus\supp(\nu)\}=0,

which, by outer regularity, implies that
\nu(E)=|\nu|(E)=0
. Hence,
\nu(E)=\nu(E\cap\supp(\nu))
also for each
E\in\mathcal{B}(X)
as claimed.

Now assume
\nu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\nu
in
\mathcal{M}(X)
with
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset S
as above. Then, for
x\notin S
,
B=X\setminus S
is an open neighbourhood of
x
, and for any
\varphi\in C_{0}(B)
it follows that

\int_{B}\varphi\>\mathrm{d}\nu=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{B}\varphi\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{n}=0.

By
8
(noting that
B
is again a locally compact Hausdorff space as being an open subset of
X
) this implies

0=\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}(B)}=|\nu|(B)

such that
x\notin\supp(\nu)
.
∎

Now a basic equivalence result on weak-
\ast
convergence and narrow convergence holds as follows.

Lemma 11

(Equivalence of weak-
\ast
and narrow convergence)
.

Let
X
be a complete separable metric space and
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots
a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(X)
weakly-
\ast
converging to
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
such that
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset S
with
S\subset X
compact. Then
\supp(\nu)\subset S
, and
\nu_{n}
converges to
\nu
also narrowly.

Proof.

The fact that
\supp(\nu)\subset S
was shown in Proposition
10
. Convergence with respect to the narrow topology is a direct consequence of Prohorovs theorem (see
[
8
, Thm. 8.6.2]
) and the fact that the sequence
\nu_{n}
is uniformly tight (see
[
8
, Def. 8.6.1]
).
∎

In case
X
is a metric space and
\sigma
-compact (that is, the countable union of compact sets), any real Borel measure is regular and thus a finite Radon measure.

Lemma 12

(Borel and Radon measure)
.

Let
X
be a
\sigma
-compact metric space. Then any real Borel measure on
\Omega
is regular.

Proof.

Let
\nu
be a real Borel measure on
X
. We need to show that the real Borel measure
|\nu|
is regular.
This follows from
[
20
, Thm. II.1.2]
together with the finiteness of
|\nu|
and the fact that,
due to
\sigma
-compactness, the compact sets in the definition of inner regularity can be replaced by closed sets. Indeed, obviously the supremum gets larger if we replace compactness by closedness,
while the other inequality follows from the fact that for any sequence of compact sets
K_{1},K_{2},\ldots
with
X=\bigcup_{i}K_{i}
and any closed
A\in\mathcal{B}(\Omega)
we have

|\nu|(A)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}|\nu|(A_{n})
with
A_{n}=A\cap\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}K_{i}
compact.
∎

Definition 13

(Pushforward measure)
.

For
(X,\Sigma_{X})
,
(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
measure spaces and
f:X\rightarrow Y
a measurable function, we define the
pushforward
of a measure
\nu:\Sigma_{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
as

{f}_{\#}\nu:\Sigma_{Y}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\},\quad A\mapsto\nu(f^{-1}(A))\,.

Theorem 14

(Basic properties of pushforward measures)
.

With the notation of
13
,
{f}_{\#}\nu
is a measure on
(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
, and a measurable function
g:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is integrable with respect to
{f}_{\#}\nu
if
g\circ f
is integrable with respect to
\nu
. If
\nu
is nonnegative, also the converse holds true. If both are integrable, it holds that

\int_{Y}g(y)\>\mathrm{d}{f}_{\#}\nu(y)=\int_{X}g(f(x))\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x).

(5)

Further, if
Y
is a
\sigma
-compact metric space, then
{f}_{\#}\nu
is a finite Radon measure on
\Sigma_{Y}
if
\nu
is a finite Radon measure on
\Sigma_{X}
.

Proof.

The fact that
{f}_{\#}\nu
is a measure and the assertion on integrability are shown in
[
9
, Thm. 3.6.1 and below]
. The fact that
{f}_{\#}\nu
is a finite Radon measure is immediate from
12
, since
Y
is a
\sigma
-compact metric space by assumption.
∎

Proposition 15

(The pushforward as operator)
.

Let
X,Y
be locally compact Hausdorff spaces,
X
be further a complete, separable,
\sigma
-compact metric space and
f:X\rightarrow Y
be continuous. Then

{f}_{\#}:\mathcal{M}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(Y),\;\nu\mapsto{f}_{\#}\nu

is a well-defined linear operator with
\|{f}_{\#}\|\leq 1
. Further, the following holds.

•

If
\nu\geq 0
, then
{f}_{\#}\nu\geq 0
and
\|{f}_{\#}\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}(Y)}=\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}(X)}
.

•

{f}_{\#}
is continuous with respect to narrow convergence of measures.

•

\supp({f}_{\#}\nu)\subset\overline{f(\supp(\nu))}
.

•

If
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{M}(X)
are such that
\nu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
and
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset S
with
S
compact, then
\supp({f}_{\#}\nu)\subset f(S)
and
({f}_{\#}\nu)_{n}\overset{\textbf{n}}{\rightharpoonup}{f}_{\#}\nu
.

Proof.

Linearity and the boundedness of
{f}_{\#}
follow from equality (
5
), which holds for any
g\in C_{0}(Y)
. Likewise, continuity with respect to narrow convergence follows from (
5
) being true for any bounded continuous function
g
. Positivity and norm preservation in case of positive
\nu
is immediate from the definition of the pushforward.

For showing the support inclusions, again we exploit that
\supp(\nu)
is the complement of the union of all open sets
V
with
|\nu|(V)=0
.
Take
x\in B:=Y\setminus\overline{f(\supp(\nu))}
. Then
B
is open, and for any
E\subset B
it follows that

{f}_{\#}\nu(E)=\nu(f^{-1}(E))=0
since
f^{-1}(E)\subset X\setminus\supp(\nu)
and
\nu
is concentrated on
\supp(\nu)
. This implies that
|{f}_{\#}\nu|(B)=0
and hence
x\notin\supp({f}_{\#}\nu)
, which shows the inclusion
\supp({f}_{\#}\nu)\subset\overline{f(\supp(\nu))}
.

The last statement is an immediate consequence of
11
and continuity of the pushforward with respect to narrow convergence.
∎

Definition 16

(Product measure)
.

Let
Y,Z
be topological spaces,
\omega:\mathcal{B}(Z)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a real Borel measure and
(\nu_{z})_{z\in Z}
with
\nu_{z}:\mathcal{B}(Y)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a family of real Borel measures such that for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(Y)
,
Z\ni z\mapsto\nu_{z}(A)
is
\omega
-measurable. Then we define the
product measure

\nu_{z}{\otimes}_{z}\omega:\mathcal{B}(Y\times Z)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

on the generator
\{A\times B\,\left|\right.\,A\in\mathcal{B}(Y),\,B\in\mathcal{B}(Z)\}
of
\mathcal{B}(Y\times Z)
via

(\nu_{z}{\otimes}_{z}\omega)(A\times B)=\int_{B}\nu_{z}(A)\>\mathrm{d}\omega(z).

Similarly, we use the notation
\omega{\otimes}_{z}\nu_{z}
for a corresponding product measure on
\mathcal{B}(Z\times Y)
.

Note that, indeed, the product measure is a well-defined,
\sigma
-additive set function on
\mathcal{B}(Y\times Z)
and, by
12
, is a finite Radon measure in case
Y
and
Z
are
\sigma
-compact metric spaces. Also, note that, for the sake of brevity, we use the term “product measure” while the above measure is actually a product measure with transition kernel
k:Z\times\mathcal{B}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
k(z,A):=\nu_{z}(A)
. It generalizes the standard product measure
\nu\otimes\omega
in the sense that the latter is obtained in the special case that
\nu_{z}=\nu
independent of
z
.

Of particular interest in this work will be the case that
\nu_{z}={[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu
, with
(f_{z})_{z\in Z}
a given family of functions. In this case, the product measure
{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega
can equivalently be written as
{F}_{\#}(\nu\otimes\omega)
with
F:X\times Z\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
F(x,z):=(f_{z}(x),z)
. In case the mapping
(x,z)\mapsto f_{z}(x)
is continuous, this allows us to directly transfer the properties of the pushforward as stated in
15
to the mapping
(\nu,\omega)\mapsto{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega
.
In the following proposition, we summarize these properties for the special case that
\omega\geq 0
with
\omega(Z)=1
is fixed, as this is the setting that is relevant for our analysis in the subsequent sections below.

Proposition 17

(Properties of the product measure)
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be
\sigma
- and locally compact metric spaces with
Y,Z
being complete and separable,
\omega\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(Z)
and
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
be finite Radon measures with
\omega(Z)=1
, and
(f_{z})_{z\in Z}
be a family of functions
f_{z}:X\rightarrow Y
such that
(x,z)\mapsto f_{z}(x)
is continuous.

Then the product measure
{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega:\mathcal{B}(Y\times Z)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is well-defined, a finite Radon measure on
Y\times Z
, and the following holds.

•

If
\nu\geq 0
, then
{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega\geq 0
and
\|{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega\|_{\mathcal{M}(Y\times Z)}=\|\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}(X)}
.

•

\nu\mapsto{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega
is continuous with respect to narrow convergence of measures.

•

\supp({[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega)\subset\overline{F(\supp(\nu)\times\supp(\omega))}
.

•

Assume that
Z
is compact. Then, if
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots\in\mathcal{M}(X)
are such that
\nu_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
and
\supp(\nu_{n})\subset S
with
S
compact, it follows that
\supp({[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega)\subset F(S\times Z)
and
{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu_{n}{\otimes}_{z}\omega(z)\overset{\textbf{n}}{\rightharpoonup}{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu{\otimes}_{z}\omega(z)
.

The same holds true for the product measure
\omega{\otimes}_{z}{[f_{z}]}_{\#}\nu:\mathcal{B}(Z\times Y)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The first two items are an immediate consequence of
15
, and the third one follows from
15
by observing that
\supp(\nu\otimes\omega)\subset\supp(\nu)\times\supp(\omega)
, which can be deduced directly from the definition of the support.
Given the third statement, the fourth is again a direct consequence of
15
.
∎

2
The reconstruction model and its properties

We aim to recover the state of particles in a compact domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(for instance
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
) at different times within a compact temporal domain
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}
, for example
\Sigma=[-1,1]
or
\Sigma
being a finite collection of points. To this end, we assume that measurements are available at finitely many different points in time
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
, where
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}}\subset\Sigma
and
|{\mathcal{T}}|<\infty
.

Our reconstruction approach uses lifted measures on the product space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as well as Radon-transform-based projections thereof. For the latter, we denote by
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
a compact set of projection directions, where we have either
\Theta=S^{d-1}
or
\Theta
being a finite set of directions in
S^{d-1}
in mind.
As domain for lifted measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we introduce the set

\Lambda:=\left\{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\middle|\,x+tv\in\Omega\text{ for all }t\in\Sigma\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(6)

This ensures that no particle leaves or enters the domain during the whole duration of the experiment.

Remark 18

(Size of the domains)
.

In practice, the region of interest (that region which is observed by the forward operator) will be a small area in the centre of
\Omega

so that particles may leave or enter the observed region, but can still be represented in
\Lambda
.
Alternatively, to avoid a too strong enlargement of the domain around the region of interest (which would be accompanied by an increased computational effort)
one could also modify the involved operators such that all mass exiting the region of interest is represented in an auxiliary point reservoir.

Projections of lifted measures, the position-velocity projections, are defined on the set

\Gamma:=\{(\theta\cdot x,\theta\cdot v)\,\left|\right.\,(x,v)\in\Lambda,\,\theta\in\Theta\}.

We will also require a domain for projections of measures living in
\Omega
that we denote by

\Xi:=\{\theta\cdot x\,\left|\right.\,x\in\Omega,\,\theta\in\Theta\}.

Note that all the sets
\Lambda
,
\Gamma
and
\Xi
are compact as being the image of compacta under continuous functions.

The sets
\Omega,\Sigma,\Theta,\Lambda,\Gamma
and
\Xi
are equipped with the standard metric of the underlying spaces
\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R},S^{d-1},\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively, rendering them to be compact, separable metric spaces.
Further, in order to define integrals of continuous functions on them, these spaces are equipped with the Borel measures
\mathcal{L}^{d},\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma},\mathcal{H}_{\Theta},\mathcal{L}^{d}\times\mathcal{L}^{d},\mathcal{L}^{2}
and
\mathcal{L}^{1}
, respectively, where
\mathcal{L}^{n}
denotes the standard Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(restricted to the underlying set) and
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
,
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
are general Borel probability measures such that
\Theta=\supp\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
. For the latter two, we have in mind in particular the case of discrete sets with counting measures
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma},\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
and the case of sets with non-empty relative interior and the (rescaled)
1
-dimensional Lebesgue measure
\mathcal{L}^{1}
or the (rescaled)
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
(for
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
, respectively).

Besides the space of Radon measures on sets like
\Omega,\Sigma,\Theta,\Lambda,\Gamma
and
\Xi
and products thereof, we will also deal with product spaces of the form

\mathcal{M}(X)^{I}=\{(\nu_{i})_{i\in I}\,\left|\right.\,\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(X)\text{ for all }i\in I\},

where
I
is a (possibly uncountable) index set. While such sets can be generically equipped with the standard product topology (that is, the coarsest topology such that all projections to a single component are continuous), we will mostly not use any topology on these spaces but rather deal only with individual elements
\nu_{i}
for
i\in I
.
For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)^{\Sigma}
we will denote the individual elements by
\nu_{t}
and for
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X)^{\Theta}
by
\nu_{\theta}
.

We will also consider product spaces for finite index sets
J
, such as
J={\mathcal{T}}
. To highlight the difference, we will denote such spaces as
Z^{|J|}
, where
|J|
defines the number of elements in
J
. In particular, we will deal with measurements
f=(f_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}
in a finite product of Hilbert spaces denoted by

H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
.

2.1
Radon and move operators and their properties

We will need the following transform operators.

Definition 19

(Radon operators)
.

•

With
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu:={[x\mapsto x\cdot\theta]}_{\#}\nu
, the
Radon transform

\mathrm{Rd}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R})
is defined as

\mathrm{Rd}\nu=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu.

•

With
\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}\nu:={[(x,t)\mapsto(x\cdot\theta,t)]}_{\#}\nu
, the
timewise Radon transform

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma)
is defined as

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}\nu=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}\nu.

•

With
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\nu:={[(x,y)\mapsto(x\cdot\theta,y\cdot\theta)]}_{\#}\nu
, the
joint Radon transform

\mathrm{Rj}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
is defined as

\mathrm{Rj}\nu=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\nu.

Remark 20

(Properties of Radon operators)
.

It follows from
14
,
15
and
17
that all Radon operators define bounded linear operators with norm bounded by
1
, that they map nonnegative measures to nonnegative measures of same norm, and that they are continuous with respect to narrow convergence of measures. Further, as can be seen by direct computation, they map
L^{1}
-functions (regarded as a subset of Radon measures) to
L^{1}
-functions, and the following explicit representations hold true almost everywhere,

\displaystyle\nu\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu(s)=\int_{\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\left|\right.\,x\cdot\theta=s\}}\nu(x)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(x),

\displaystyle\nu\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma):

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}\nu(s,t)=\int_{\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\left|\right.\,x\cdot\theta=s\}}\nu(x,t)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(x),

\displaystyle\nu\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}):

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\nu(s,r)=\int_{\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\left|\right.\,x\cdot\theta=s,\,y\cdot\theta=r\}}\nu(x,y)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2d-2}(x,y),

where
\mathcal{H}^{n}
denotes the
n
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.

Remark 21

(Decomposition of Radon operators)
.

A straightforward computation shows that, in case
\nu=\nu_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)
,
the Radon operators as above can be decomposed as

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}\nu=\mathrm{Rd}\nu_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}=(\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu_{t}){\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}.

In the variational problem setting considered later on, we will only deal with measures with support contained in a compact set and exploit weak-
\ast
sequential compactness properties. In such a setting,
15
and
17
provide a characterization of the support of the Radon transforms of such measures and corresponding weak-
\ast
continuity assertions, which are summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 22

(Radon operators for compactly supported measures)
.

With
\theta\in\Theta
arbitrary, we have the following.

•

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\supp(\nu)\subset\Omega
it holds that

\supp(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu)\subset\Xi
,
\supp(\mathrm{Rd}\nu)\subset\Theta\times\Xi
,
and, using zero extension, the operator restrictions

\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Xi)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Rd}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)

are weakly-
\ast
continuous.

•

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)
with
\supp(\nu)\subset\Omega\times\Sigma
it holds that

\supp(\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}\nu)\subset\Xi\times\Sigma
,
\supp(\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}\nu)\subset\Theta\times\Xi\times\Sigma
,
and, using zero extension, the operator restrictions

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega\times\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Xi\times\Sigma)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega\times\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi\times\Sigma)

are weakly-
\ast
continuous.

•

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\supp(\nu)\subset\Lambda
it holds that

\supp(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\nu)\subset\Gamma
,
\supp(\mathrm{Rj}\nu)\subset\Theta\times\Gamma
,
and, using zero extension, the operator restrictions

\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}:\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Rj}:\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)

are weakly-
\ast
continuous.

Remark 23

(Alternative definition of Radon operators)
.

Note that an alternative definition of
\mathrm{Rd}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R})
(and similarly for
\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}
and
\mathrm{Rj}
) could be the dual of the operator
\mathrm{Rd}^{*}:C_{0}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R})\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given as

\mathrm{Rd}^{*}\varphi(x)=\int_{\Theta}\varphi(\theta,x\cdot\theta)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta).

While this would directly yield weak-
\ast
continuity of
\mathrm{Rd}
also for measures on unbounded domains, it requires the measure
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
to be absolutely continuous with respect to the standard Hausdorff measure on the sphere
S^{d-1}
. Indeed, the crucial point here is to show that
\ x\mapsto\int_{\Theta}\varphi(\theta,x\cdot\theta)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)
indeed vanishes at infinity, which can be done as in
[
10
, Lemma 1]
in case of absolute continuity of
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
, but does not hold true for instance if
\Theta
is finite and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
is the counting measure.

We will also need injectivity of the Radon operator
\mathrm{Rd}
acting on measures. As in the classical setting with Schwartz functions, this can be obtained from a Fourier slice theorem provided that sufficiently many directions
\theta\in\Theta
are measured.

Definition 24

(Fourier transform)
.

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the Fourier transform

\mathcal{F}(\nu)(\xi)=\hat{\nu}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}x\cdot\xi}\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

and the inverse Fourier transform

\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\nu)(\xi)=\check{\nu}(\xi)=(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}x\cdot\xi}\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x)

Note that, since
x\mapsto e^{\pm ix\cdot\xi}
is continuous and bounded,
\hat{\nu}
and
\check{\nu}
are well-defined and are uniformly continuous, bounded functions.

The Fourier slice theorem relates the Fourier transform and the Radon transform.

Proposition 25

(Fourier slice theorem)
.

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\widehat{R_{\theta}\nu}(\sigma)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}s\sigma}\>\mathrm{d}R_{\theta}\nu(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}x\cdot\theta\sigma}\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x)=\hat{\nu}(\theta\sigma)\qquad\text{for all }\sigma\in\mathbb{R},\,\theta\in S^{d-1}.

This allows to reduce injectivity of the Radon transform to injectivity of the Fourier transform on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Proposition 26

(Injectivity of Radon operators)
.

If
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
has a (nonzero) lower semi-continuous density with respect to the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
S^{d-1}
, then
the Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R})
is injective.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{Rd}\nu=0
for
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we obtain that
R_{\theta}\nu=0
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta\in\Theta
and, from the Fourier slice theorem, that
\hat{\nu}(\theta\sigma)=0
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta\in\Theta
and every
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. Now using the assumption on
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
, there exists a point
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
such that the density of
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
does not vanish in a neighbourhood of
\theta
in
S^{d-1}
.
This implies that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x)=0
for any
\varphi\in A:=\vspan(\{x\mapsto e^{-{\mathrm{i}}\sigma\theta\cdot x}\,\left|\right.\,\sigma\in\mathbb{R},\theta\in S^{d-1},\,\|\theta-\theta_{0}\|<\epsilon\})
,
with
\epsilon>0
suitably chosen. Now given any
\psi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
A
is a subalgebra of
C(\supp(\psi))
that separates points and contains the constant one function, thus by the Stone–Weierstrass Theorem,
A
is dense in
C(\supp(\psi))
. Approximating
\psi
with elements in
A
we thus obtain that also
\langle\psi,\nu\rangle=0
and, as
\psi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
was arbitrary, it follows from density that
\nu=0
.
∎

Next, we introduce two different operators that project lifted measures to a time-series.

Definition 27

(Move operators)
.

Take
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

With
\mathrm{Mv}^{n}_{t}\nu:={[(x,v)\mapsto x+tv]}_{\#}\nu
, the
move operator

\mathrm{Mv}^{n}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Sigma)
is defined as

\mathrm{Mv}^{n}\nu=\mathrm{Mv}^{n}_{t}\nu{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}.

We set
\mathrm{Mv}:=\mathrm{Mv}^{1}
.

•

With
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{n}_{t}}\nu:={[(\theta,x,v)\mapsto(\theta,x+tv)]}_{\#}\nu
, the
anglewise move operator

\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{n}}:\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\Sigma)
is defined as

\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{n}}\nu=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{n}_{t}}\nu{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}.

We set
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}:=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}
.

Remark 28

(Properties of move operators)
.

Again, it follows from
14
,
15
and
17
that all move operators define bounded linear operators with norm bounded by
1
, that they map nonnegative measures to nonnegative measures of same norm, and that they are continuous with respect to narrow convergence of measures. Just like for the Radon operators, they map
L^{1}
-functions to
L^{1}
-functions with the following explicit representations almost everywhere,

\displaystyle\nu\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}):

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\nu(z)=\int_{\{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\left|\right.\,x+tv=z\}}\nu(x,v)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x,v),

\displaystyle\nu\in L^{1}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}):

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\nu(\theta,z)=\int_{\{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\,\left|\right.\,x+tv=z\}}\nu(\theta,x,v)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{n}(x,v).

Remark 29

(Decomposition of move operators)
.

Again, a straightforward computation shows that in case
\nu=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\nu_{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
the move operators as above can be decomposed as

\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{n}}\nu=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}^{n}\nu_{\theta}=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}(\mathrm{Mv}^{n}_{t}\nu_{\theta}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}).

Again, we are interested in the restriction of move operators to measures on bounded domains and corresponding weak-
\ast
continuity assertions, for which
15
and
17
provide several properties which are summarized in the following
Section
.

Proposition 30

(Move operators for compactly supported measures)
.

With
t\in\Sigma
arbitrary, we have the following.

•

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\supp(\nu)\subset\Lambda
it holds that
\supp(\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\nu)\subset\Omega
,
\supp(\mathrm{Mv}^{d}\nu)\subset\Omega\times\Sigma
, and, using zero extension, the operator restrictions

\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}:\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Omega)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Mv}^{d}:\mathcal{M}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Omega\times\Sigma)

are weakly-
\ast
continuous.

•

For
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\supp(\nu)\subset\Theta\times\Gamma
it holds that
\supp(\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\nu)\subset\Theta\times\Xi
,
\supp(\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\nu)\subset\Theta\times\Xi\times\Sigma
, and, using zero extension, the operator restrictions

\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}:\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}:\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi\times\Sigma)

are weakly-
\ast
continuous.

Remark 31

(Alternative definition of move operators)
.

As with the Radon operators, an alternative definition of
\mathrm{Mv}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)
(and similarly for
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}
) could be the dual of the operator
\mathrm{Mv}^{*}:C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
given as

\mathrm{Mv}^{*}\varphi(x,v)=\int_{\Sigma}\varphi(x+tv,t)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}(t),

which in particular implies weak-
\ast
continuity of
\mathrm{Mv}
also for measures on unbounded domains. However, this requires to show that
(x,v)\mapsto\int_{\Sigma}\varphi(x+tv,t)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}(t)
indeed vanishes at infinity. Again, while this can be done with similar techniques as in
[
10
, Lemma 1]
in case of absolute continuity of
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
, it does not hold true for instance if
\Sigma
is finite and
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
is the counting measures.

The following
Section
provides a commutation rule for certain Radon and move operators.

Lemma 32

(Commutation of Radon and move operators)
.

It holds that

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\mathrm{Rj}\quad\text{as well as}\quad\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R},\theta\in S^{d-1}.

Proof.

Take
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\varphi\in C_{0}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma)
. Then

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}\nu,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Theta}\varphi(\theta,\theta\cdot(x+ty),t)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}(t)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Theta}\int_{\Sigma}\varphi(\theta,(\theta\cdot x)+t(\theta\cdot y),t)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}(t)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Theta}(\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}^{*}\varphi)(\theta,\theta\cdot x,\theta\cdot y)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathrm{Rj}^{*}\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}^{*}\varphi)(x,y)\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x,y)=\langle\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\mathrm{Rj}\nu,\varphi\rangle,

where we applied Fubini’s theorem since both
(\Sigma,\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma})
and
(\Theta,\mathcal{H}_{\Theta})
are finite measure spaces by assumption. The equality
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R},\theta\in S^{d-1}
follows similarly.
∎

We will also need that the constraint
\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma\quad\text{for all }t\in\Sigma
for
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\Sigma}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
provides a decomposition of
\gamma
as in the following lemma.

Lemma 33

(Measure decomposition with projected lifting)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\Sigma}
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
be such that

\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma\quad\text{for all }t\in\Sigma

Then
\gamma
can be decomposed as

\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}

with
(\gamma_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
a family of measures in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
. Further,
\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for all
t\in\Sigma
and

\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}

for every
t
and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
.

Proof.

At first, by the properties of the Radon and move operator,
\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma
implies that
\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and

\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma

for all
t\in\Sigma
. Now using the disintegration theorem
[
5
, Thm. 5.3.1]
, we decompose
\gamma=\nu_{\gamma}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}
with
\nu_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}}{(\pi^{\Theta})}_{\#}\gamma
a probability measure and
\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for almost every
\theta
. Here,
\pi^{\Theta}:\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\Theta
denotes the canonical projection.
From this decomposition it follows by a direct computation that

\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\nu_{\gamma}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}.

Hence we obtain

\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\nu_{\gamma}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}

for all
t\in\Sigma
. Evaluating the measures on both sides
at
\omega\times\mathbb{R}
for
\omega\subset\Theta
an arbitrary Borel set leads to
\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\nu_{\gamma}(\omega)=\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\omega)
for
t\in\Sigma
arbitrary and thus
\nu_{\gamma}=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
. This proves the decomposition of
\gamma
.
In order to show that
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}
for every
t
and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
, fix
t
and note that we have already obtained that, for every
A\subset\mathbb{R}
and
\omega\subset\Theta
,

\int_{\omega}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}(A)-\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}(A)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)=0.

In particular, this implies that, for any
A\subset\mathbb{R}
there exists
\omega_{A}\subset\Theta
with
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\omega_{A})=0
such that
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}(A)-\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}(A)=0
for all
\theta\in\Theta\setminus\omega_{A}
. Now taking
(A_{n})_{n}
to be a countable generator of the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}
,
\omega_{A_{n}}\subset\Theta
with
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\omega_{A_{n}})=0
such that
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}({A_{n}})-\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}({A_{n}})=0
for all
\theta\in\Theta\setminus\omega_{A_{n}}
, and
\omega=\bigcup_{n}\omega_{A_{n}}
, it follows that
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}({A_{n}})-\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}({A_{n}})=0
for every
n
and
\theta\in\Theta\setminus\omega
with
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\omega)=0
, and, consequently, that
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}
for every
\theta\in\Theta\setminus\omega
as claimed.
∎

Finally, we also obtain an injectivity result on the move operator as follows.

Proposition 34

(Injectivity of move operators)
.

If

\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
has a (nonzero) lower semi-continuous density with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}

the move operator
\mathrm{Mv}:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma)
is injective.

Proof.

Analogously to the Fourier slice theorem, we first observe for
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
, every
t\in\Sigma
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
the equality

\widehat{\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\nu}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}sr}\>\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\nu(r)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}s(x+tv)}\>\mathrm{d}\nu(x,v)=\hat{\nu}(s,st),

from which injectivity again follows as in the proof of
26
.
∎

Finally we introduce the operators providing the observations of the particle configurations.

Definition 35

(Observation operators)
.

With
H
a Hilbert space, for each
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
we set

\mathrm{Ob}_{t}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)\rightarrow H
to be an
observation operator
that is continuous with respect to the weak-
\ast
topology in
\mathcal{M}(\Omega)
and
H

(or equivalently, that is the dual to a bounded linear operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}:H\rightarrow C(\Omega)
).
The observation of a configuration
u\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}
is then given as

u\mapsto(\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}.

For the purpose of this article, the exact form of
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
does not matter so much
– as long as it leads to good reconstructions for the static problem (
1
), it will do so as well for our proposed dimension-reduced model.
Typically the Hilbert space
H
of measurements is finite-dimensional.
The example considered in
[
15
]
is a truncated Fourier series.

Example 36

(Truncated Fourier series observation)
.

Assume
\Omega\subset(0,1)^{d}
, then one can interpret
[0,1)^{d}
with periodic boundary conditions as the flat torus and take
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
to be a Fourier series,
truncated at some maximum frequency
\Phi\geq 0
,
i.e.
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
where

\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}u\coloneqq\left(\int_{\Omega}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}2\pi x\cdot\xi}\>\mathrm{d}u(x)\right)_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\lVert\xi\rVert_{\infty}\leq\Phi}.

2.2
Problem setting and dimensionality reduction

As outlined in
Section

1.1
, the approach of Alberti et al. in
[
3
]
for reconstructing a measure
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}
(describing the masses
m_{i}
, initial positions
x_{i}
and velocities
v_{i}
of a finite number of particles) from data
(f_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\in H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
can be written as

\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)}\|\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda=f_{t}\quad\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}}.

(7)

Using that both
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{d}
as well as
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
are weakly-
\ast
continuous, it follows by standard arguments that a solution to (
7
) exists. Further, in the case that each
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
samples a finite number of Fourier coefficients and under a separation condition on the particle distribution, Alberti et al. show exact reconstruction of particle trajectories in the noise-free case and, in the discretized setting, stability of the reconstruction approach with respect to noise (replacing the equality constraint by a fidelity term).

Our focus here is on reformulations of (
7
) that replace the high-dimensional unknown
\lambda
, which lives in a space of dimension
2d
, by unknowns defined in spaces of dimension at most
d+1
. To this end we first introduce explicit variables
u_{t}
(which we term
snapshots
) for the spatial particle configuration at different times
t
in the extended time-domain
\Sigma\supset{\mathcal{T}}
. With those we can write (
7
) equivalently as

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\|\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{d}\lambda=u_{t}\quad\text{for all }t\in\Sigma,\\
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f_{t}\quad\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(8)

Indeed, the problems are equivalent since, for
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)
,
\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
for any choice of
t
.

Now we further reformulate the above problem by applying the Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}
on both sides of the constraint
\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda=u_{t}
. Using that
\mathrm{Rd}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\mathrm{Rj}
(see
32
) and introducing
\gamma=\mathrm{Rj}\lambda
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)\\
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\left\{\begin{aligned} \overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma&=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}\quad&&\text{for all }t\in\Sigma,\\
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}&=f_{t}\quad&&\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}},\\
\mathrm{Rj}\lambda&=\gamma.\end{aligned}\right.

(9)

Using that
\|\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\mathrm{Rj}\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)
, this problem is equivalent to (
7
) whenever
\Theta
is such that
\mathrm{Rd}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)
is injective (see
26
). Also, we note that the full-dimensional variable
\lambda
defined on a domain of dimension
2d
only appears in the constraint
\mathrm{Rj}\lambda=\gamma
, all other variables are (depending on the choice of
\Theta
and
\Sigma
) of dimension at most
d+1
.

In the following, we will drop the constraint
\mathrm{Rj}\lambda=\gamma
, thereby getting rid of the high-dimensional variable
\lambda
. Allowing also a deviation from the data
(f_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}
in case of measurement noise we arrive at

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\quad\text{such that }\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}\quad\text{for all }t\in\Sigma

\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)

where
\alpha\in[0,\infty)
and we include the hard constraint
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f_{t}
on the data by setting
\frac{1}{2\alpha}=\infty
in case
\alpha=0

(specific instances of the optimization problem with parameter
\tilde{\alpha}
and data
\tilde{f}
will be referred to as
\mathrm{P}_{\tilde{\alpha}}(\tilde{f})
).
Note that the choice of the data term is not essential, for instance one could develop the theory analogously without the square on the norm.
The main question of research in this work is to what extent such a dimension-reduced problem is a good approximation of the original problem (
7
).

Remark 37

(Equivalent model formulations)
.

Note that here, different from the formal model (
4
) as presented in
Section

1
, we consider the constraint
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
rather than
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}\text{ for all }t\!\in\!\Sigma,\,\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\text{-a.a.\,}\theta\!\in\!\Theta
. The equivalence of these two formulations is apparent if
\gamma
can be disintegrated to
\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\otimes\gamma_{\theta}
, and we refer to
87
for a proof on when the constraint
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
implies the disintegration to exist.

In this context, an alternative would be to directly use the product space
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}
instead of the measure space
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
for the dimension-reduced measure. In fact, there are different alternative formulations of

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

depending on whether product or measure spaces are used, and we refer to
A.1
for a detailed discussion of different model variants and their equivalence.

For the well-posedness and exact recovery results of this paper,

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

will be the main setting, because using
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}
for
u
(as opposed to
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)
) allows to apply the observation operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
at every
t\in\Sigma
and because using
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
for
\gamma
(as opposed to
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}
) already provides some regularity of the measures
\gamma
with respect to
\theta\in\Theta
. For the results on convergence for noisy data in
Section

4
, however, we will start by treating an (in that case simpler) product topology version of

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

.

At first, we deal with well-posedness of

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

, showing in particular that existence is always ensured for
\alpha>0
and holds for
\alpha=0
whenever the original problem (
7
) has a solution.
We further show stability of the solution with respect to changes in the data.
To this end, let us call
(u,\gamma)\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point
of a sequence
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\times\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
,
n=1,2,\ldots
,
if there exists a subsequence
n_{1},n_{2},\ldots
with
u_{t}^{n_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}u_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
and
\gamma^{n_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\gamma
as
k\to\infty

and if for all
t\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}}
the measure
u_{t}
is a (standard) cluster point of the subsequence
u_{t}^{n_{1}},u_{t}^{n_{2}},\ldots
with respect to weak-
\ast
convergence.

Proposition 38

(Well-posedness)
.

Let
f=(f_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\in H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
and
\alpha\in[0,\infty)
.
If
\alpha=0
, assume
f
to be such that there exists
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)
with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda=f_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
.

Then problem

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

has a solution.
Moreover, for
\alpha>0
the solutions are stable in the following sense:
Let
f^{1},f^{2},\ldots\in H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
be a sequence with
f^{n}_{t}\to f_{t}\in H
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
as
n\to\infty
, and let
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
be a corresponding sequence of solutions to
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f^{n})
. Then the following holds.

i)

The sequence
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
has a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point.

ii)

Any
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point
(u,\gamma)
of the sequence
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
is a solution of
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
.

iii)

If the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
is unique, the entire sequences
u_{t}^{n}
and
\gamma^{n}
weakly-
\ast
converge to
u_{t}
and
\gamma
, respectively, for all
t\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

We start with showing existence. To this end, first note that
(u,\gamma)=(0,0)
is admissible for
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
in case
\alpha>0
and
((\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda)_{t\in\Sigma},\mathrm{Rj}\lambda)
is admissible in case
\alpha=0
, hence the minimization is not over the empty set.

Take
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, to be a minimizing sequence. Then
\|\gamma^{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
is uniformly bounded, and the constraint
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
together with
20
and
28
implies that also
\|u^{n}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
is bounded uniformly in
n
and
t
. Hence, there exists a subsequence
\gamma^{n_{k}}
,
k=1,2,\ldots
, converging weakly-
\ast
to some
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
, and for every
t\in\Sigma
there is a further subsequence
u_{t}^{n_{k,t}}
of
u_{t}^{n_{k}}
(depending on
t
) which converges weakly-
\ast
to some
u_{t}\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega)
.

Now we show that
((u_{t})_{t\in\Sigma},\gamma)
is a solution of
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
. First note that weak-
\ast
-closedness of the positivity constraints on measures implies that both
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
and
u_{t}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
for all
t\in\Sigma
. Further, for each
t\in\Sigma
, both
\gamma^{n_{k,t}}
and
u_{t}^{n_{k,t}}
weakly-
\ast
converge to
\gamma
and
u_{t}
, respectively. Hence, taking the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
in
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma^{n_{k,t}}=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}^{n_{k,t}}
and using weak-
\ast
-to-weak-
\ast
continuity of both
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}
and
\mathrm{Rd}
we obtain
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
. In addition, for
\alpha=0
the weak-
\ast
-to-weak continuity of
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
together with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}^{n_{k,t}}=f_{t}
for all
k
implies
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Finally, weak-
\ast
lower semi-continuity of the Radon norm on
\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
and weak lower semi-continuity of
v\mapsto\|v-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}
in case
\alpha>0
imply that
((u_{t})_{t\in\Sigma},\gamma)
is a minimizer as claimed.

Regarding the stability assertion, take
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, to be a sequence of solutions as claimed. With
(\tilde{u},\tilde{\gamma})
a solution of
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
it follows that

\|\gamma^{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u^{n}_{t}-f^{n}_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\leq\|\tilde{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\tilde{u}_{t}-f^{n}_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\to\|\tilde{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\tilde{u}_{t}-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}

(10)

as
n\to\infty
.
This implies in particular that
\|\gamma^{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
is uniformly bounded and, consequently, also
