\|u^{n}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for each
t\in\Sigma
. Hence, as
{\mathcal{T}}
is finite, we can select a joint subsequence
(u^{n_{k}},\gamma^{n_{k}})
,
k=1,2,\ldots
, such that
u_{t}^{n_{k}}
and
\gamma^{n_{k}}
converge weakly-
\ast
to some
u_{t}
and
\gamma
as
k\to\infty
for each
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
. In addition, for each
t\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}}
we can select a further subsequence
u^{n_{k,t}}_{t}
that weakly-
\ast
converges to some
u_{t}
. This shows the existence of a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point.

Now assume that
(u,\gamma)
is a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point of the sequence
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
.
Then, convergence of
\gamma^{n_{k,t}}
to
\gamma
for each
t
together with weak-
\ast
continuity of
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}
and
\mathrm{Rd}
implies that
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
. Also, convergence of
\gamma^{n_{k}}
and
u^{n_{k}}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
to
\gamma
and
u_{t}
, respectively, allows us to take the limit inferior over indices
n=n_{k}
in the left-hand side of (
10
), which, by lower semi-continuity, can be estimated from below by
\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}
. Thus, (
10
) shows that
(u,\gamma)
has energy no larger than
(\tilde{u},\tilde{\gamma})
and so is optimal as claimed.

Now assume that the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
is unique. If
\gamma^{n}
does not converge weakly-
\ast
to
\gamma
, we can select
\varphi\in C(\Theta\times\Gamma)
,
\epsilon>0
and a subsequence
\gamma^{n_{m}}
,
m=1,2,\ldots
, such that
|\langle\gamma^{n_{m}},\varphi\rangle-\langle\gamma,\varphi\rangle|>\epsilon
for all
m
. Applying the same arguments as above we can obtain a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point
(\tilde{u},\tilde{\gamma})
of that subsequence that is a solution to

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

. Uniqueness implies
\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma
and hence a contradiction. Now assume that there is some
t\in\Sigma
such that
u^{n}_{t}
does not converge weakly-
\ast
to
u_{t}
. In an analogous manner as for
\gamma
we select a subsequence
u^{n_{m}}_{t}
,
m=1,2,\ldots
, and show that
(u,\gamma)
must be a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point of
(u^{n_{m}},\gamma^{n_{m}})
, which again yields a contradiction. Hence also
u^{n}_{t}
converges to
u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
as claimed.
∎

Next, we show a result on convergence for vanishing noise.
This result will also follow from the error estimates in
Section

4
,
but those error estimates require some additional properties of the ground truth particle configuration and the observation operator.

Proposition 39

(Convergence for vanishing noise)
.

Let
f^{\dagger}\in H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
such that there exists
\lambda^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)
with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Let
f^{1},f^{2},\ldots\in H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
be a sequence of noisy data such that

\delta^{n}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|f^{n}_{t}-f^{\dagger}_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n\rightarrow\infty.

Take
\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2},\ldots>0
to be a sequence of parameters such that

\alpha^{n}\rightarrow 0\text{ and }\frac{\delta^{n}}{\alpha^{n}}\rightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n\rightarrow\infty,

and let
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, be corresponding solutions to
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha^{n}}(f^{n})
.
Then the sequence
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
has a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point, and any such cluster point is a solution to
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.
Further, if the solution to
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
is unique, the entire sequences
\gamma^{n}
and
u^{n}_{t}
weakly-
\ast
converge to
\gamma^{\dagger}
and
u^{\dagger}_{t}
, respectively, for all
t\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

By
38
there exists a solution
(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger})
to
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
. By optimality of
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
for
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha^{n}}(f^{n})
it follows that

\|\gamma^{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha^{n}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u^{n}_{t}-f^{n}_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\leq\|\gamma^{\dagger}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{\delta^{n}}{\alpha^{n}}\to\|\gamma^{\dagger}\|_{\mathcal{M}}

(11)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
.
This implies boundedness of
\|\gamma^{n}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\|u^{n}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
uniformly in
n
and
t\in\Sigma
and, arguing as in the proof of
38
, existence of a
{\mathcal{T}}
-cluster point
(u,\gamma)
as claimed.
Also, we note that estimating the left-hand side in (
11
) from below by the data term and multiplying by
\alpha^{n}
yields

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u^{n}_{t}-f^{n}_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\alpha^{n}\|\gamma^{\dagger}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+\delta^{n}=0,

(12)

which together with
f^{n}\to f^{\dagger}
implies
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u^{n}_{t}\to f^{\dagger}_{t}
as
n\to\infty
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
and in particular
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f^{\dagger}_{t}
.
Also, weak-
\ast
lower semi-continuity of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}
implies by (
11
) that
\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\|\gamma^{\dagger}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
. Finally, as in the proof of
38
we obtain
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
such that
(u,\gamma)
is a solution of
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.
In case of uniqueness, we again argue as in the proof of
38
to obtain convergence of the entire sequence
(u^{n},\gamma^{n})
.
∎

3
Exact reconstruction in the absence of noise

As discussed in
Section

1.1
it was shown in
[
15
]
that the exact positions and intensities of point sources (or equivalently positions and masses of particles) in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be recovered from incomplete Fourier measurements. Alberti et al.
[
3
]
extended this result to the dynamic setting by representing moving particles as a collection of point sources at points
(x_{i},v_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
i=1,\dotsc,N
, where
x_{i}
is the position of the
i
-th particle at time
t=0
and
v_{i}
is its velocity.
Our aim in this
Section
is to prove exact reconstruction of the target measures also in our dimension-reduced problem
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.

The conditions under which we prove exact reconstruction are of a geometric nature and just concern the particle positions and velocities, but not their masses.
For this reason we will in this
Section
sometimes identify a particle configuration
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with its support
\supp\lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We also extend the definition of the joint Radon transform correspondingly by defining

\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)=\{(\theta\cdot x,\theta\cdot v)\;|\;(x,v)\in S\}

for any
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\theta\in\Theta
.

The geometric conditions to extend exact reconstruction results from a static to the dynamic setting are based on the notion of
ghost particles
from
[
3
]
.
To introduce it, for a given particle configuration
S=\{(x_{i},v_{i})\;|\;i=1,\dotsc,N\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
(where we will use either
n=d
or
n=1
)
we denote by
L_{i,t}
the set of all points in position-velocity space for which the corresponding particle shares the same position at time
t
with the
i
-th particle of
S
,

L_{i,t}\coloneqq\{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\;|\;x+tv=x_{i}+tv_{i}\}.

Definition 40

(Coincidence and ghost particle)
.

Let
S=\{(x_{i},v_{i})\;|\;i=1,\dotsc,N\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a particle configuration and
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{R}
a set of times.

1.

Configuration
S
has a
coincidence
with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
if the locations of two distinct particles
i\neq j
coincide at some time
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, that is,
x_{i}+tv_{i}=x_{j}+tv_{j}

or equivalently
(x_{i},v_{i})\in L_{j,t}
or
(x_{j},v_{j})\in L_{i,t}
.

2.

A point
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus S
is called a
ghost particle
of
S
with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
if there exist distinct indices
(i_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\subset\{1,\dotsc,N\}
such that

\bigcap_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}L_{i_{t},t}=\{(x,v)\}.

The set of ghost particles of
S
with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
is denoted
G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})
.

Note that the equality
x+t_{i}v=\tilde{x}+t_{i}\tilde{v}
at two distinct time points
t_{i}\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
,
i=1,2
, implies already
(x,v)=(\tilde{x},\tilde{v})
. In view of this, in the relevant case
|{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}|\geq 2
, the intersection
\bigcap_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}L_{i_{t},t}
contains always at most one point and
it is straightforward to see that
40
is consistent with
1
;
while the latter introduces coincidences and ghost particles for particle configurations
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the former does so for their supports
\supp\lambda
.

Throughout this
Section
we will denote the (noisefree) observations by
(f^{\dagger}_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}

and the
ground truth
particle configuration that led to the observations by
\lambda^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)
, thus
f^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda^{\dagger}
.
Furthermore, we will set
u^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda^{\dagger}
,
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
, to be the ground truth snapshots and
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\lambda^{\dagger}
,
\theta\in\Theta
, to be the ground truth position-velocity projections.

3.1
Exact reconstruction for dimension-reduced problem

The basic idea of Alberti et al. was to simply transfer the exact reconstruction property of the
static
reconstruction problem from
[
15
]
to their dynamic one.
The static reconstruction problem for time instant
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
is here given by

\min_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)}\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u=f^{\dagger}_{t}.

\mathrm{P}_{\text{stat}}^{t}(f^{\dagger}_{t})

Definition 41

(Exact reconstruction)
.

We say that the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

reconstructs exactly
if the unique solution to

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

is
u^{\dagger}_{t}
.

Below we prove stepwise that our dimension-reduced model
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
essentially recovers the ground truth snapshots and position-velocity projections
as soon as the static problem reconstructs exactly at selected time points
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
.
The overall flow of the argument is the following:
We first show that if there are time points
t
at which a static reconstruction of the correct particle configuration
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is possible,
then also our dimension-reduced dynamic problem will reconstruct the snapshot at those time points exactly;
in other words, the exact reconstruction at single time points is not hindered by the dynamic setting.
These exactly reconstructed particle configurations at a few time points then serve as a seed to allow exact reconstruction also at other time points.
However, since the snapshots
u_{t}
at the different time points only interact indirectly via the position-velocity projections
\gamma_{\theta}
,
we first have to show that those position-velocity projections are indeed exactly reconstructed, from which it follows in the last step that all snapshots
u_{t}
are exactly reconstructed.

We begin by showing that the dynamic setting does not interfere with exact reconstruction at single time points.

Theorem 42

(Exact reconstruction of good snapshots)
.

Let
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
be a subset of times
t
at which the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

reconstructs exactly,
and let
(u,\gamma)
solve
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.
Then
u_{t}=u^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
(u,\gamma)
solve
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
and
fix an arbitrary time
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Then
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}=f^{\dagger}_{t}
so that
u_{t}
is feasible for

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

.
Furthermore, optimality of
(u,\gamma)
in
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
yields

\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}

since
(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger})
is an admissible competitor in
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.
The condition
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
together with
20
and
28
then implies

\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert u^{\dagger}_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}.

Thus we see that
u_{t}
is in fact a solution to

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

.
Since we assumed
u^{\dagger}_{t}
to be the unique solution to

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

, we can conclude
u_{t}=u^{\dagger}_{t}
.
∎

As explained before, the above proven exact snapshot reconstruction at few time points induces the exact recovery of the position-velocity projections, which is shown next.

Theorem 43

(Exact reconstruction of position-velocity projections)
.

As in
42
let
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
be a subset of times
t
at which the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

reconstructs exactly,
and let
(u,\gamma)
solve
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.
Further define

\Theta^{\prime}\coloneqq\{\theta\in\Theta\mid\text{$\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(\supp\lambda^{\dagger})$ has neither coincidences nor ghost particles with respect to ${\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}$}\}.

Then for any disintegration
\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}
(which exists by
33
)
we have
\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By
33
, for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
we have

\lVert\gamma_{\theta}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}\quad\text{for all }t\in\Sigma.

Now fix one such
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
and
introduce the reconstruction error
\xi\coloneqq\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Letting
\supp\lambda^{\dagger}=\{(x_{i},v_{i})\,|\,i=1,\ldots,N\}
, we consider the Lebesgue decomposition of
\xi
with respect to
|\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}|
, which yields a representation

\xi=\xi_{a}+\xi_{s},\quad\xi_{a}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\beta_{i}\delta_{(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})},\quad\xi_{s}\perp\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}

with weights
\beta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. Next, for each
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
define the function
\tilde{q}_{t}:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
by

\tilde{q}_{t}(s)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\sgn\beta_{i}&\text{if $s=\theta\cdot(x_{i}+tv_{i})$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

These functions are well-defined, since the projected configurations are free of coincidences by choice of
\theta
. Further define
q:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

q(y,w)\coloneqq\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}|}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\tilde{q}_{t}(y+tw),

then we have
q(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})=\sgn\beta_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dotsc,N\}
.

The function
q
is defined as an average of terms with modulus no larger than 1. Therefore, if
(y,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
|q(y,w)|=1
, we necessarily have
|\tilde{q}_{t}(y+tw)|=1
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and thus

\forall t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\,\exists i_{t}\in\{1,\dotsc,N\}:\quad y+tw=\theta\cdot(x_{i_{t}}+tv_{i_{t}}).

(13)

There are now two possible cases:

1.

One of the indices
i_{t}
repeats. In this case the point
(y,w)
is uniquely determined by the equation (
13
) at the two corresponding times, and it follows that
(y,w)=(\theta\cdot x_{i_{t}},\theta\cdot v_{i_{t}})
.

2.

The indices
(i_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}
are all distinct. This would mean that
(y,w)
is a ghost particle of the projected configuration, which is not allowed by choice of
\theta
.

We have thus shown that

|q(y,w)|<1\quad\text{for all }(y,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}.

(14)

By
42
we have the exact reconstruction
u_{t}=u^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Thus, by choice of
\theta
we obtain

\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}

and therefore
0=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\xi
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Using the previously defined function
q
we can calculate

0=\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}|}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{q}_{t}\>\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\>\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\>\mathrm{d}\xi_{a}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\>\mathrm{d}\xi_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\beta_{i}|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\>\mathrm{d}\xi_{s}.

If
\xi_{s}=0
, then also
\lVert\xi_{a}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\beta_{i}|=0
, so there is nothing left to show.
On the other hand,
\xi_{s}\neq 0
leads to a contradiction:
Since
|q|<1
outside the support of
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
by (
14
), we get the inequality

\lVert\xi_{a}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\beta_{i}|=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}q\>\mathrm{d}\xi_{s}<\lVert\xi_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}.

By optimality of
\gamma
we get

\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\geq\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert\gamma_{\theta}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}+\xi\rVert_{\mathcal{M}},

and from
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}+\xi_{a}\perp\xi_{s}
it follows that

\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}+\xi\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}=\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}+\xi_{a}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}+\lVert\xi_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}\geq\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}-\lVert\xi_{a}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}+\lVert\xi_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}>\lVert\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}.

Combining the last two inequalities yields the desired contradiction.
∎

Finally, from the exactly reconstructed position-velocity projections we can infer the snapshots at all times
(or at least those times for which the information in the position-velocity projections suffices to uniquely specify a particle configuration).

Theorem 44

(Exact reconstruction of arbitrary snapshots)
.

Again as in
42
let
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
be a subset of times
t
at which the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

reconstructs exactly,
and let
(u,\gamma)
solve
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
.
Further, fix some
t\in\Sigma
. If
\Theta^{\prime}
from
43
is such that
u^{\dagger}_{t}
is uniquely determined by
(\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\mbox{\LARGE$\llcorner$}}\Theta^{\prime}){\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}^{\dagger}
, the Radon transform restricted to the directions in
\Theta^{\prime}
, then
u_{t}=u^{\dagger}_{t}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the relation
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}
, which holds for all
t\in\Sigma
and by
43
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
.
∎

The above reconstruction results depend on the set
\Theta^{\prime}
for which reason we analyse it in more detail in the next section.
In particular, we show that
\Theta^{\prime}
coincides with
\Theta
up to a
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-nullset,
which for instance implies that for
\Theta=S^{d-1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
the (scaled)
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure both
\gamma^{\dagger}
and
u^{\dagger}
are exactly reconstructed.
This fact is discussed in the next section, while the subsequent section considers the opposite case of a finite set
\Theta
.

3.2
Coincidences and ghost particles in projections

In this section we show that
\Theta^{\prime}
from
43
contains
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all directions of
\Theta

(which will eventually imply
3
).
To this end we consider projections
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)
of particle configurations
S=\{(x_{i},v_{i})\;|\;i=1,\dotsc,N\}
,
which themselves represent particle configurations in one-dimensional space.
Their sets of ghost particles with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
are
G(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S),{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})
, and we furthermore abbreviate

L_{i,t}(\theta)\coloneqq\{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\;|\;x+tv=\theta\cdot x_{i}+t\theta\cdot v_{i})\}=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(L_{i,t}).

We will separately consider coincidences and ghost particles
and show that for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all directions
\theta
,
if a particle configuration
S
is free of coincidences or ghost particles, then so is its projection
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)
.
We begin with coincidences.

Lemma 45

(Coincidences in projections)
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
be a finite set of times and
let the particle configuration
S
be free of coincidences at those times.
Then also
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)
is free of coincidences at those times for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta\in S^{d-1}
.

Proof.

For a
\theta\in S^{d-1}
and indices
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,N\}
,
i\neq j
, the projected particles
(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})
and
(\theta\cdot x_{j},\theta\cdot v_{j})
take the same position at a time
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
if and only if
\theta
fulfills the equation

\theta\cdot(x_{i}-x_{j}+t(v_{i}-v_{j}))=0.

Therefore we can write

\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;|\;\text{$\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)$ has a coincidence at a time in ${\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}$}\}\\
=\bigcup_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\bigcup_{i\neq j}\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;|\;\theta\cdot(x_{i}-x_{j}+t(v_{i}-v_{j}))=0\}.

We have
x_{i}-x_{j}+t(v_{i}-v_{j})\neq 0
for all
i\neq j
since the configuration is assumed to be free of coincidences for
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Hence, the above is a finite union of
(d-1)
-dimensional hyperplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
intersected with the sphere
S^{d-1}
.
Since those intersections are
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-nullsets, their union is as well.
∎

For ghost particles the situation is slightly more complicated than for coincidences:
As the below result shows, the projection
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}
may induce new, additional ghost particles.
However, it does not for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all directions
\theta
.

Lemma 46

(Ghost particle sets in projections)
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
be a finite set of times.
The ghost particles of a particle configuration
S
and of its projections
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}S
satisfy the following relation.

1.

For all
\theta\in S^{d-1}
, as long as
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}
is injective on
S
, it holds that

G(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S),{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})\supset\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))
.

2.

For
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta\in S^{d-1}
we even have

G(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S),{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))
.

Proof.

To prove the first statement, take a ghost particle
({x},{v})\in G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})
of the original configuration. By definition, there exist pairwise distinct indices
(i_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\subset\{1,\dotsc,N\}
such that

x_{i_{t}}+tv_{i_{t}}={x}+t{v}\quad\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}.

Taking the inner product with
\theta
on both sides of these equations, we see that the projected ghost particle
(\theta\cdot{x},\theta\cdot{v})=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}({x},{v})
is a ghost particle of the projected configuration
\{(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})\;|\;i=1,\dotsc,N\}
. The assumption of injectivity is necessary to ensure that the indices
(i_{t})_{t}
still address distinct particles after the projection.

To prove the second statement, we first show that the inclusion from the first statement holds for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta\in S^{d-1}
. Indeed, in order for
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}
to not be injective on
S
, there need to exist indices
1\leq i,j\leq N,i\neq j
, such that
(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})=(\theta\cdot x_{j},\theta\cdot v_{j})
.
We can thus write

\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;|\;\text{$\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}$ is not injective on $S$}\}\subset\bigcup_{i\neq j}\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;|\;\text{$\theta\cdot(x_{i}-x_{j})=0$ and $\theta\cdot(v_{i}-v_{j})=0$}\}.

Since the particles in the original configuration are assumed to be distinct, for
i\neq j
we either have
x_{i}-x_{j}\neq 0
or
v_{i}-v_{j}\neq 0
, which implies that the sets in the union are intersections of
S^{d-1}
with hyperplanes and thus
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-nullsets. Thus
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}
is injective on
S
for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta
as desired.

It remains to show the inclusion
G(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S),{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})\subset\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))
for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta\in S^{d-1}
. To this end, we define the set

M\coloneqq\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;|\;\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))\subsetneq G(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S),{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})\}

of projection directions for which the above inclusion is violated.
We need to show that this set has measure zero. Writing
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}=\{t_{1},\dotsc,t_{r}\}
,
M
can be expressed as

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;\middle|\;\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))\subsetneq\bigcup_{(i_{p})_{p}}\bigcap_{p=1}^{{r}}L_{i_{p},t_{p}}(\theta)\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\bigcup_{(i_{p})_{p}}\left\{\theta\in S^{d-1}\;\middle|\;\bigcap_{p=1}^{{r}}L_{i_{p},t_{p}}(\theta)\setminus\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))\neq\varnothing\right\},

(15)

where the union runs over all families of distinct indices
(i_{p})_{1\leq p\leq{r}}\subset\{1,\dotsc,N\}
.
Since the union is finite, it suffices to show that every set in the union is a
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-nullset.

Let
(i_{p})_{1\leq p\leq{r}}
be such a family of indices and fix a
\theta\in S^{d-1}
.
It is straightforward to see that the pairwise intersections
L_{i_{p},t_{p}}\cap L_{i_{p+1},t_{p+1}}
are singletons. We write

\{(a_{p},b_{p})\}=L_{i_{p},t_{p}}\cap L_{i_{p+1},t_{p+1}},\quad p=1,\dotsc,{r}-1,

and calculate

\bigcap_{p=1}^{r}L_{i_{p},t_{p}}(\theta)=\bigcap_{p=1}^{{r}-1}L_{i_{p},t_{p}}(\theta)\cap L_{i_{p+1},t_{p+1}}(\theta)\\
=\bigcap_{p=1}^{{r}-1}\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(L_{i_{p},t_{p}}\cap L_{i_{p+1},t_{p+1}})=\bigcap_{p=1}^{{r}-1}\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\{(a_{p},b_{p})\}.

(16)

Now we can distinguish two cases:

1.

All points
(a_{p},b_{p})
coincide, that is,

\bigcap_{p=1}^{r}L_{i_{p},t_{p}}=\{(a_{1},b_{1})\}.

In this case,
(a_{1},b_{1})\in G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}})
is a ghost particle of the full-dimensional configuration, and (
16
) implies

\bigcap_{p=1}^{r}L_{i_{p},t_{p}}(\theta)\setminus\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(G(S,{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}))=\varnothing.

2.

There exists a
q\in\{1,\dotsc,{r}-1\}
such that
(a_{q},b_{q})\neq(a_{q+1},b_{q+1})
. Assume without loss of generality that
a_{q}\neq a_{q+1}
.
If the intersection in (
16
) is nonempty, we see from its right-hand side that

(\theta\cdot a_{q},\theta\cdot b_{q})=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(a_{q},b_{q})=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(a_{q+1},b_{q+1})=(\theta\cdot a_{q+1},\theta\cdot b_{q+1}).

This can only hold if
\theta
is contained in the hyperplane

\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;|\;\theta\cdot(a_{q}-a_{q+1})=0\}.

By these cases, every set in the union in (
15
) is either empty or contained in a hyperplane intersected with
S^{d-1}
, which means that
M
is contained in a finite union of
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-nullsets.
∎

The following
Section
is an immediate consequence.

Lemma 47

(Ghost particles in projections)
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
be a finite set of times
and assume that the particle configuration
S
admits no ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Then for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta
the projected configuration
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)
does not admit any ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
either.

In fact, the arguments in this section even show that
\Theta^{\prime}
from
43
differs from
\Theta
at most by a set of finite
(d-2)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure.

3
now simply follows by a combination of previous results (note that we use solutions of
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
instead of the formal model (
4
) from the introduction, cf.
37
):

Proof of
3
.

Let
(u,\gamma)
be a solution of
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
and let
\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}
be a disintegration of
\gamma
(which exists by
33
). Further, let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
be a subset of time steps which fulfills
2
. Since for
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
the snapshot
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is assumed to be reconstructed exactly from observations with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
by the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

, we immediately get
u_{t}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
by
42
.

Denote by
S=\supp(\lambda^{\dagger})
the ground truth configuration.
43
now yields
\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all directions
\theta
from the set

\Theta^{\prime}=\{\theta\in\Theta\mid\text{$\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)$ has neither coincidences nor ghost particles with respect to ${\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}$}\}.

Again by
2
, the ground truth configuration
S
contains neither coincidences nor ghost particles with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Thus, combining
45
and
47
, for
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-almost all
\theta\in S^{d-1}
, the projected configuration
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(S)
is free of coincidences and ghost particles as well.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
is assumed to be absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
, the set
\Theta^{\prime}
contains
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all directions
\theta\in\Theta
.

This fact allows us to conclude that
\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
holds even for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Secondly, the Radon transform, which we know to be injective when applied to all directions
\theta\in\Theta
by
26
, remains injective when restricted to the directions in
\Theta^{\prime}
.
Therefore, by
44
, we see that
u_{t}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
for all
t\in\Sigma
, which completes the proof.
∎

3.3
Exact reconstruction with finitely many directions

It is clear intuitively that exact reconstruction becomes more difficult the more particles need to be reconstructed.
Thus, given
N
particles, a natural question is how large the set
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
of exactly reconstructible snapshots needs to be
in order to exactly reconstruct the correct velocity information in any
\gamma_{\theta}
.
Likewise one can ask how big a set
\Theta
is necessary in order to be able to also exactly reconstruct all snapshots
(not just those that are exactly reconstructible directly from the measurements).
To this end, we briefly generalize a theorem on the X-ray transform due to Hajós and Rényi
[
21
]
to the setting of the Radon transform.
Several proofs can be found in the literature for the theorem by Hajós and Rényi;
a particularly simple one is given in
[
7
]
whose idea we also use here.

Proposition 48

(Determination of finite set from Radon transform)
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be finite, containing at most
N
points,
and let
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
contain
(d-1)N+1
directions such that any
d
of them are linearly independent.
Then
S
is uniquely determined by its Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}S
for
\theta\in\Theta
.

Proof.

Let the set
S^{\prime}
satisfy
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}S=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}S^{\prime}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Assume there exists
x\in S^{\prime}\setminus S
, then for each of the
(d-1)N+1
directions
\theta\in\Theta
there is a point in
S
being projected under
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}
to the same position as
x
.
Since there are at most
N
points in
S
, there is at least one
y\in S
which is projected onto the same position as
x
for at least
d
many projections
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}
.
As a point is uniquely determined by its projections onto
d
linearly independent coordinate lines, we must have
y=x
, a contradiction.
Knowing now that
S^{\prime}\subset S
and thus that
S^{\prime}
contains no more than
N
points, the same argument with the roles of
S
and
S^{\prime}
swapped yields
S\subset S^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 49

(Determination of discrete measure from Radon transform)
.

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a nonnegative Radon measure with support in at most
N
points,
and let
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
contain
(d-1)N+1
directions such that any
d
of them are linearly independent.
Then
\nu
is uniquely determined among all nonnegative Radon measures by its Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu
for
\theta\in\Theta
.

Proof.

For a contradiction assume there is a second nonnegative Radon measure
\nu^{\prime}
satisfying
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu^{\prime}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Then in particular
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\supp\nu=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\supp\nu^{\prime}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
so that necessarily
\supp\nu=\supp\nu^{\prime}
.
Now consider the signed measure
\xi=\nu-\nu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and decompose it into its positive and negative part
\xi_{+}
and
\xi_{-}
.
By linearity of
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}
, we have
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\xi_{+}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\xi_{-}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Therefore, by
48
we have
\supp\xi_{+}=\supp\xi_{-}
, which is impossible unless
\xi_{+}=\xi_{-}=0
.
∎

An immediate consequence is the following result.
It exploits the fact, already used implicitly before, that the move operator
\mathrm{Mv}
is equivalent to the two-dimensional Radon transform in the sense

\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\nu={[s\mapsto\sqrt{1+t^{2}}s]}_{\#}(\mathrm{Rd}_{(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\nu).

Corollary 50

(Exact reconstruction)
.

Let the ground truth particle configuration
\lambda^{\dagger}
contain at most
N
particles,
let
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
contain
(d-1)N+1
directions such that any
d
of them are linearly independent,
and let
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
be the counting measure on
\Theta
.
If there are
N+1
times
t
at which the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

reconstructs exactly,
then the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
satisfies
(u,\gamma)=(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger})
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
denote the
N+1
times, then by
42
we have
u_{t}=u^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Now for any
\theta\in\Theta
we know
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}

and thus
\mathrm{Rd}_{(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
for all
N+1
times
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
By
49
this uniquely determines
\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Now for any
t\in\Sigma
we have
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
(d-1)N+1
many directions
\theta\in\Theta
,
which again by
49
uniquely determines
u_{t}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
.
∎

The above estimates can be improved in various directions.
We only considered the worst case analysis: we even obtain exact reconstruction if the times
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and the directions
\Theta
are configured in the worst possible case
(we did so since the
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
is in general prescribed by the given observations and cannot be chosen).
If one chooses the times
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and directions
\Theta
optimally, exact reconstruction may even be achieved for more points than considered above.
To indicate directions of possible improvement we briefly summarize results from the literature on the two-dimensional Radon transform:

•

If the directions
\Theta
are not a subset of the sides of an affinely deformed regular
N
-gon
(or if the points do not form an affinely deformed regular
N
-gon),
then
N
points are uniquely determined by
m
projections already if
m>N-\frac{\sqrt{N+9}-3}{2}

[
7
, Prop. 3]
.

•

If the directions
\Theta
are chosen optimally (not just satisfying the previous condition),
then
N
points are uniquely determined by
m
projections already if
N\leq\max\{m_{0},2^{cm/\log m}\}
for two constants
m_{0},c>0

[
19
, Thm. 1.1]
.
This bound is quite sharp since for
N>6^{m/3}
one can always find
N
points that are not uniquely determined by
m
fixed projections
[
19
, Thm. 1.2]
.

•

In higher dimensions
d
, the previous point still holds true (up to changed constants) for the X-ray transform,
while for
m
arbitrary, non-optimal projection directions spanning
\mathbb{R}^{d}

one can asymptotically no longer guarantee reconstruction of arbitrary
N
points as soon as
N>m^{d+1+\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
arbitrarily small
[
4
, Cor. 4.1]
.

All above results are concerned with worst case point configurations, that is,
they ask for fixed projections such that all possible configurations of
N
points can be uniquely reconstructed.
In applications it is probably of higher relevance whether
most
point configurations can be reconstructed.

Proposition 51

(Determination of generic set from Radon transform)
.

Let
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
contain
d+1
directions such that any
d
of them are linearly independent,
and consider any probability distribution of
N
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is absolutely continuous with respect to the
Nd
-dimensional Lebesgue measure
(for instance Gaussian i.i.d. points).
Then almost surely a set
S
of
N
points is uniquely determined among all subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by its Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}S
for
\theta\in\Theta
.

Proof.

Let the directions in
\Theta
be numbered as
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d+1}
.
Consider a set
S=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and denote the hyperplanes through those points normal to
\theta\in\Theta
by
H^{i}_{\theta}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,(x-x_{i})\cdot\theta=0\}
.
The set
S
is uniquely determined if the only mappings
\iota:\Theta\to\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that

H^{\iota(\theta_{1})}_{\theta_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap H^{\iota(\theta_{d+1})}_{\theta_{d+1}}\neq\varnothing

are the constant mappings
\iota\equiv i
(in which case

H^{\iota(\theta_{1})}_{\theta_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap H^{\iota(\theta_{d+1})}_{\theta_{d+1}}=\{x_{i}\}
).
Thus it remains to show

H^{\iota(\theta_{1})}_{\theta_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap H^{\iota(\theta_{d+1})}_{\theta_{d+1}}=\varnothing

almost surely for all nonconstant
\iota
.
Now the condition

H^{\iota(\theta_{1})}_{\theta_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap H^{\iota(\theta_{d+1})}_{\theta_{d+1}}\neq\varnothing

is equivalent to the existence of a solution
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the overdetermined system of equations

Ax=b_{\iota}\quad\text{for }A=(\theta_{1}\ \cdots\ \theta_{d+1})^{T},\quad b_{\iota}=(\theta_{1}\cdot x_{\iota(\theta_{1})}\ \cdots\ \theta_{d+1}\cdot x_{\iota(\theta_{d+1})})^{T}

or equivalently (by the Fredholm alternative) to
b_{\iota}\cdot y=0
for
y\in S^{d}
the vector (unique up to sign) spanning
\ker A^{T}
.
Thus we require that for any nonconstant
\iota
the solutions
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{Nd}
to
\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}y_{i}\theta_{i}^{T}x_{\iota(\theta_{i})}=b_{\iota}\cdot y=0

form a set of dimension strictly smaller than
Nd
and thus a Lebesgue-nullset.
However, the solution set can only have dimension
Nd
if the equation is trivial, which is never the case:
The coefficient of
x_{\iota(\theta_{1})}
is the sum of at most
d
terms of the form
y_{i}\theta_{i}^{T}
.
Since all
y_{i}
are nonzero and any
d
directions
\theta_{i}
are linearly independent, it is therefore nonzero.
∎

Corollary 52

(Determination of generic measures from Radon transform)
.

Let
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
contain
d+1
directions such that any
d
of them are linearly independent,
and consider any probability distribution of discrete nonnegative measures
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}

such that the induced distribution of their
N
support points is absolutely continuous with respect to the
Nd
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.
Then almost surely such a measure
\nu
is uniquely determined among all nonnegative Radon measures by its Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu
for
\theta\in\Theta
.

Proof.

Let
\nu
be a measure consisting of
N
weighted Dirac measures, then its support is almost surely uniquely determined by
51
.
Supposing that its support is indeed uniquely determined, it remains to show that also its weights are uniquely determined.
Thus let
\nu^{\prime}
be another admissible measure with
\supp\nu^{\prime}=\supp\nu
and
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu^{\prime}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\nu
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Let
\xi_{+}
and
\xi_{-}
denote the positive and negative part, respectively, of
\nu^{\prime}-\nu
, then
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\xi_{+}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}\xi_{-}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Since
\supp\xi_{+}\cup\supp\xi_{-}\subset\supp\nu
also the supports of
\xi_{+}
and
\xi_{-}
are uniquely determined by their Radon transforms,
which therefore implies
\supp\xi_{+}=\supp\xi_{-}
.
This is only possible if
\xi_{+}=\xi_{-}=0
.
∎

As a direct consequence we obtain generic exact reconstruction even with only
d+1
directions in
\Theta
, covering in the result of
4
.

Corollary 53

(Exact reconstruction)
.

Assume the ground truth
\lambda^{\dagger}
to contain
N
particles (some potentially with zero mass) whose location vector in
(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
is distributed
according to a probability distribution which is absolutely continuous with respect to the
2Nd
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.
Let
\Theta\subset S^{d-1}
contain
d+1
directions such that any
d
of them are linearly independent,
and let
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
be the counting measure on
\Theta
.
If there are three times
t
at which the static problem

⁢

P
stat

t

(

f
†

t

)

reconstructs exactly,
then almost surely the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
satisfies
(u,\gamma)=(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger})
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
denote the three times, then by
42
we have
u_{t}=u^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Now for any
\theta\in\Theta
we know
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}

and thus
\mathrm{Rd}_{(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
for all three times
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
By
52
this almost surely uniquely determines
\gamma_{\theta}=\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
for all finitely many
\theta\in\Theta
.
Now for any
t\in\Sigma
we have
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
d+1
many directions
\theta\in\Theta
,
which again by
52
almost surely uniquely determines
u_{t}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
for each fixed
t\in\Sigma
.
That the fixed
t\in\Sigma
may even be replaced by all
t\in\Sigma
follows from reinspection of the proof of
51
,
which can be extended to show that generically all
u_{t}
(or rather their supports) can simultaneously be uniquely reconstructed.
∎

Remark 54
.

The results in this section were obtained independent of the ones in
Section

3.1
, in particular not relying on assumptions on ghost particles and coincidences. Alternatively, it would have also been possible to investigate the set
\Theta^{\prime}
of
43
also for the case of finitely many directions, and show that it is sufficiently large to ensure exact reconstruction (almost surely). While this would have provided a common framework for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
being either the Hausdorff or the counting measure, a direct treatment of the latter turned out to be more concise and, for this reason, was preferred here.

4
Reconstruction from noisy data

While the previous
Section
dealt with noisefree observations
f^{\dagger}
and a reconstruction via
\mathrm{P}_{0}(f^{\dagger})
,
in this section we will consider noisy observations
f^{\delta}
and a reconstruction via
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f^{\delta})
with positive
\alpha
.
We aim to derive error estimates for our reconstruction.
To this end we will first introduce a measure to quantify the error in
Section

4.1
,
then derive abstract error estimates for general inverse problems of Radon measures in
Section

4.2
.

In order to finally apply these results to our model
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f^{\delta})
, we will first consider an (in this case simpler) product-topology version of
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f^{\delta})
in
Section

4.3
and then extend the results to
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f^{\delta})
in
Section

4.4
.

4.1
Unbalanced optimal transport as error measure

In the setting of particle reconstruction, the positions and masses of the reconstructed particles will slightly deviate from the ground truth due to noise in the measurement.
A quantification of this deviation thus has to account for both sources of error simultaneously, the mass relocation and the mass change.
A clear separation of these two is not meaningful since a Dirac mass in the wrong place
can both be explained by an incorrect positioning of a ground truth Dirac mass or simply by some added mass to the ground truth (which just happened to be placed in that position).
Therefore we will quantify the reconstruction error in so-called unbalanced Wasserstein divergences (variants of optimal transport distances that allow for mass changes).
The following unbalanced Wasserstein divergences turn out to be the most natural in our setting.

Definition 55

(Unbalanced Wasserstein divergence)
.

Let
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be nonnegative Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The
Wasserstein-
p
distance
between
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
is defined for
p\geq 1
as

W_{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\inf\left\{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dist(x,y)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\pi(x,y)\right)^{1/p}\,\middle|\,\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\right.\\
\left.\vphantom{\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\right)^{1/p}}{[(x,y)\mapsto x]}_{\#}\pi=\nu_{1},{[(x,y)\mapsto y]}_{\#}\pi=\nu_{2}\right\}.

If
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
have unequal mass
\nu_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\neq\nu_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the above infimum is over the empty set and thus infinite.
For fixed parameter
R>0
we define the
unbalanced Wasserstein-
p
divergence
between
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
as

W_{p,R}^{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\inf\left\{W_{p}^{p}(\nu,\nu_{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}R^{p}\|\nu_{1}-\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}}\,\middle|\,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}.

Remark 56

(Interpretation of unbalanced Wasserstein divergence)
.

The Wasserstein distance is well-known to be a metric on the space of nonnegative Radon measures with fixed mass, supported on a fixed compact domain,
and it metrizes weak-
\ast
convergence on that space.
The quantity
W_{p}^{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
measures the cost for transporting the material from
\nu_{1}
such that after the transport it is distributed according to
\nu_{2}
.
The unbalanced Wasserstein divergence
W_{p,R}^{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
thus measures the cost for first changing the mass of
\nu_{1}
to some intermediate measure
\nu

and then transporting that new mass distribution to
\nu_{2}
.
Thus it can be used to quantify weak-
\ast
convergence of nonnegative measures of not necessarily the same mass.
The weight
R^{p}
of the mass change cost governs up to which distance a mass transport is less costly than removing the mass in the initial position and regrowing it in the destination;
this distance is exactly
R
.

Remark 57

(Symmetry of unbalanced Wasserstein divergence)
.

Obviously, an alternative definition would have been based on changing the mass of
\nu_{2}
or of both
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
.
It can readily be seen that those are equivalent:
Indeed, any good candidate intermediate measure
\nu
should be absolutely continuous with respect to and no larger than
\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}
.
Then it is easy to check that
\tilde{\nu}=\nu_{2}+\nu_{1}-\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies

W_{p}^{p}(\nu,\nu_{2})=W_{p}^{p}(\nu_{1},\tilde{\nu})
and
\|\nu_{1}-\nu\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\nu_{2}-\tilde{\nu}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
.

The unbalanced Wasserstein divergence is nonnegative, and
W_{p,R}^{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=0
implies
\nu_{1}=\nu_{2}
.
Furthermore, it is symmetric by the previous remark.
However, it does not satisfy the triangle inequality (unless
p=1
).
Therefore it is not a metric, but metric variants can easily be defined.

A bound on the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence between a discrete measure
\nu_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is readily obtained from estimates of the mass distribution within and outside balls around
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}
, which is the content of the following theorem.
Such estimates were first derived by Candès and Fernandez-Granda to analyse spike recovery from noisy data,
and in the subsequent sections we will show how they can also be derived for our dimension-reduced dynamic particle tracking setting.

Theorem 58

(Unbalanced Wasserstein divergence from mass estimates)
.

Let
p\geq 1
,
R>0
, and let
\nu_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with the distance between the
x_{i}
being at least
2R
, and
\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy

\displaystyle|\nu_{2}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}))

\displaystyle\leq A,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}|(\nu_{1}-\nu_{2})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))|

\displaystyle\leq B,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{p}\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p},

where
B_{R}(S)
denotes the open
R
-neighbourhood of the set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle W_{p,R}^{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}R^{p}(A+B)+C_{p},

\displaystyle W_{q,R}^{q}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}R^{q}(A+B)+R^{q-p}C_{p}\text{ if }q>p,

\displaystyle W_{q,R}^{q}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}R^{q}(A+B)+\tfrac{p}{p-q}\left(\tfrac{p-q}{q}\right)^{q/p}C_{p}^{q/p}\|\nu_{2}\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{1-q/p}\text{ if }q<p.

Proof.

In the definition of the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence simply take

\nu=\nu_{2}{\mbox{\LARGE$\llcorner$}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}))+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{2}(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))\delta_{x_{i}}
,
then the first estimate immediately follows.
The second one follows, using the same
\nu
, from
\dist(x,x_{i})^{q}\leq R^{q-p}\dist(x,x_{i})^{p}
on
B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})
for any
q>p
.
Finally, if
q<p
we again pick the same
\nu
and estimate

\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{q}\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}(x)\leq\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})\setminus B_{r}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{q}\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}(x)+\int_{B_{r}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{q}\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}(x)\\
\leq r^{q-p}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})\setminus B_{r}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{p}\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}(x)+r^{q}\|\nu_{2}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq r^{q-p}C_{p}+r^{q}\|\nu_{2}\|_{\mathcal{M}}

for any
r>0
. Optimizing in
r
yields
r=(\frac{p-q}{q}\frac{C_{p}}{\|\nu_{2}\|_{\mathcal{M}}})^{1/p}
and thus

\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{q}\>\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}(x)\leq\frac{p}{p-q}\left(\frac{p-q}{q}\right)^{q/p}C_{p}^{q/p}\|\nu_{2}\|_{\mathcal{M}}^{1-q/p}

so that the third estimate follows.
∎

4.2
General strategy for Bregman distance estimates

To derive noise-dependent convergence rates or error estimates for our reconstruction in case of noisy data, we follow a classical strategy in inverse problems that is based on duality.
In this section we recapitulate the corresponding procedure in an abstract setting.
It reduces the derivation of error estimates to the construction of appropriate dual variables.
The explicit construction of such dual variables for our specific setting will be performed in the subsequent section.

The abstract setting is as follows.
Let
X,Y
be topological vector spaces,
K:X\to Y
a continuous linear observation operator,

G:X\to\mathbb{R}
a convex regularization energy,
and
F_{f}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
a nonnegative convex fidelity term checking the fidelity of its argument to a measurement
f\in Y
.
We will assume
F_{f}(f)=0
and
F_{f}>0
elsewhere
(otherwise the data fidelity would not be able to discriminate between multiple, apparently equally well fitting reconstructions).
We will denote by
y^{\dagger}\in X
the ground truth configuration with noise-free measurement
f^{\dagger}=Ky^{\dagger}\in Y
,
while the noisy measurement is denoted
f^{\delta}
, the amount of noise
\delta
being quantified by the condition

F_{f^{\delta}}(f^{\dagger})\leq\delta.

In our application, the spaces
X,Y
will be given by products of spaces of Radon measures, hence we assume
X
and
Y
to actually be dual spaces to topological vector spaces
(otherwise the roles of primal and dual spaces simply swap in the following).
For simplicity we denote the predual spaces by
X^{*}
and
Y^{*}
.
We now consider the problem to reconstruct an approximation to
y^{\dagger}
by solving

\min_{y^{\delta}\in X}E_{\alpha,f^{\delta}}(y^{\delta})\quad\text{for }E_{\alpha,f^{\delta}}(y^{\delta})=G(y^{\delta})+\frac{1}{\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta}),

(17)

where
\alpha,\delta>0
corresponds to the noisy measurement case and
\alpha=\delta=0
to the noise-free setting
(with the notational convention
\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f}(\tilde{f})=0
if
\tilde{f}=f
and
\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f}(\tilde{f})=\infty
otherwise).

Generally, if
y^{\dagger}
is the minimizer of a smooth energy
E
and
y^{\delta}
an approximation,
then the deviation of
y^{\delta}
from
y^{\dagger}
can be estimated from the difference
E(y^{\delta})-E(y^{\dagger})
and lower bounds on the Hessian of
E
.
In the case of nonsmooth convex energies the role of Hessian-based distance estimates is naturally replaced by Bregman distances for
E
,
where the Bregman distance of
a
to
b
with respect to a convex energy
E
and a subgradient element
w\in\partial E(b)
is

D^{E}_{w}(a,b)=E(a)-E(b)-\langle w,a-b\rangle\geq 0.

However, since in our setting the energy difference
E_{0,f^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta})-E_{0,f^{\dagger}}(y^{\dagger})
is infinite if
Ky^{\delta}\neq f^{\dagger}
,
the resulting Bregman distance estimates would not be very useful.
Therefore, the fidelity term needs to be dualized first, that is, for some fixed
w^{\dagger}\in Y^{*}
, instead of
E_{0,f^{\dagger}}
we consider

G(\cdot)+\langle K^{*}w^{\dagger},\cdot\rangle-(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})

(which by weak duality is never larger than
E_{0,f^{\dagger}}
).
Above,
K^{*}:Y^{*}\to X^{*}
is the operator having
K
as dual,
and
(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})=\sup_{y\in Y}\langle y,w^{\dagger}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}}(y)=\langle f^{\dagger},w^{\dagger}\rangle
represents the (predual) Legendre–Fenchel conjugate.
This then leads to the following estimates (as for instance derived for quadratic
F_{f^{\dagger}}
in
[
13
, Thm. 2]
).

Theorem 59

(Bregman distance estimate for noisy reconstruction)
.

Let
w^{\dagger}\in Y^{*}
satisfy the source condition
-K^{*}w^{\dagger}\in\partial G(y^{\dagger})
.
Then a minimizer
y^{\delta}
of (
17
) satisfies

\displaystyle D^{G}_{-K^{*}w^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\leq\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})\right)/(2\alpha),

\displaystyle F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta})

\displaystyle\leq\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})\right),

\displaystyle\langle K^{*}w,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left(4\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w)\right)/(2\alpha)\quad\text{for all }w\in Y^{*}.

Proof.

First observe that

\left[G(y^{\delta})+\langle K^{*}w^{\dagger},y^{\delta}\rangle-(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})\right]-\left[G(y^{\dagger})+\langle K^{*}w^{\dagger},y^{\dagger}\rangle-(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})\right]\\
=G(y^{\delta})-G(y^{\dagger})-\langle-K^{*}w^{\dagger},y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle=D^{G}_{-K^{*}w^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger}).

Now the energy difference on the left-hand side can be estimated from above exploiting the optimality of
y^{\delta}
,

G(y^{\delta})+\frac{1}{\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta})=E_{\alpha,f^{\delta}}(y^{\delta})\leq E_{\alpha,f^{\delta}}(y^{\dagger})=G(y^{\dagger})+\frac{1}{\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(f^{\dagger})\leq G(y^{\dagger})+\frac{\delta}{\alpha}.

Furthermore, we use Fenchel’s inequality to obtain

\langle K^{*}w,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle=\frac{\langle 2\alpha w,Ky^{\delta}\rangle+\langle-2\alpha w,Ky^{\dagger}\rangle}{2\alpha}\leq\frac{F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w)}{2\alpha},

which already proves the third statement in case the second holds.
Using these two inequalities we obtain

\left[G(y^{\delta})+\langle K^{*}w^{\dagger},y^{\delta}\rangle-(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})\right]-\left[G(y^{\dagger})+\langle K^{*}w^{\dagger},y^{\dagger}\rangle-(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})\right]\\
\leq\frac{\delta}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta})+\langle w^{\dagger},K(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})\rangle\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})\right)-\frac{1}{2\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta}).

Identifying the left-hand side with
D^{G}_{-K^{*}w^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger})

and adding
\frac{1}{2\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(Ky^{\delta})
on both sides, the first two statements immediately follow.
∎

Remark 60

(Interpretation of source condition)
.

The source condition
-K^{*}w^{\dagger}\in\partial G(y^{\dagger})
is one of the two necessary and sufficient primal-dual optimality conditions for the optimization problem;
the other one is
Ky^{\dagger}\in\partial(\tfrac{1}{0}F_{f^{\dagger}})^{*}(w^{\dagger})=\{f^{\dagger}\}
and thus automatically satisfied for the ground truth.

Remark 61

(Rates from estimates)
.

Note that due to the strict convexity of
F_{f^{\delta}}
in
f^{\delta}
, the Legendre–Fenchel conjugate
F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}
is differentiable in
0

so that
(F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w))/\alpha
converges to zero as
\alpha\to 0
.
Thus, by choosing
\alpha
as the minimizer of
(\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w))/\alpha
,
the right-hand sides in
59
become functions of
\delta
that decrease to zero as
\delta\to 0
.

If the regularization contains the Radon norm of a measure,
the above abstract estimates of Bregman distances can be turned into unbalanced Wasserstein estimates of how much
y^{\delta}
deviates from
y^{\dagger}
.
In essence, this is the basic idea underlying the classical stability analysis of superresolution or spike reconstruction as introduced by Candès and Fernandez-Granda in
[
15
]
.

Theorem 62

(Mass distribution from Bregman distance)
.

Assume
y^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be a discrete measure with support in
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and let
v^{\dagger}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}(y^{\dagger})\subset C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is,
|v^{\dagger}|\leq 1
and
v^{\dagger}(x_{i})=\sgn(y^{\dagger}(\{x_{i}\}))
.
If
v^{\dagger}
satisfies

|v^{\dagger}(x)|\leq 1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist(x,\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})\}^{2}

for some
\kappa,R>0
with
2R
smaller than the minimum distance between the
x_{i}
, then for any
y^{\delta}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle|y^{\delta}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger}),\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

Furthermore, let
v\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}(y)\subset C(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
y=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))\delta_{x_{i}}
, thus
v(x_{i})=\sgn(y(\{x_{i}\}))
for
i=1,\ldots,N
. If for some
\mu>0
the function
v
satisfies

v(x_{i})v(x)\geq 1-\mu\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\quad\text{for all }x\in B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}),i=1,\ldots,N,

then we additionally have

\sum_{i=1}^{N}|(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))|\leq\frac{1+\mu R^{2}}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger})+\langle v,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle.

Proof.

Abbreviate
B^{c}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})
and let
\frac{\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}}{\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|}
denote the Radon–Nikodym derivative of
y^{\delta}
with respect to its total variation measure, then the first two inequalities follow from

\kappa R^{2}|y^{\delta}|(B^{c})+\kappa\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|(x)\leq\int_{B^{c}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}}{\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|}-v^{\dagger}\>\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\frac{\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}}{\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|}-v^{\dagger}\>\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}(x)\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}}{\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|}-v^{\dagger}\>\mathrm{d}y^{\delta}(x)=\|y^{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}-\langle v^{\dagger},y^{\delta}\rangle=\|y^{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}-\|y^{\dagger}\|_{\mathcal{M}}-\langle v^{\dagger},y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle=D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger}).

The third inequality is obtained as follows.
We have

\sum_{i=1}^{N}|(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))|=\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})}v\>\mathrm{d}(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}v(x_{i})-v\>\mathrm{d}(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}),

where the first summand can be estimated as

\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})}v\>\mathrm{d}(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})=\langle v,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle-\int_{B^{c}}v\>\mathrm{d}(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})\\
\leq\langle v,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle+|y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}|(B^{c})\leq\langle v,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle+\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger}),

while the second term is estimated from above via

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}v(x_{i})-v\>\mathrm{d}(y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\mu\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}|(x)\leq\frac{\mu}{\kappa}D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger}).\qed

We finally combine
58
,
62
and
59
:
Via
58
an unbalanced Wasserstein error estimate can be expressed with the help of terms
that can be estimated by Bregman distances via
62
,
which in turn are estimated via
59
.
In the case
G(y^{\delta})=\|y^{\delta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
the combination is immediate,
however, to allow for a little more generality (which is required for the application to our setting) we shall assume

G(y^{\delta})=\|y^{\delta}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+G_{2}(y^{\delta}_{2}),\quad\text{where }y^{\delta}=(y^{\delta}_{1},y^{\delta}_{2}).

Corollary 63

(Unbalanced Wasserstein estimate for noisy reconstruction)
.

Consider the choice
G(y^{\delta})=G(y^{\delta}_{1},y^{\delta}_{2})=\|y^{\delta}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{M}}+G_{2}(y^{\delta}_{2})
.
Let
y^{\dagger}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
have support in
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
let
y^{\delta}
with
y^{\delta}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the approximation to
y^{\dagger}
obtained from (
17
),
and let
w^{\dagger},w\in Y^{*}
satisfy

\displaystyle-K^{*}w^{\dagger}=-((K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1},(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{2})

\displaystyle\in\partial G(y^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle-(K^{*}w)_{1}

\displaystyle\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}(y^{\delta}_{1}-y^{\dagger}_{1})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))\delta_{x_{i}}\right),

\displaystyle|(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq 1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist(x,\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})\}^{2},

\displaystyle(K^{*}w)_{1}(x_{i})(K^{*}w)_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mu\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\quad\text{for all }x\in B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}),i=1,\ldots,N,

for some
\mu,\kappa,R>0
with
2R
smaller than the minimum distance between the
x_{i}
. Then we have

\displaystyle|y^{\delta}_{1}|(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{R}(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha\kappa R^{2}}\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})}\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|y^{\delta}_{1}|(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha\kappa}\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}|(y^{\delta}_{1}-y^{\dagger}_{1})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\mu R^{2}}{2\alpha\kappa R^{2}}\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})\right)+\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},y^{\delta}_{2}-y^{\dagger}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\left(4\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w)\right).

In particular, by
58
this implies

W_{2,R}^{2}(y^{\delta}_{1},y^{\dagger}_{1})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}R^{2}\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},y^{\delta}_{2}-y^{\dagger}_{2}\rangle\\
+\frac{12+7\max\{\kappa,\mu\}R^{2}}{4\kappa\alpha}\left(\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha w)\right).

Proof.

This immediately follows from choosing
v^{\dagger}=(-K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1}
,
v=(-K^{*}w)_{1}
in
62

and noting that
D^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta}_{1},y^{\dagger}_{1})\leq D^{G}_{-K^{*}w^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger})

as well as
\langle v,y^{\delta}_{1}-y^{\dagger}_{1}\rangle=\langle-K^{*}w,y^{\delta}-y^{\dagger}\rangle+\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},y^{\delta}_{2}-y^{\dagger}_{2}\rangle
so that
59
can be inserted.
∎

This way, the problem of proving the error estimate is reduced to the problem of constructing
w^{\dagger}
and
w
.
Note for instance that for
G_{2}=0
the condition
-(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{2}\in\partial G_{2}(y^{\dagger}_{2})
implies
(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{2}=0

so that the corresponding term in the estimate of
63
vanishes.

Remark 64

(Adaptation for nonnegative measures and compact domains)
.

It is straightforward to check that all arguments stay the same when replacing
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a compact domain
A
and when taking the slightly different regularizer choice

G(y^{\delta})=G(y^{\delta}_{1},y^{\delta}_{2})=\|y^{\delta}_{1}\|_{+}+G_{2}(y^{\delta}_{2})\quad\text{with }\|y^{\delta}_{1}\|_{+}=\begin{cases}\|y^{\delta}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{M}}&\text{if }y^{\delta}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(A),\\
\infty&\text{else.}\end{cases}

For this choice one obtains the estimates of
63
under the slightly weaker conditions

\displaystyle-K^{*}w^{\dagger}=-((K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1},(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{2})

\displaystyle\in\partial G(y^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle-(K^{*}w)_{1}

\displaystyle\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}(y^{\delta}_{1}-y^{\dagger}_{1})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}))\delta_{x_{i}}\right),

\displaystyle-(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist(x,\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\})\}^{2},

\displaystyle(K^{*}w)_{1}(x_{i})(K^{*}w)_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mu\dist(x,x_{i})^{2}\quad\text{for all }x\in B_{R}(\{x_{i}\}),i=1,\ldots,N.

4.3
Construction of dual variables for a product-topology setting

In this and the next
Section
we prove error estimates for the particle reconstruction from noisy observations.
As in
Section

3
we denote the ground truth particle configuration by
\lambda^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Lambda)

with
\lambda^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}
,
with snapshots
u^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda^{\dagger}
,
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
,
and with position-velocity projections
\gamma^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Rj}\lambda^{\dagger}
or
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}\lambda^{\dagger}
,
\theta\in\Theta
.
The noisefree observations are denoted
f^{\dagger}_{t}=\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda^{\dagger}
, the noisy ones
f^{\delta}_{t}
,
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
, with noise strength

\delta=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|f_{t}^{\delta}-f_{t}^{\dagger}\|_{H}^{2}.

(18)

The main result of these
Sections
is the below stated more quantitative version of
6
.
Its error estimates depend on how close we are to a coincidence or a ghost particle.
This proximity to a degenerate configuration is quantified via the notion of
\Delta
-coincidences and
\Delta
-ghost particles
that will be introduced in
69
and
72
,
in which
\Delta
can be thought of as the smallest necessary shift of the particles to produce a coincidence or a ghost particle.
Vanishing
\Delta
indicates a degenerate configuration, and in this case the error estimates become void
(since the error is in those cases measured in the trivial unbalanced Wasserstein divergence
W_{2,0}^{2}\equiv 0
).
Finally note that those snapshots that cannot directly be reconstructed from measurements will instead be reconstructed from the position-velocity projections
\gamma_{\theta}

with
\theta
from a certain set of
good directions

\Theta^{\prime}\subset\Theta
.

We start in this subsection with a derivation of error estimates for a product-topology version of
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)
given as

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\!\|\gamma_{\hat{\theta}}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\!+\!\frac{1}{2\alpha}\!\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}\!-\!f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\quad\text{ such that }\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}\text{ for all }t\!\in\!\Sigma,\,\theta\!\in\!\Theta.

\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)

Regarding the well-posedness and the equivalence of
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
to
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
we refer to
A.1
.
The reason for using this version first is that it simplifies the construction of dual variables. An extension of the results to
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
will subsequently be carried out in the next subsection below.
The error estimation result for both models, to be obtained in
Sections

4.3
and
4.4
, can be summarized as follows.

Theorem 65

(Reconstruction error estimates)
.

Let
(u,\mu)
be the solution of
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
or
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
, and let
\varnothing\neq{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}
such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is stably reconstructible for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Pick some
\Theta^{\prime}\subset\Theta
.
Then there exist constants
R,C_{1}>0
(depending on
\lambda^{\dagger}
,
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
) and
C_{2}>0
(depending on
\lambda^{\dagger}
,
\Theta^{\prime}
) such that

1.

we have
W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u_{t}^{\dagger})\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\delta}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
,

2.

we have
W_{2,R_{\theta}}^{2}(\gamma_{\theta},\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})\leq\frac{C_{1}}{R_{\theta}^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
(
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta\in\Theta
in case of model
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
), where
R_{\theta}=\min\{R,\Delta_{\theta}/3\}/\sqrt{1+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}t^{2}}
and
\Delta_{\theta}\geq 0
is such that
\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
contains no
\Delta_{\theta}
-coincidence or
\Delta_{\theta}
-ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
according to
69
,

3.

we have
W_{2,R_{t}}^{2}(u_{t},u_{t}^{\dagger})\leq C_{1}C_{2}(t^{2}+\frac{1}{R_{t}^{4}})\sqrt{\delta}
for all
t\in\Sigma
,
where
R_{t}=\min\{R,\Delta_{t}/({3\sqrt{1+t^{2}}})\}
and
\Delta_{t}\in[0,\inf_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}\Delta_{\theta}]
is such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
contains no
\Delta_{t}
-coincidence or
\Delta_{t}
-ghost particle with respect to
\Theta^{\prime}
according to
72
.

65
implies
6
:
Given
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, the minimum
\Delta
such that
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
contains a
\Delta
-coincidence or
\Delta
-ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

is continuous in
\theta
, thus it assumes a minimum
M
at some
\theta^{\prime}
in the compact
\overline{\Theta}
.
This minimum is positive since
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta^{\prime}}
contains no coincidence or ghost particle.
Hence, in
65
(
2
) we may take
\Delta_{\theta}=\frac{M}{2}>0
for all
\theta\in\overline{\Theta}
,
which then yields
6
(
2
).
To deduce
6
(
1
)
from
65
(
1
) and (
3
)
it suffices to show that
t^{2}
can be bounded from above and
\Delta_{t}
away from zero, uniformly for
t\in\overline{\Sigma}
.
Boundedness of
t^{2}
follows from compactness of
\overline{\Sigma}
, so it remains to show uniform positivity of
\Delta_{t}
.
Picking
\Theta^{\prime}=\overline{\Theta}
, let
\Delta
be minimal such that
u^{\dagger}_{t}
contains a
\Delta
-coincidence or
\Delta
-ghost particle with respect to
\Theta^{\prime}
.
As before,
\Delta
depends continuously on
t
and thus attains its minimum
N
at some
t^{\prime}
in the compact
\overline{\Sigma}
.
That the particles have positive mutual distance in the projections
(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t^{\prime}})_{\theta\in\overline{\Theta}}

and the support
\supp u^{\dagger}_{t^{\prime}}
can be uniquely reconstructed from
(\supp(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u^{\dagger}_{t^{\prime}}))_{\theta\in\overline{\Theta}}

implies that
u^{\dagger}_{t^{\prime}}
contains no
0
-coincidence or
0
-ghost particle, hence
N>0
and we may choose
\Delta_{t}=\frac{N}{2}>0
for all
t\in\overline{\Sigma}
, as desired.

For proving our results on convergence rates we will exploit that the optimization problem has multiple equivalent formulations due to our constraint of nonnegative masses.
Indeed, we may write the regularizer as the norm of the snapshots or equivalently the norm of the auxiliary variable
\gamma
.
As seen in the previous section, the derivation of error estimates
requires the construction of appropriate dual variables, which in our case is somewhat involved.
In particular, to obtain error estimates on the particle configurations at all time points outside
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
,
the error information needs to be passed from the snapshots at times
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
to the variable
\gamma
and then back again,
just like the procedure for exact reconstruction in
Section

3.1
,
which is reflected in a stepwise construction of the corresponding dual variables.
In particular, we will reduce the derivation of error estimates on
(u,\gamma)
to the derivation of error estimates for the much simpler static reconstruction problem
of finding an approximation
u_{t}
to
u_{t}^{\dagger}
from a noisy observation of
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}^{\dagger}
.
This way the role of the observation operator
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
is separated from the role of combining measurements from multiple time points.
An error analysis of the
static problem
is a classic superresolution problem and has for a particular choice of
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
already been performed by Candès and Fernandez-Granda
[
14
]

(even though they did not use our language of unbalanced Wasserstein distances).
Our contribution, in contrast, is to show how the error estimates for the static problem transfer to error estimates of the
dynamic problem
.
Our analysis also applies to the reconstruction model (
7
) by Alberti et al.
[
3
]
, which forms the motivation of our work,
but we will only perform it explicitly for our novel reconstruction model

Π

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

,
since this is the more complicated case and has the dimensionality advantage over (
7
).
In the following we will say the measure
\nu^{\dagger}
from
5
is
(\kappa,\mu,R)
-stably reconstructible via
v^{\dagger},v^{s}

to indicate the corresponding dual variables and values of
\kappa
,
\mu
, and
R
.
The static problem admits an error analysis if the measure to be reconstructed is stably reconstructible, as stated next.

Theorem 66

(Stably reconstructible measure)
.

If
\nu^{\dagger}
is
(\kappa,\mu,R)
-stably reconstructible via
v^{\dagger},v^{s}
and
f^{\delta}
is an approximation to
f^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\nu^{\dagger}
with
F_{f^{\delta}}(f^{\dagger})\leq\delta
,
then the solution to

\min_{\nu^{\delta}}\|\nu^{\delta}\|_{+}+\frac{1}{\alpha}F_{f^{\delta}}(\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\nu^{\delta})

satisfies

W_{2,R}^{2}(\nu^{\delta},\nu^{\dagger})\leq\tfrac{12\!+\!7\max\{\kappa,\mu\}R^{2}}{4\kappa\alpha}\left(\delta\!+\!F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha v^{\dagger})\!+\!F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha v^{\dagger})\!+\!\!\!\max_{s\in\{-1,1\}^{N}}\!\!F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\alpha v^{s})\!+\!F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\alpha v^{s})\right).

In particular, for
\delta=0
the limit
\alpha\to 0
implies
\nu^{\delta}=\nu^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

This is just the special case of
64
for
y^{\delta}_{1}=\nu^{\delta}
,
K=\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
,
w^{\dagger}=v^{\dagger}
,
w=v^{s}
with
s_{i}=\sgn((\nu^{\delta}-\nu^{\dagger})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})))
,

y^{\delta}_{2}
and
G_{2}
nonexistent.
∎

Depending on the measurement operator, simple criteria for stable reconstructibility can be found.
The following example in one space dimension is proven in
[
14
, Lem. 2.4 & 2.5]

(higher-dimensional versions are also provided in that work, albeit without detail).

Theorem 67

(Stably reconstructible measure for truncated Fourier series, Candès & Fernandez-Granda 2013)
.

Let
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
be the truncated Fourier series on
\Omega=S^{1}
which only keeps coefficients up to a maximum absolute frequency
\Phi>0
.
There exist positive constants
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
such that,
if the Dirac locations in
u^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
have distance no smaller than
\Delta=c_{0}/\Phi
to each other,
then
u^{\dagger}
is
(c_{1}\Phi^{2},c_{2}\Phi^{2},c_{3}/\Phi)
-stably reconstructible.

Before we begin the derivation of error estimates for
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
, let us introduce the abbreviations

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\to\mathcal{M}(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}u

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t})_{\theta\in\Theta,t\in\Sigma},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}\to\mathcal{M}(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}\gamma

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}\gamma_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta,t\in\Sigma},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\to H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}u

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}},

\displaystyle\iota_{+}

\displaystyle:\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{M}(A_{i})\to\{0,\infty\},

\displaystyle\iota_{+}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(A_{i})\text{ for all }i\in I,\\
\infty&\text{else,}\end{cases}

where
I
denotes an arbitrary index set and
A_{i}
are Lipschitz domains.
Note that the above linear operators are all continuous with respect to the product topology
(a map
M:\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}\to\prod_{j\in J}Y_{j}
between product spaces with the product topology is continuous
if and only if
p_{j}\circ M
is continuous for all projections
p_{j}:\prod_{j\in J}Y_{j}\to Y_{j}
,
and for all above operators
p_{j}\circ M
is the composition of a continuous projection
q_{i}:\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}\to X_{i}
with a continuous linear operator between Banach spaces).
Furthermore,
\iota_{0}
shall be the convex indicator function of the set
\{0\}
.
To translate the setting from the previous section to

Π

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

we choose

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}\equiv(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger}),\,y^{\delta}\equiv(u,\gamma)\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\times\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}\equiv X,

\displaystyle K\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}&0\\
\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}&-\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}\end{smallmatrix}\right):X\to H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\times\mathcal{M}(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma}\equiv Y,

\displaystyle F_{f}(a,b)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|a_{t}-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}+\iota_{0}(b),\,G(u,\gamma)=\iota_{+}(u,\gamma)+\ast,

where
\ast
will either be
\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
or
\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for particular choices of
\theta
and
t
,
