depending on what is most convenient for the desired estimate
(by
20
and
28
and the constraint
\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}u=\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}\gamma
, all these choices are equivalent).
The corresponding predual spaces are given by

\displaystyle X^{*}

\displaystyle=\{(\phi,\psi)\in C(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\times C(\Gamma)^{\Theta}\,|\text{ only finitely many }\phi_{t},\psi_{\theta}\text{ are nonzero}\},

\displaystyle Y^{*}

\displaystyle=\{(v,q)\in(H^{*})^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\times C(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma}\,|\text{ only finitely many }q_{\theta,t}\text{ are nonzero}\},

endowed with the final topology induced by the inclusions

\phi_{t}\mapsto(\phi,\psi)\in X^{*}
with
\phi_{\tilde{t}}=0
for all
\tilde{t}\neq t
,

\psi_{\theta}\mapsto(\phi,\psi)\in X^{*}
with
\psi_{\tilde{\theta}}=0
for all
\tilde{\theta}\neq\theta

and analogously for
Y^{*}

(it is straightforward to see that any continuous linear functional on
X^{*}
or
Y^{*}
is an element of
X
or
Y
).
Note that above we slightly changed our notation compared to the previous section in that
f
here only denotes the first part of
Ky^{\dagger}
.
This change has no consequence for the validity of the results and arguments,
hence we decided not to complicate the notation of the previous section further by directly including this from the start.
This choice of fidelity has Legendre–Fenchel conjugate
F_{f}^{*}(w_{1},w_{2})=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\tfrac{1}{2}\|(w_{1})_{t}\|_{H^{*}}^{2}+\langle(w_{1})_{t},f_{t}\rangle
, thus

F_{f}^{*}(2\alpha w)+F_{f}^{*}(-2\alpha w)=4\alpha^{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|(w_{1})_{t}\|_{H^{*}}^{2}=:4\alpha^{2}|w_{1}|^{2}.

(19)

For easier reference let us also recall the following subgradients,

\displaystyle w\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{+}(\nu)\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad w\leq 1\text{ everywhere, }w=1\text{ on }\supp\nu,

\displaystyle w\in\partial\iota_{+}(\nu)\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad w\leq 0\text{ everywhere, }w=0\text{ on }\supp\nu.

Just like in
Section

3.1

we begin with estimating the reconstruction properties only for those snapshots that are stably reconstructible:
We show that the error estimate from
66
is not hindered by the dynamic setting.

Theorem 68

(Error estimates for good snapshots)
.

Let
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
be such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is
(\kappa,\mu,R)
-stably reconstructible via
v^{\dagger},v^{s}
from measurements with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
.
Then the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
with
\alpha=\sqrt{\delta}
satisfies

\displaystyle W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u_{t}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12\!+\!7\max\{\kappa,\mu\}R^{2}}{4\kappa}\left(1+4|v^{\dagger}|^{2}+\max_{s\in\{-1,1\}^{N}}4|v^{s}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta}.

Proof.

We have
u_{t}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}+tv_{i}}
.
Set
s=(\sgn((u_{t}-u_{t}^{\dagger})(B_{R}(\{x_{i}+tv_{i}\}))))_{i=1,\ldots,N}
and let
v^{\dagger}
and
v^{s}
be the dual variables from
5
(with
\nu^{\dagger}=u_{t}^{\dagger}
).
In order to apply
64
we split
y^{\delta}=(u,\gamma)
into
y^{\delta}_{1}=u_{t}
and
y^{\delta}_{2}=((u_{\tilde{t}})_{\tilde{t}\neq t},\gamma)

and consider the regularizer

G(u,\gamma)=\|u_{t}\|_{+}+\iota_{+}((u_{\tilde{t}})_{\tilde{t}\neq t},\gamma).

Then the choice
w^{\dagger}={\tilde{v}^{\dagger}\choose 0}
,
w={\tilde{v}\choose 0}
satisfies the conditions of
64
,
where
\tilde{v}^{\dagger}_{t}=v^{\dagger}
,
\tilde{v}_{t}=v^{s}
and
\tilde{v}^{\dagger}_{\tilde{t}}=\tilde{v}_{\tilde{t}}=0
for all
\tilde{t}\neq t
.
Furthermore,
(K^{*}w)_{2}=0
so that the result follows from
63
.
∎

In analogy to
Section

3.1
,
from the error estimates for the stably reconstructible snapshots we cannot directly obtain error estimates for all other snapshots
since all snapshots are only indirectly coupled via the position-velocity projections.
We therefore first use the just constructed dual variables to obtain dual variables for error estimates on
\gamma
.
Here we will also obtain an unbalanced Wasserstein bound;
its maximum transport radius
R
will depend on how close the configuration is to producing a coincidence or a ghost particle in the following sense.

Definition 69

(
\Delta
-coincidence and
\Delta
-ghost particle)
.

Let
\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},v_{i})}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
represent a set of particles and
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{R}
a set of times.

1.

Configuration
\nu
contains a
\Delta
-coincidence
with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

if two distinct particles
i\neq j
come
\Delta
-close at some time
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, that is,
\dist(x_{j}+tv_{j},x_{i}+tv_{i})\leq\Delta
.

2.

Configuration
\nu
contains a
\Delta
-ghost particle
at
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

if at each
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
there is a particle
i
(not the same for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
) with
\dist(x+tv,x_{i}+tv_{i})\leq\Delta
.

A
\Delta
-ghost particle is thus a nonexistent particle which would be consistent with all snapshots if the finest measurement resolution at the different time points were
\Delta
.
Plain ghost particles are
0
-ghost particles in this sense, plain coincidences are
0
-coincidences.

Theorem 70

(Error estimates for
\gamma
)
.

Let
\theta\in\Theta
and assume there is
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}

such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is
(\hat{\kappa},\hat{\mu},\hat{R})
-stably reconstructible via
\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger},\hat{v}^{s}_{t}
from measurements with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Then the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
satisfies

\displaystyle W_{2,R}^{2}(\gamma_{\theta},\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12+7\kappa R^{2}}{4\kappa}\left(1+4\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta}+\frac{1}{2R^{2}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u^{\dagger}_{t}).

for
\kappa=\sigma\hat{\kappa}
and
R=\min\{\hat{R},\Delta/3,\Delta^{\prime}\}/\sqrt{1+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}t^{2}}
,
where
\sigma
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\frac{1}{\card{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}{1\choose-t}\otimes{1\choose-t}

and
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
are such that
\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
contains no
\Delta
-coincidence or
\Delta^{\prime}
-ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

This time split
y^{\delta}=(u,\gamma)
into
y^{\delta}_{1}=\gamma_{\theta}
and
y^{\delta}_{2}=(u,(\gamma_{\tilde{\theta}})_{\tilde{\theta}\neq\theta})

and consider the regularizer

G(u,\gamma)=\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{+}+\iota_{+}(u,(\gamma_{\tilde{\theta}})_{\tilde{\theta}\neq\theta}).

In order to apply
64
we need to construct appropriate dual variables
w^{\dagger}
and
w
.
To this end define
v^{\dagger}\in(H^{*})^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}

via
v^{\dagger}_{t}=\hat{v}^{\dagger}_{t}/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and
v^{\dagger}_{t}=0
else.
Furthermore define
q^{\dagger}\in C(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma}
as

q^{\dagger}_{\theta,t}(x)=(1-\hat{\kappa}\min\{\bar{R},\dist(x,\supp\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}^{\dagger})\}^{2})/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\quad\text{for }t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

and
q^{\dagger}_{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{t}}=0
for
\tilde{t}\notin{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
or
\tilde{\theta}\neq\theta
,
where
\bar{R}=\min\{\hat{R},\Delta/3,\Delta^{\prime}\}
.
Due to
\bar{R}\leq\Delta/3
, each
q_{\theta,t}^{\dagger}
contains
\card{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
distinct parabolas of width
2\bar{R}
.
Also, let
s_{i}=\sgn(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{(x_{i}\cdot\theta,v_{i}\cdot\theta)\}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, and define
q\in C(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma}

as
q_{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{t}}=0
for
\tilde{t}\notin{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
or
\tilde{\theta}\neq\theta
and, for
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, as

q_{\theta,t}(x)=s_{i}/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\quad\text{if }x\in B_{\bar{R}}(\{\theta\cdot(x_{i}+tv_{i})\}),

continuously extended onto the rest of
\Xi
with absolute value no larger than
1/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}
(which again is possible due to
\bar{R}\leq\Delta/3
).
Now
w^{\dagger}={v^{\dagger}\choose q^{\dagger}}
and
w={0\choose q}
satisfy the conditions of
64
with
\mu=0
.
Indeed, due to
\bar{R}\leq\hat{R}
we have
-\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}^{*}q^{\dagger}_{\theta,t}\leq\mathrm{Ob}_{t}^{*}v^{\dagger}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
by construction (with equality on the support of
u_{t}^{\dagger}
) so that
\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*}v^{\dagger}+\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger}\geq 0
, thus

-(\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*}v^{\dagger}+\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger})\in\partial\iota_{+}.

(20)

Furthermore, by construction, in the neighbourhood

\mathcal{N}_{i}=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\textstyle\left\{{x\choose v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\middle|\,-\bar{R}\leq{1\choose t}\cdot\left[{x\choose v}-{x_{i}\cdot\theta\choose v_{i}\cdot\theta}\right]\leq\bar{R}\right\}

of
(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})
we have

((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q^{\dagger})_{\theta}(x,v)=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}q_{\theta,t}^{\dagger}(x,v)=1-\hat{\kappa}(x-\theta\cdot x_{i},v-\theta\cdot v_{i})Q{x-\theta\cdot x_{i}\choose v-\theta\cdot v_{i}}

for the positive definite matrix
Q=\frac{1}{\card{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}{1\choose-t}\otimes{1\choose-t}
so that from
\kappa\leq\sigma\hat{\kappa}
we obtain

((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q^{\dagger})_{\theta}(x,v)\leq 1-\kappa\dist((x,v),\supp{\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}})^{2}

in the neighbourhood
\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{N}_{i}
of
\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
.
In particular,
((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q^{\dagger})_{\theta}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{+}(\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})
(note that it is everywhere bounded by one).
Furthermore, due to
\bar{R}\leq\Delta^{\prime}
there are no regions other than the
\mathcal{N}_{i}
in which
((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q^{\dagger})_{\theta}=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}q_{\theta,t}^{\dagger}
can be greater than
1-\hat{\kappa}\bar{R}^{2}
.
Since
B_{R}(\{x_{i}\})\subset\mathcal{N}_{i}
for the choice
R=\bar{R}/\sqrt{1+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}t^{2}}
we thus actually have

((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q^{\dagger})_{\theta}(x,v)\leq 1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist((x,v),\supp{\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}})\}^{2}.

(21)

Summarizing,
w^{\dagger}
satisfies the conditions of
64
.
Finally, by the same argument,

\displaystyle\textstyle((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q)_{\theta}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\sum_{(x,v)\in\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}}(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{(x,v)\}))\delta_{(x,v)}\right)

\displaystyle\text{and }((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q)_{\theta}(x,v)=s_{i}\quad\text{for }(x,v)\in B_{R}(\{(x_{i}\cdot\theta,v_{i}\cdot\theta)\})

so that also
w
satisfies the conditions of
64
and we obtain the estimate from
63
with
\mu=0
.

To conclude, we note that by construction
1=|\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q)_{t}(x)|
for
x\in B_{\bar{R}}(\supp u_{t}^{\dagger})
and any
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Thus,

\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},y^{\delta}_{2}-y^{\dagger}_{2}\rangle=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\langle(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q)_{t},u_{t}-u^{\dagger}_{t}\rangle\leq\frac{1}{\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\bigg(|u_{t}-u^{\dagger}_{t}|(\Omega\setminus B_{R}(\supp u_{t}^{\dagger}))\\
+\sum_{x\in\supp u_{t}^{\dagger}}|(u_{t}-u_{t}^{\dagger})(B_{R}(\{x\}))|\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{2R^{2}\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u^{\dagger}_{t}),

where we exploited that in the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence
W_{2,R}^{2}
mass is never transported further than
R
.
∎

Remark 71

(Signed particles)
.

For the above error estimates it is essential that all particles have nonnegative mass.
We exploited this explicitly by the fact that each time we can choose our regularizer to be the total variation of exactly that variable
for which we would like to derive an error estimate.
Furthermore, we exploit this in the fact that we only require
\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*}v^{\dagger}+\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger}\geq 0
in the above construction of dual variables.
Had we considered signed measures, then this condition would turn into
\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*}v^{\dagger}+\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger}=0
.
However, the range of the predual observation operator
\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*}
and the predual Radon transform
\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}
will typically have a very small intersection
which leaves too little freedom to construct appropriate dual variables.
For instance, if we want
q^{\dagger}_{\theta,t}
to be nonzero only for one
\theta
and if
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
measures the Fourier coefficients at a finite number of integer frequencies,
then for irrational
\theta
the only possible
q^{\dagger}_{\theta,t}
is a constant.

Finally, we derive an error estimate for all those “bad” snapshots that are not stably reconstructible from
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
,
for instance because their particles are too close together.
Their reconstruction works nevertheless based solely on those snapshots that
are
stably reconstructible from
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
.
Again in analogy to
Section

3.1
,
the necessary additional information to reconstruct the bad snapshots comes from the already reconstructed position-velocity projections,
so the corresponding dual variables will be based on the dual variables from
70
for reconstructing
\gamma
.
In addition, the construction of the dual variables is analogous to the construction in
70
,
only the roles of
u
and
\gamma
as well as of
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and
\Theta^{\prime}
swap.
This also means that we require an analogue of
\Delta
-coincidences and
\Delta
-ghost particles.

Definition 72

(
\Delta
-coincidence and
\Delta
-ghost particle with respect to directions)
.

Let
u=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
represent a set of particles and
\Theta^{\prime}\subset S^{d-1}
a set of directions.

1.

Configuration
u
contains a
\Delta
-coincidence
with respect to
\Theta^{\prime}

if two distinct particles
i\neq j
seem
\Delta
-close in some projection
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
, that is,
\dist(\theta\cdot x_{j},\theta\cdot x_{i})\leq\Delta
.

2.

Configuration
u
contains a
\Delta
-ghost particle
at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to
\Theta^{\prime}

if for each
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
there is a particle
i
(not the same for all
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
) with
\dist(\theta\cdot x,\theta\cdot x_{i})\leq\Delta
.

Theorem 73

(Error estimates for bad snapshots)
.

Assume there is
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}

such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is
(\hat{\kappa},\hat{\mu},\hat{R})
-stably reconstructible via
\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger},\hat{v}^{s}_{t}
from measurements with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Let
t\in\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and pick any finite
\Theta^{\prime}\subset\Theta
.
Then the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
satisfies

\displaystyle W_{2,\bar{R}}^{2}(u_{t},u_{t}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12+7\bar{\kappa}\bar{R}^{2}}{4\bar{\kappa}}\left(1+4\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta}+\frac{1}{2\bar{R}^{2}}\max_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}W_{2,\bar{R}}^{2}(\gamma_{\theta},\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})

for
\bar{\kappa}=\sigma\kappa/(1+t^{2})
and
\bar{R}=\min\{(1+t^{2})R,\Delta/(3\sqrt{1+t^{2}}),\Delta^{\prime}\}
,
where
\sigma
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\frac{1}{\card\Theta^{\prime}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}\theta\otimes\theta
,

\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
are such that
u^{\dagger}_{t}
contains no
\Delta
-coincidence or
\Delta^{\prime}
-ghost particle with respect to
\Theta^{\prime}
,
and
\kappa,R\geq 0
are the minimum values from the statement of
70
, applied to all
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This time we consider the splitting of
y^{\delta}=(u,\gamma)
into
y^{\delta}_{1}=u_{t}
and
y^{\delta}_{2}=((u_{\tilde{t}})_{\tilde{t}\neq t},\gamma)
as well as the regularizer choice

G(u,\gamma)=\|u_{t}\|_{+}+\iota_{+}((u_{\tilde{t}})_{\tilde{t}\neq t},\gamma).

For
\tilde{\kappa}
to be determined later, define
q^{\dagger}\in C(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma}
as

q^{\dagger}_{\theta,t}(x)=-(1-\tilde{\kappa}\min\{\bar{R},\dist(x,\supp\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})\}^{2})/\card{\Theta^{\prime}}\quad\text{for }\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}

and
q^{\dagger}_{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{t}}=0
for
\tilde{t}\neq t
or
\tilde{\theta}\notin\Theta^{\prime}
.
Due to
\bar{R}\leq\Delta/3
, each
q_{\theta,t}^{\dagger}
contains
\card{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
distinct parabolas of width
2\bar{R}
.
Also, let
s_{i}=\sgn(u_{t}-u_{t}^{\dagger})(B_{\bar{R}}(\{x_{i}+tv_{i}\}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, and define
q\in C(\Xi)^{\Theta\times\Sigma}

as
q_{\tilde{\theta},\tilde{t}}=0
for
\tilde{t}\neq t
or
\tilde{\theta}\notin\Theta^{\prime}
and, for
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
, as

q_{\theta,t}(x)=-s_{i}/\card{\Theta^{\prime}}\quad\text{if }x\in B_{\Delta/3}(\theta\cdot(x_{i}+tv_{i})),

continuously extended onto the rest of
\Xi
with absolute value no larger than
1/\card{\Theta^{\prime}}
.
Let further
w^{\theta}\in Y^{*}
for
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
denote the first dual variable constructed in the proof of
70
(there denoted
w^{\dagger}
),
and define

w^{\dagger}={0\choose q^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{\card{\Theta^{\prime}}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}w^{\theta},\quad w={0\choose q}.

We now pick
\tilde{\kappa}
small enough such that the conditions of
64
are satisfied with
\mu=0
.
First note that
((0,(\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*})w^{\dagger})_{\theta}
is zero for all
\theta\notin\Theta^{\prime}
.
Now recall from (
21
) that for
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
we have

(0,(\mathrm{Mv}^{1})^{*})w^{\theta}(x,v)\leq 1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist((x,v),\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})\}^{2}
.
Thus, for the choice
\tilde{\kappa}=\kappa/(1+t^{2})
and due to
\bar{R}\leq(1+t^{2})R
and
\bar{R}\leq\Delta/3
we have
(0,(\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*})w^{\dagger}\leq 0
everywhere
with equality on
\supp\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta}
.
Next recall from (
20
) that
w^{\theta}
satisfies
(\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*},\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*})w^{\theta}\geq 0
(with equality on the support of
u^{\dagger}
),
thus by construction we have
(-(\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*},\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*})w^{\dagger})_{\tilde{t}}\leq 0
for
\tilde{t}\neq t
with equality on
\supp u_{\tilde{t}}^{\dagger}
.
Furthermore,
(-(\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}^{*},\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*})w^{\dagger})_{t}=-(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger})_{t}\leq 1
with equality on
\supp u_{t}
.
Even more, by construction, in the neighbourhood

\mathcal{N}_{i}=\bigcap_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}\textstyle\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\middle|\,-\bar{R}\leq\theta\cdot\left[x-(x_{i}+tv_{i})\right]\leq\bar{R}\right\}

of
x_{i}+tv_{i}
we have

-(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger})_{t}=-\sum_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}^{*}q^{\dagger}_{\theta,t}=1-\tilde{\kappa}(x-x_{i}-tv_{i})^{T}Q(x-x_{i}-tv_{i})

for the symmetric positive semi-definite
Q=\frac{1}{\card\Theta^{\prime}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}\theta\otimes\theta

so that from
\bar{\kappa}\leq\sigma\tilde{\kappa}
we obtain

-(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q^{\dagger})_{t}(x)\leq 1-\bar{\kappa}\min\{\bar{R},\dist(x,\supp u_{t})\}^{2}

(here we exploited
\bar{R}\leq\Delta^{\prime}
).
Summarizing,
w^{\dagger}
fulfills the conditions from
64
.
By the same argument,

\displaystyle\textstyle-(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q)_{t}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\sum_{x\in\supp u_{t}^{\dagger}}(u_{t}-u_{t}^{\dagger})(B_{\bar{R}}(x))\delta_{x}\right)

\displaystyle\text{and }-(\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}^{*}q)_{t}(x)=s_{i}\text{ for }x\in B_{\bar{R}}(\{x_{i}+tv_{i}\})

so that also
w
satisfies the conditions of
64
and we obtain the estimate from
63
with
\mu=0

(note that
w^{\dagger}_{1}=v^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{\card{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\hat{v}^{\dagger}
using the notation from the proof of
70
).

To conclude, we note that, due to
\bar{R}\leq\Delta/(3\sqrt{1+t^{2}})
, by construction we have
1=|\card{\Theta^{\prime}}((\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}})^{*}q)_{\theta}(x,v)|
for
(x,v)\in B_{\bar{R}}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})
and any
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
. Thus,

\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},y^{\delta}_{2}-y^{\dagger}_{2}\rangle=-\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}\langle(\mathrm{Mv}^{1})^{*}q_{\theta,t},\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}\rangle\leq\frac{1}{\card{\Theta^{\prime}}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}\bigg(|\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}|(\Gamma\setminus B_{\bar{R}}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}))\\
+\sum_{(x,v)\in\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}}|(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})(B_{\bar{R}}(\{(x,v)\}))|\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{2\bar{R}^{2}\card{\Theta^{\prime}}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}}W_{2,\bar{R}}^{2}(\gamma_{\theta},\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}),

where again we exploited that in the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence
W_{2,\bar{R}}^{2}
mass is never transported further than
\bar{R}
.
∎

65
for
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
now follows from combining
68
,
70
and
73
.

4.4
Construction of dual variables for

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

We now modify the error analysis from the previous
Section
for reconstruction via
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})

to an error analysis for the model
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
.
Since in this model the slices
\gamma_{\theta}
are only defined for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta\in\Theta
,
our error estimate will also just hold for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every slice.
This time we employ the operators

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\to\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)^{\Sigma},

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}}u

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Rd}u_{t})_{t\in\Sigma},

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\to\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)^{\Sigma},

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}}\gamma

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}}\gamma)_{t\in\Sigma},

and choose

\displaystyle y^{\dagger}\equiv(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger}),\,y^{\delta}\equiv(u^{\delta},\gamma^{\delta})\in\mathcal{M}(\Omega)^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\times\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\equiv X,

\displaystyle K\equiv\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\underline{\mathrm{Ob}}&0\\
\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}}&-\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Mv}^{1}}}\end{smallmatrix}\right):X\to H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\times\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)^{\Sigma}\equiv Y,

\displaystyle F_{f}(a,b)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|a_{t}-f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}+\iota_{0}(b),\,G(u,\gamma)=\iota_{+}(u,\gamma)+\ast,

where
\ast
will either be
\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for some
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
or a weighted total variation of
\gamma
,

\|\gamma\|_{h}=\int_{\Theta\times\Gamma}h(\theta)\>\mathrm{d}|\gamma|(\theta,(x,v))=\int_{\Theta}\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}h(\theta)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}

for some fixed weight
h:\Theta\to[0,\infty)
with
\int_{\Theta}h\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}=1

(the rightmost expression is well-defined for all
\gamma
admissible in

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

by
33
).
As before, all these choices for
\ast
are equivalent.

Essentially, the necessary changes to the analysis are the following, where we now work backwards through the previous
Sections
.

•

For the estimates we needed to construct dual variables
w^{\dagger}
(and similarly
w
) such that
K^{*}w^{\dagger}\in\partial G(y^{\dagger})
.
Since in the previous
Section
the regularizer acted separately on each
u_{t}
and
\gamma_{\theta}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
and
\theta\in\Theta
,
this amounted to constructing continuous functions
q_{\theta,t}^{\dagger}
separately for each
t,\theta
.
Now, however, that our variable
\gamma
stems from
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
, these dual variables will have to be continuous in
\theta
,
thus we need new versions of
70
and
73

(while the construction from
68
stays unchanged apart from replacing the space
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}
by
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
everywhere).
Roughly, the strategy is to smooth
q_{\theta,t}^{\dagger}
a little out in
\theta
.

•

So far we only had to estimate the mass distribution of approximations to discrete measures,
for which we used Bregman distances with respect to the total variation. Now however, when we aim for estimating the mass distribution in
\gamma
,
the corresponding ground truth measure
\gamma^{\dagger}
is not discrete, but rather a measure concentrated on codimension-2 manifolds
(each
\theta
-slice
\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
is discrete, however, these are only well-defined
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost everywhere so that our earlier estimates do not carry over).
Hence we will require a new version of
62
,
where weighted averages of mass distributions of
\gamma_{\theta}
are estimated based on Bregman distances with respect to weighted total variations.

•

An additional argument is required to pass from mass distribution estimates of weighted averages of
\gamma_{\theta}
to estimates for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta
.

We begin with a new version of
62
, for simplicity directly adapted to our setting of estimating the mass distribution in
\gamma
.

Theorem 74

(Weighted mass distribution from Bregman distance)
.

Let
h:\Theta\to[0,\infty)
be continuous with
\int_{\Theta}h\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}=1
.
Assume
\gamma^{\dagger}=\mathrm{Rj}\lambda^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
for a discrete measure
\lambda^{\dagger}
with particles
(x_{1},v_{1}),\ldots,(x_{N},v_{N})
,
and let
v^{\dagger}\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{h}(\gamma^{\dagger})\subset C(\Theta\times\Gamma)
.
If
v^{\dagger}
satisfies

v^{\dagger}(\theta,(x,v))\leq h(\theta)(1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist((x,v),\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})\}^{2})

for some
\kappa,R>0
with
2R
smaller than the minimum distance between the points in the support of
\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
for
\theta\in\supp h
,
then for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
of the form
\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)|\gamma_{\theta}|(\Gamma\setminus B_{R}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{v^{\dagger}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}\dist(p,p_{i}^{\theta})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|\gamma_{\theta}|(p)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{v^{\dagger}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger}),

where we abbreviated
p_{i}^{\theta}=(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})
.
Furthermore, let
v\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{h}(\bar{\gamma})\subset C(\Theta\times\Gamma)
for

\bar{\gamma}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}{\mbox{\LARGE$\llcorner$}}\{p_{i}^{\theta}\,|\,\theta\in\Theta\}\quad\text{ with }s_{i}=\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}.

If
v
satisfies

s_{i}v(\theta,p)\geq h(\theta)(1-\mu\dist(p,p_{i}^{\theta})^{2})\quad\text{for all }p\in B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}),\,i=1,\ldots,N,

for some
\mu>0
, then we additionally have

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\right|\leq\frac{1+\mu R^{2}}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{v^{\dagger}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger})+\langle v,\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle.

Proof.

Abbreviate
B_{\theta}^{c}=\Gamma\setminus B_{R}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})
, then the first two inequalities follow from

\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)\kappa R^{2}|\gamma_{\theta}|(B_{\theta}^{c})+h(\theta)\kappa\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}\dist(p,p_{i}^{\theta})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|\gamma_{\theta}|p\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\\
\leq\int_{\Theta}\int_{B_{\theta}^{c}}h(\theta)-v^{\dagger}(\theta,p)\>\mathrm{d}\gamma_{\theta}(p)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}h(\theta)-v^{\dagger}(\theta,p)\>\mathrm{d}\gamma_{\theta}(p)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\\
=\int_{\Theta\times\Gamma}(h(\theta)-v^{\dagger}(\theta,p))\>\mathrm{d}\gamma(\theta,p)=\|\gamma\|_{h}-\langle v^{\dagger},\gamma\rangle=\|\gamma\|_{h}-\|\gamma^{\dagger}\|_{h}-\langle v^{\dagger},\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle=D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{v^{\dagger}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger}).

The third inequality is obtained as follows. We have

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\right|\\
=\int_{\{(\theta,p)\,|\,\dist(p,\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})<R\}}v\>\mathrm{d}(\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Theta}\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}h(\theta)s_{i}-v\>\mathrm{d}(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta},

where the first summand can be estimated as

\int_{\{(\theta,p)\,|\,\dist(p,\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})<R\}}v\>\mathrm{d}(\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger})=\langle v,\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle-\int_{\Theta}\int_{B_{\theta}^{c}}v\>\mathrm{d}(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\\
\leq\langle v,\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle+\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)|\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}|(B_{\theta}^{c})\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\leq\langle v,\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle+\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{v^{\dagger}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger}),

while the second term satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Theta}\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}h(\theta)s_{i}-v\>\mathrm{d}(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\\
\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}\mu\dist(p,p_{i}^{\theta})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|\gamma_{\theta}|(p)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta)\leq\frac{\mu}{\kappa}D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{v^{\dagger}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger}).\mbox{\qed}

The previous mass distribution estimate can now be used to obtain estimates on
\gamma

in the following, previously announced new version of
70
.
In contrast to
70
it does not give an estimate for single slices
\gamma_{\theta}
since now
\gamma
is viewed as a variable in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
.
For this reason the error estimate is not merely expressed in terms of the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence, which would be too weak an estimate:
The unbalanced Wasserstein divergence between the reconstruction
\gamma
and the ground truth
\gamma^{\dagger}
would also allow mass exchange between different slices
\gamma_{\theta}
.
Instead we will turn the error estimate into an unbalanced Wasserstein divergence estimate for
each separate
slice
\gamma_{\theta}
in the subsequent result.

Theorem 75

(Error estimates for
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
)
.

Let
h:\Theta\to[0,\infty)
be continuous with
\int_{\Theta}h\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}=1
and assume there is
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}

such that
u_{t}^{\dagger}
is
(\hat{\kappa},\hat{\mu},\hat{R})
-stably reconstructible via
\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger},\hat{v}_{t}^{s}
from measurements with
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.
Then, abbreviating
p_{i}^{\theta}=(\theta\cdot x_{i},\theta\cdot v_{i})
, the solution
(u,\gamma)
to
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)|\gamma_{\theta}|(\Gamma\setminus B_{R}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}+2\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\Theta}\!\!h(\theta)\!\!\int_{B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\})}\!\!\!\!\dist(p,p_{i}^{\theta})^{2}\>\mathrm{d}|\gamma_{\theta}|(p)\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\left(\frac{3}{2}+2\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\kappa R^{2}}{\kappa R^{2}}\left(2\!+\!2\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta}\!+\!\frac{1}{2R^{2}}\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u^{\dagger}_{t})

for
\kappa=\sigma\hat{\kappa}
and any
R\leq\min\{\hat{R},\Delta/3,\Delta^{\prime}\}/\sqrt{1+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}t^{2}}
,
where
\sigma
is the smallest eigenvalue of
\frac{1}{\card{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}{1\choose-t}\otimes{1\choose-t}

and
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0
are such that
\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}
for any
\theta\in\supp h
contains no
\Delta
-coincidence or
\Delta^{\prime}
-ghost particle with respect to
{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We split
y^{\delta}=(u,\gamma)
into
y^{\delta}_{1}=\gamma
and
y^{\delta}_{2}=u
and consider the regularizer

G(u,\gamma)=\|\gamma\|_{h}+\iota_{+}(\gamma)+\iota_{+}(u).

As in the proof of
70
define
v^{\dagger}\in(H^{*})^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}

via
v^{\dagger}_{t}=-\hat{v}^{\dagger}_{t}/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and
v^{\dagger}_{\tilde{t}}=0
else.
The dual variables are now constructed as weighted averages (with weight
h
) of the dual variables from
70
.
Indeed, we define
q^{\dagger}\in C(\Theta\times\Gamma)^{\Sigma}
as

q^{\dagger}_{t}(\theta,x)=h(\theta)(1-\hat{\kappa}\min\{\bar{R},\dist(x,\supp\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}^{\dagger})\}^{2})/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\quad\text{for }t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}

and
q^{\dagger}_{t}=0
for
t\notin{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, where
\bar{R}=\min\{\hat{R},\Delta/3,\Delta^{\prime}\}
.
Also, let
s_{i}=\sgn\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)(\gamma_{\theta}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})(B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
,
where the slices
\gamma_{\theta}
are defined via the relation
\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}

(which is possible by
33
).
With this
s_{i}
we define
q\in C(\Theta\times\Gamma)^{\Sigma}

as
q_{t}=0
for
t\notin{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
and, for
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
, as

q_{t}(\theta,x)=h(\theta)s_{i}/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\quad\text{if }x\in B_{\bar{R}}(\theta\cdot(x_{i}+tv_{i})),

continuously extended onto the rest of
\Theta\times\Gamma
with absolute value no larger than
h(\theta)/\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}
.
Now analogously to the proof of
70
it follows that
w^{\dagger}={v^{\dagger}\choose q^{\dagger}}
and
w={0\choose q}
satisfy

\displaystyle-K^{*}w^{\dagger}=-((K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1},(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{2})

\displaystyle\in\partial G(y^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle-(K^{*}w)_{1}

\displaystyle\in\partial\|\cdot\|_{h}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}{\mbox{\LARGE$\llcorner$}}\{p_{i}^{\theta}\,|\,\theta\in\Theta\}\right),

\displaystyle-(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1}(\theta,p)

\displaystyle\leq h(\theta)(1-\kappa\min\{R,\dist(p,\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger})\}^{2}),

\displaystyle-(K^{*}w)_{1}(\theta,p)

\displaystyle=s_{i}h(\theta)\quad\text{for all }p\in B_{R}(\{p_{i}^{\theta}\}).

Thus, using (in this order)
74
,
59
and (
19
) we conclude

\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)|\gamma_{\theta}|(\Gamma\setminus B_{R}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}))\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\leq\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{\|\cdot\|_{h}}_{-(K^{*}w^{\dagger})_{1}}(\gamma,\gamma^{\dagger})\leq\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}D^{G}_{-K^{*}w^{\dagger}}(y^{\delta},y^{\dagger})\\
{}^{\leq}\left(3\delta+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(2\sqrt{\delta}w^{\dagger})+F_{f^{\delta}}^{*}(-2\sqrt{\delta}w^{\dagger})\right)/(2\kappa R^{2}\sqrt{\delta})=\frac{3+4|v^{\dagger}|^{2}}{2\kappa R^{2}}\sqrt{\delta}\leq\frac{3+4\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}^{\dagger}_{t}|^{2}}{2\kappa R^{2}}\sqrt{\delta},

proving the first inequality of the statement.
The second and third inequality follow in exactly the same way (using
\mu=0
in
74
),
where the third needs one additional estimate for
\langle-(K^{*}w)_{1},\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle
:
We employ
\langle-(K^{*}w)_{1},\gamma-\gamma^{\dagger}\rangle=\langle-K^{*}w,(u,\gamma)-(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger})\rangle+\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},u-u^{\dagger}\rangle
,
where the first summand can be estimated via
59
and (
19
) as

\langle-K^{*}w,(u,\gamma)-(u^{\dagger},\gamma^{\dagger})\rangle\leq 2(1+|v^{\dagger}|)\sqrt{\delta}\leq 2(1+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}^{\dagger}_{t}|)\sqrt{\delta},

and for the second summand we note that by construction
1=|\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}(\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}}^{*}q)_{t}(x)|
for
x\in B_{\bar{R}}(\supp u_{t}^{\dagger})
and any
t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
. Thus,

\langle(K^{*}w)_{2},y^{\delta}_{2}-y^{\dagger}_{2}\rangle=\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\langle(\overline{\underline{\mathrm{Rd}}}^{*}q)_{t},u_{t}-u^{\dagger}_{t}\rangle\leq\frac{1}{\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}\bigg(|u_{t}-u^{\dagger}_{t}|(\Omega\setminus B_{R}(\supp u_{t}^{\dagger}))\\
+\sum_{x\in\supp u_{t}^{\dagger}}|(u_{t}-u_{t}^{\dagger})(B_{R}(x))|\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{2R^{2}\card{{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{t},u^{\dagger}_{t}),

where we exploited that in the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence
W_{2,R}^{2}
mass is never transported further than
R
.
∎

As promised,
this result can now be turned into an unbalanced Wasserstein estimate for the slices of
\gamma
,
a yet different variant of
70
.

Theorem 76

(Error estimates for slices of
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
)
.

The statement of
70
holds for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
if
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
is replaced with
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
.

Proof.

Based on
58
,
it suffices to show that one may replace
h\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
in
75
with a Dirac measure
\delta_{\theta}
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost all
\theta
.
We only show this for the first inequality of
75
(the others follow analogously).
Now let

\displaystyle\Delta(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sup\{\Delta\geq 0\,|\,\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}\text{ contains no $\Delta$-coincidence with respect to }{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sup\{\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0\,|\,\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}\text{ contains no $\Delta^{\prime}$-ghost particle with respect to }{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}^{\prime}\}

and note that both are continuous in
\theta
.
Thus, so is
R(\theta)=\min\{\hat{R},\Delta(\theta)/3,\Delta^{\prime}(\theta)\}/\sqrt{1+\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}t^{2}}
.
We will show that for any nonnegative continuous function
\hat{R}(\theta)\leq R(\theta)
and any
\rho<1

there is a piecewise constant function
\bar{R}:S^{d-1}\to[0,\infty)
with
\rho^{2}\hat{R}(\theta)\leq\bar{R}(\theta)\leq\hat{R}(\theta)
and such that

|\gamma_{\theta}|(\Gamma\setminus B_{\bar{R}(\theta)}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}))\leq\frac{1}{\kappa\bar{R}(\theta)^{2}}(\frac{3}{2}+2\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2})\sqrt{\delta}

for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
, which then implies the desired result due to the arbitrariness of
\rho
and
\hat{R}
.
To this end, fix some
\rho\in(0,1)
and let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary.
Abbreviate

S=\{\theta\in\Theta\,|\,\hat{R}(\theta)=0\},

then it suffices to prove the inequality for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
in
\Theta\setminus S
(since on
S
there is nothing to show).
Due to the regularity of
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
we can pick a compact
C\subset\Theta\setminus S
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}((\Theta\setminus S)\setminus C)<\epsilon
.
We will show the inequality for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
in
C
, then the result for
\Theta\setminus S
follows from the arbitrariness of
\epsilon
.
For each
\theta\in C
define

r(\theta)=\sup\{r\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,\rho^{2}\hat{R}(\theta^{\prime})<\rho\hat{R}(\theta)<\hat{R}(\theta^{\prime})\text{ for all $\theta^{\prime}\in S^{d-1}$ with }\dist(\theta,\theta^{\prime})<r\},

then
r(\theta)>0
due to the continuity of
\hat{R}(\theta)
.
Consider the open
r(\hat{\theta})
-ball
B\subset S^{d-1}
around
\hat{\theta}\in C
.
It suffices to prove the desired inequality for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta\in B
(choosing
\bar{R}(\theta)=\rho\hat{R}(\hat{\theta})
),
since the compactum
C
can be covered with finitely many of these balls and the function
\bar{R}(\theta)
can then simply be chosen constant on each ball
(minus the finitely many balls where it was already defined).
Now on
B
, by
75
we have

\int_{\Theta}h(\theta)\left[|\gamma_{\theta}|(\Gamma\setminus B_{R}(\supp\gamma_{\theta}^{\dagger}))-\frac{1}{\kappa R^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}+2\max_{t\in{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}}|\hat{v}_{t}^{\dagger}|^{2}\right)\sqrt{\delta}\right]\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}\leq 0

for
R=\bar{R}(\theta)=\rho\hat{R}(\hat{\theta})

and for any nonnegative continuous
h:B\to[0,\infty)
of compact support.
Therefore the square-bracketed term must be nonpositve for
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta\in B
, which is what we needed to prove.
∎

The error estimate for all remaining snapshots finally stays the same for model
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
as for model
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
.

Theorem 77

(Error estimates for bad snapshots)
.

73
still holds when replacing model
\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
with
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
.

Proof.

The left- and right-hand side of the estimate depend continuously on
\bar{R},\bar{\kappa}

so that it suffices to prove the estimate for any
\bar{\kappa},\bar{R}
strictly smaller than their values from
73
.
As in
73
we consider the splitting of
y^{\delta}=(u,\gamma)
into
y^{\delta}_{1}=u_{t}
and
y^{\delta}_{2}=((u_{\tilde{t}})_{\tilde{t}\neq t},\gamma)
as well as the regularizer choice

G(u,\gamma)=\|u_{t}\|_{+}+\iota_{+}((u_{\tilde{t}})_{\tilde{t}\neq t},\gamma).

We will also use almost exactly the same dual variables as in
73
, only we will smooth them out a little in
\theta
-direction.
To this end, for a finite set
\Theta^{\prime\prime}\subset S^{d-1}
let

\displaystyle\Delta(\Theta^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup\{\Delta\geq 0\,|\,u_{t}^{\dagger}\text{ contains no $\Delta$-coincidence with respect to }\Theta^{\prime\prime}\},

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}(\Theta^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup\{\Delta^{\prime}\geq 0\,|\,u_{t}^{\dagger}\text{ contains no $\Delta^{\prime}$-ghost particle with respect to }\Theta^{\prime\prime}\}

and let
\sigma(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
be the smallest eigenvalue of
\frac{1}{\card\Theta^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{\theta\in\Theta^{\prime\prime}}\theta\otimes\theta
.
Furthermore, let
\kappa(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
and
R(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
be the values of
\kappa
and
R
from the statement of
73
for the choice
\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta^{\prime\prime}
.
Now let
\zeta>0
be small enough and pick
\tilde{\kappa},\tilde{R}
such that the balls
B_{\zeta}(\{\theta\})
are disjoint for all
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}

and such that
\bar{\kappa}\leq\sigma(\Theta^{\prime\prime})\tilde{\kappa}
,
\tilde{\kappa}\leq\kappa(\Theta^{\prime\prime})/(1+t^{2})
,

\bar{R}\leq\min\{(1+t^{2})R(\Theta^{\prime\prime}),\tilde{R}/\sqrt{1+t^{2}},\Delta^{\prime}(\Theta^{\prime\prime})\}
and
\tilde{R}\leq\Delta(\Theta^{\prime\prime})/3

for any perturbation
\Theta^{\prime\prime}
of
\Theta^{\prime}
that perturbs no
\theta\in\Theta^{\prime}
by more than distance
\zeta

(this is possible since we assume
\bar{\kappa}
and
\bar{R}
strictly smaller than their values in
73

and since
\Delta(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
,
\Delta^{\prime}(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
,
\sigma(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
,
\kappa(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
and
R(\Theta^{\prime\prime})
depend continuously on
\Theta^{\prime\prime}
).
Furthermore, for
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta^{\prime}
let
h_{\bar{\theta}}:\Theta\to[0,\infty)
be continuous with
\supp h_{\bar{\theta}}\subset B_{\zeta}(\{\bar{\theta}\})
and
\int_{\Theta}h_{\bar{\theta}}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}=1
.
Now define
q^{\dagger}\in C(\Theta\times\Xi)^{\Sigma}
as

q^{\dagger}_{t}(\theta,x)=-h_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)(1-\tilde{\kappa}\min\{\bar{R},\dist(x,\supp\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\theta})\}^{2})/\card{\Theta^{\prime}}\quad\text{for }\theta\in B_{\zeta}(\{\bar{\theta}\}),\bar{\theta}\in\Theta^{\prime}

and
q^{\dagger}_{\tilde{t}}=0
for
\tilde{t}\neq t
.
Also, let
s_{i}=\sgn(u_{t}-u_{t}^{\dagger})(B_{\bar{R}}(\{x_{i}+tv_{i}\}))
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, and define
q\in C(\Theta\times\Xi)^{\Sigma}

as
q_{\tilde{t}}=0
for
\tilde{t}\neq t
and, otherwise,

q_{t}(\theta,x)=-\sum_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta^{\prime}}h_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)s_{i}/\card{\Theta^{\prime}}\quad\text{if }x\in B_{\tilde{R}}(\theta\cdot(x_{i}+tv_{i})),

continuously extended onto the rest of
\Theta\times\Xi
with absolute value no larger than
h_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)/\card{\Theta^{\prime}}
.
Finally, let
w^{\theta}\in Y^{*}
for
\theta\in\Theta
denote the first dual variables constructed in
70
,
and define

w^{\dagger}={0\choose q^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{\card{\Theta^{\prime}}}\sum_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta^{\prime}}\int_{\Theta}h_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)w^{\theta}\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\theta),\quad w={0\choose q}.

The remainder of the proof (checking the properties of the constructed dual variables and estimating one dual pairing) proceeds analogously to the proof of
73
.
∎

65
for
\mathrm{P}_{\sqrt{\delta}}(f^{\delta})
now follows from combining
68
,
76
and
77
.

5
Discretization and numerical validation

Our goal is now to develop a numerical method in order to solve the dimension-reduced problem

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

. Such a method has to translate the dimension reduction from the continuous formulation to a discrete formulation, which then is more efficient to solve than comparable discretizations of the full-dimensional problem. A straightforward discretization of

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

is achieved by simply gridding the parameter space and replacing Radon measures on the continuous parameter space by vectors representing the weights of a discrete measure supported in (or around) the grid points.

In this section, we assume that the sets
\Sigma
and
\Theta
are finite. Some choices of these parameter sets might be better suited than others, see
Section

3.3
.
To be able to compare different choices of
\Sigma
for our method without changing datasets or domains, we define a slightly less restrictive domain in phase space:
We require particles to stay in
\Omega
only during the measurement times
{\mathcal{T}}
instead of
\Sigma
, i. e., instead of
\Lambda
as defined in (
6
) we use

\tilde{\Lambda}\coloneqq\{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\;|\;x+tv\in\Omega\text{ for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}}\}.

The code to reproduce the results of this paper will be made available at
https://github.com/alexschlueter/supermops
.

5.1
Gridding for snapshots and position-velocity projections

The first challenge is that problem

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

contains multiple Radon measures as variables that should be discretized consistently, namely measures
\gamma_{\theta}
for directions
\theta\in\Theta
as well as snapshots
u_{t}
for times
t\in\Sigma
. For each of these variables we need to generate a corresponding grid.

One option for
\gamma
would be to use a single grid on
\Gamma
in order to discretize all
\gamma_{\theta}
equally, independent of the direction
\theta
.
This however would result in wasted degrees of freedom, because the
\gamma_{\theta}
occupy different parts of
\Gamma
.
For this reason we will generate individual grids for each direction
\theta
.
These should be compatible with the (non-discretized) phase space domain
\tilde{\Lambda}
in the following sense:
If
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}(\tilde{\Lambda})
is a high-dimensional phase space measure, the grid for
\gamma_{\theta}
should cover enough of
\Gamma
such that the joint Radon transform of
\lambda
along direction
\theta
can still be represented, thus at least
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(\tilde{\Lambda})\subset\Gamma
.
Analogous considerations hold for
u
so that we also use individual grids for all
u_{t}
.

In the following we will consider

\Omega=[0,1]^{d}

and furthermore centre without loss of generality the times
{\mathcal{T}}
around
0
so that there exists
T>0
with

[\min{\mathcal{T}},\max{\mathcal{T}}]=[-T,T].

Based on this we now compute the domains of all
\gamma_{\theta}
and
u_{t}
in order to generate tailored grids on these.

Proposition 78

(Domain of
\gamma_{\theta}
)
.

For a given
\theta\in S^{d-1}
define
s_{+}\coloneqq\sum_{j:\theta_{j}>0}\theta_{j}
and
s_{-}\coloneqq\sum_{j:\theta_{j}<0}\theta_{j}
.
The set
\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(\tilde{\Lambda})\subset\Gamma
is a parallelogram in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with vertices

\begin{pmatrix}s_{-}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}s_{+}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\frac{s_{+}+s_{-}}{2}\\
\frac{s_{+}-s_{-}}{2T}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\frac{s_{+}+s_{-}}{2}\\
\frac{s_{-}-s_{+}}{2T}\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

Since we assume
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle(x,v)\in\tilde{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\iff\forall j=1,\dotsc,d:0\leq x_{j}+Tv_{j}\leq 1\land 0\leq x_{j}-Tv_{j}\leq 1

\displaystyle\iff\forall j=1,\dotsc,d:(x_{j},v_{j})\in\conv\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1/2\\
1/(2T)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1/2\\
-1/(2T)\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Denote the convex hull above by
\Lambda_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For a given
\theta\in S^{d-1}
we have, using the Minkowski sum of sets,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(\tilde{\Lambda})

\displaystyle=\{(\theta\cdot x,\theta\cdot v)\;|\;(x,v)\in\tilde{\Lambda}\}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{(y,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\bigg|\exists(x_{j},v_{j})\in\Lambda_{1},j=1,\dotsc,d:(y,w)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\theta_{j}(x_{j},v_{j})\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}\Lambda_{1}+\dotsb+\theta_{d}\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j:\theta_{j}>0}(\theta_{j}\Lambda_{1})+\sum_{j:\theta_{j}<0}(\theta_{j}\Lambda_{1}).

By convexity of
\Lambda_{1}
, we can use the distributivity for Minkowski sums after introducing a minus sign,

\sum_{j:\theta_{j}>0}(\theta_{j}\Lambda_{1})+\sum_{j:\theta_{j}<0}(\theta_{j}\Lambda_{1})=\sum_{j:\theta_{j}>0}(\theta_{j}\Lambda_{1})+\sum_{j:\theta_{j}<0}((-\theta_{j})(-\Lambda_{1}))=s_{+}\Lambda_{1}+(-s_{-})(-\Lambda_{1}).

Note that
-\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{1}-(1,0)
so that

\displaystyle s_{+}\Lambda_{1}+(-s_{-})(-\Lambda_{1})=s_{+}\Lambda_{1}+(-s_{-})\Lambda_{1}+(s_{-},0)=(s_{+}-s_{-})\Lambda_{1}+(s_{-},0).

Applying the scaling and translation in the last expression to the four points inside the definition of
\Lambda_{1}
, we obtain the stated result.
∎

Proposition 79

(Domain of
u_{t}
)
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
be given.

1.

If
|t|\leq T
, we have
\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}(\tilde{\Lambda})=[0,1]^{d}
.

2.

If
|t|>T
, we have
\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}(\tilde{\Lambda})=\left[\frac{1}{2}-\frac{|t|}{2T},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{|t|}{2T}\right]^{d}
.

Proof.

Write

S\coloneqq\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1/2\\
1/(2T)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1/2\\
-1/(2T)\end{pmatrix}\right\}

and define again
\Lambda_{1}=\conv S
. We have

\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}(\tilde{\Lambda})=\{x+tv\;|\;(x,v)\in\tilde{\Lambda}\}=\bigtimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}(\Lambda_{1})

and by linearity of
\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}
we know that

\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}(\Lambda_{1})=\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}(\conv S)=\conv\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}(S)=\conv\left\{0,1,\frac{1}{2}+\frac{t}{2T},\frac{1}{2}-\frac{t}{2T}\right\}\,,

from which the claim follows easily.
∎

Now knowing the exact domains, we generate regular grids for each variable, that is, grids
G_{u}(t)
for each
t\in\Sigma
and grids
G_{\gamma}(\theta)
for each
\theta\in\Theta
. Examples can be seen in
Fig.

1
. The grids
G_{\gamma}(\theta)
are generated by applying an appropriate affine transform to a standard rectangular grid as suggested by
78
.
We employ the same number of
M
grid cells along each dimension to obtain a more or less isotropic resolution (in our numerical experiments,
M=100
or
200
).
Each variable
u_{t}
,
t\in\Sigma
, and
\gamma_{\theta}
,
\theta\in\Theta
, is now discretized as a piecewise constant function on the corresponding grid,
whose masses in the different grid cells (function value times cell volume) are collected in the vectors
\vec{u}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{M^{d}}
,
t\in\Sigma
, and
\vec{\gamma}_{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M^{2}}
,
\theta\in\Theta
.

5.2
Discretized operators and optimization problem

Figure 1
:
Example grids and projections. The green line in projection direction
\theta
is subdivided into red bins corresponding to the grid
G_{r}(\theta,t)
. The contribution of a grid cell to the projection onto a red bin
[r_{i},r_{i+1}]
is determined by its relative area of intersection (visualized by opacity) with the strip orthogonal to
\theta
.

Next, the operators
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}
and
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}
need to be discretized. We restrict our attention to the case
d=2
for simplicity and note that, for two spatial dimensions, these operators are identical up to a rescaling: For every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}={[x\mapsto\sqrt{1+t^{2}}x]}_{\#}\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta(t)}
, where
\theta(t)\coloneqq(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}
. Thus it will be sufficient to only describe the implementation of the operator
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}
in the following.

For every direction
\theta\in\Theta
, we need to discretize the operation
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}
, where
u_{t}
is represented by the vector
\vec{u}_{t}
of function values on the grid
G_{u}(t)
.
The result of the projection
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}
is a measure on
\Xi\subset\mathbb{R}
,
whose domain
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}(\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}(\tilde{\Lambda}))=\mathrm{Mv}^{1}_{t}(\mathrm{Rj}_{\theta}(\tilde{\Lambda}))\subset\Xi
depends on
t
and
\theta
and is again discretized via a one-dimensional equispaced grid
G_{r}(\theta,t)
.
To have a comparable resolution as for the variables
u_{t}
and
\gamma_{\theta}
we pick
G_{r}(\theta,t)
to have the same number
M
of intervals as the other grids have along each direction.
Again, measures on
\Xi
are discretized as piecewise constant functions on that grid.

After discretization, the Radon transform
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}
will be represented by
M_{\mathrm{Rd}}(t,\theta)\vec{u}_{t}
for a matrix
M_{\mathrm{Rd}}(t,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M^{2}}
.
(In a completely analogous manner the move operator
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}
in
\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}\gamma_{\theta}
will be represented by a matrix
M_{\mathrm{Mv}}(\theta,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M^{2}}
).
The entry of the
i
-th row and
j
-th column in
M_{\mathrm{Rd}}(t,\theta)
is given by the relative area of intersection between the strip

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\;|\;r_{i}\leq\theta\cdot x\leq r_{i+1}\}

(where
[r_{i},r_{i+1}]
represents the
i
-th grid cell of the grid
G_{r}(\theta,t)
)
and the
j
-th cell of the grid
G_{u}(t)
(cf.
Fig.

1
).

Finally, we take
M_{\mathrm{Ob}}(t)
to be appropriate matrices discretizing the forward operators
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
on the grids
G_{u}(t)
for all
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
.
In our experiments we consider as forward operator the truncated discrete Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
on
\Omega=[0,1]^{2}
from
36
.
To compute the entries of
M_{\mathrm{Ob}}(t)
we simply apply
\mathrm{Ob}_{t}
to Dirac measures located at the centres of the grid cells in
G_{u}(t)
for each time
t\in{\mathcal{T}}
.

In principle one would now obtain a discretized version of

Π

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

by replacing all variables and operators by their discretized counterparts.
However, the constraints
\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{1}\gamma_{\theta}
should not be enforced exactly in the discretized setting
since this is likely to introduce discretization artefacts.
Therefore we replace them by a bound
\tau>0
on the violation so that the discretized problem reads

\begin{split}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vec{\gamma}_{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{M^{2}},\theta\in\Theta\\
\vec{u}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{M^{d}},t\in\Sigma\end{subarray}}\,\sum_{\theta\in\Theta}\lVert\vec{\gamma}_{\theta}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{t\in\Sigma}\lVert\vec{u}_{t}\rVert_{1}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\lVert M_{\mathrm{Ob}}(t)\vec{u}_{t}-f_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\quad\text{ such that }\left(\sum_{\theta\in\Theta,t\in\Sigma}\lVert M_{\mathrm{Mv}}(\theta,t)\vec{\gamma}_{\theta}-M_{\mathrm{Rd}}(t,\theta)\vec{u}_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\tau.\end{split}

(22)

A coarse parameter search has shown a value of
\tau=0.001
to be a good choice, which we use in all our experiments.
The resulting second-order cone program is solved using the commercial MOSEK solver
[
6
]
called through the Python library CVXPY
[
18
,
1
]
.

5.3
Application to truncated Fourier measurements

As a model super-resolution problem, we consider the case of truncated Fourier measurements
\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
from
36
in
d=2
dimensions, where we set the frequency cutoff to
\Phi=2
.
This setting in particular serves to benchmark our dimension reduction against the full-dimensional dynamic reconstruction proposed by Alberti et al.

Measurement times.

We are interested in evaluating the performance of the method for different numbers of measurement times
{\mathcal{T}}
. For simplicity, we choose times
{\mathcal{T}}\subset[-1,1]
centred around 0 as follows: Given a
K\in\mathbb{N}
, choose
{\mathcal{T}}\coloneqq\{k/K\;|\;k\in\{-K,\dotsc,K\}\}
. The values of
K
used in the following are
K=1,2,3
resulting in 3, 5 and 7 times respectively.

Datasets.

To compare different parameter choices for the algorithm and to benchmark it against other methods, we generate three datasets
D_{3}
,
D_{5}
,
D_{7}
consisting of 2000 particle configurations in
d=2
dimensions, one dataset for each choice of times
{\mathcal{T}}
.
Each configuration within a dataset consists of
4\leq N\leq 20
particles with randomly chosen positions and velocities
(x_{i},v_{i})\in\tilde{\Lambda}
and weights
m_{i}
drawn from the uniform distribution on
[0.9,1.1]
.

We define the minimal dynamic separation of a particle configuration
S=\{(x_{i},v_{i})\;|\;i=1,\dotsc,N\}
as

\Delta_{\mathrm{dyn}}(S)\coloneqq\min_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\min_{i\neq j}\lVert x_{i}+tv_{i}-(x_{j}+tv_{j})\rVert.

Since we are interested in testing the reconstruction methods for a range of separation values, we want configurations with low and high separations to be equally well represented in each dataset. To this end, we use a
rejection-sampling
method adapted from
[
3
]
, which roughly works by drawing configurations from the uniform distribution on
\tilde{\Lambda}
and accepting or rejecting the new configuration based on an acceptance probability. For our datasets this probability is chosen as to make the dynamic separation
\Delta_{\mathrm{dyn}}
uniformly distributed on
[0,0.1]
.

Postprocessing and error measures.

Because static reconstruction will be one of the methods we compare against, we only consider the reconstruction results for the centre time
t=0
, that is, the reconstructed snapshot
u_{0}
.
We use two different error measures to evaluate the reconstruction results.
The first is simply the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence
W_{2,R}^{2}(u_{0}^{\dagger},u_{0})
as defined in
Section

4.1
, where
u_{0}^{\dagger}
is the ground truth as before and
u_{0}
is the measure obtained by placing a Dirac measure at each cell centre of
G_{u}(0)
with weights as given in
\vec{u}_{0}
. The radius is chosen as
R=0.05
to balance transport and mass creation inside the unit square.
The second error measure uses a clustering method:
Since usually the ground truth particle is not exactly representable on the discrete grid, the solution to the discretized problem will typically contain a cluster of positive weights near a true particle position.
Therefore, in a post-processing step, we first set all weights with values below a threshold
w_{\mathrm{min}}=0.1
to zero. Afterwards, we cluster neighbouring non-zero weights and create a list of detected particles, where each cluster is assumed to be a detected particle with its position equal to the cluster’s centre of mass. Now, a configuration of particles is seen as correctly reconstructed if there exists a pairing between true and reconstructed particles at time
t=0
such that the paired particles have distance less than
0.01
and there remain no unpaired particles.

Methods and parameters.

Our goal is to compare the following methods:

•

Static:
We reconstruct
u_{0}
by solving

\min_{u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)}\lVert u\rVert_{\mathcal{M}}+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\lVert\mathrm{Ob}_{0}u-f_{0}\rVert_{2}^{2}

(the fidelity-term version of the static reconstruction problem (
1
)),
disregarding the data
f_{t}
for
t\in{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\{0\}
.
In contrast to (
22
), the discretization of this problem is straightforward.
In practice, we reuse our implementation of the dimension-reduced problem (
22
), setting
\Theta=\varnothing
.

•

ADCG:
We use the implementation of Alberti et al. in
[
3
]
, which applies the Alternating Descent Conditional Gradient method (ADCG) from
[
11
]
to the full-dimensional dynamic formulation

\min_{\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\tilde{\Lambda})}\|\lambda\|_{\mathcal{M}}\quad\text{such that }\mathrm{Ob}_{t}\,\mathrm{Mv}^{d}_{t}\lambda=f_{t}\quad\text{for all }t\in{\mathcal{T}}.

In short, this method is an extension of the well-known Frank–Wolfe method and works by alternating between adding new source points and differentiable optimization of source position and weights.

•

Dimred. low
: Our method with
|\Theta|=3
and
|\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}}|=0
.

•

Dimred. mid
: Our method with
|\Theta|=5
and
|\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}}|=3
.

•

Dimred. high
: Our method with
|\Theta|=10
and
|\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}}|=7
.

The directions
\Theta
for the dimension-reduced methods are chosen as equidistributed points on the right half of the unit circle
S^{1}
with maximum possible spacing (depending on the number of directions),
\Theta=\{\exp({\mathrm{i}}\pi(\frac{j}{|\Theta|}-\frac{1}{2}))\,|\,j=0,\ldots,|\Theta|-1\}
when expressed in the complex plane. The additional times in
\Sigma\setminus{\mathcal{T}}
are similarly chosen after applying the transformation
t\mapsto(1,t)/\sqrt{1+t^{2}}
to map times to points on
S^{1}
.
For the ADCG method we chose the parameters according to the reference implementation as follows:

Description

Value

Maximum iterations in outer loop

100

Maximum iterations in coordinate descent

200

Grid size to find initial guess for new source point

20\times 20

Minimum optimality gap before termination

10^{-5}

Minimum objective progress before termination

10^{-4}

Table 2
:
Parameters for ADCG

Experiments without noise.

In order to evaluate the exact reconstruction properties, we run two experiments.
In both, rather than actually imposing the constraint that the measurements
f_{t}
are exactly matched, we pick a very small regularization parameter
\alpha=0.005
. The size of the grid is chosen as
M=100
.

First, we study the influence of the number of measurement times by
comparing the performance of our “dimred. mid” method for each number of measurement times
|{\mathcal{T}}|=3,5,7
to the static reconstruction. To this end, we run both methods on truncated Fourier measurements of particle configurations from dataset
D_{3}
. Since here we are only interested in the effect of
|{\mathcal{T}}|
independent of the dynamic separation values, we use the dataset
D_{3}
for all choices of
|{\mathcal{T}}|
. Each reconstruction result is evaluated with the clustering method as either correctly or incorrectly reconstructed. For each number of times, the results are split into four groups depending on whether both, one or neither of the methods was successful. The size of the groups is visualized by the area of the green blobs in
Fig.

2
, top.

We see a large increase in the number of correctly reconstructed samples by our (dimension-reduced) dynamic method as the number of measurement times increases from
|{\mathcal{T}}|=3
to
|{\mathcal{T}}|=5
, as expected. Meanwhile, adding two more measurement times (
|{\mathcal{T}}|=7
) seems to have less of an impact.
As expected, we also see that it is very rare for the dynamic reconstruction to fail on a configuration which is correctly reconstructed by the static method. Such cases cannot be fully excluded however, since the additional constraints in our dynamic formulation introduce some numerical dissipation, which sometimes results in spurious or smeared out non-zero weights. These might then be incorrectly identified as clusters by the clustering evaluation.
Since we chose the frequency cutoff of the measurement operator quite low at
\Phi=2
, the reconstruction task is challenging so that the number of configurations on which both methods fail stays large even for
|{\mathcal{T}}|=7
.

Figure 2
:
Top row:
Comparing static reconstruction to our dynamic method with parameters as in dimred. mid, applied to the
D_{3}
dataset with 3, 5 and 7 measurement times (left to right). Results on each configuration evaluated by the clustering method as “correct” or “failed” and divided into four groups per number of measurement times.
Bottom row:
Comparing the correct reconstruction rate of our dimension-reduced methods against static reconstruction and ADCG applied to the full-dimensional model. Datasets used are
D_{3}
,
D_{5}
,
D_{7}
(left to right), particle configurations are binned by their dynamic separation, and the rate of correctly reconstructed configurations, as evaluated by the clustering method, is plotted for each bin.

As a second experiment, we compare the performance of our dimension-reduced method for different parameter choices against the static as well as the full-dimensional reconstruction solved by ADCG. Here, we use the dataset
D_{n}
,
n=3,5,7
for each respective number of measurement times in order to have a uniform sample of configurations across the whole range of dynamic separation values. The resulting reconstruction rates are displayed as a function of the dynamic separation in
Fig.

2
, bottom.

We notice that the dynamic reconstruction method “dimred. low”, which uses only three projection directions to couple the information from multiple measurements, is able to improve upon the static reconstruction by a large margin. Unsurprisingly, all reconstruction methods improve with higher dynamic separation of the particle configurations, since our measurement operator filters out fine scale information.
For all values of
|{\mathcal{T}}|
, higher numbers of projection directions in our formulation (dimred. low to high) result in better reconstruction.
For
|{\mathcal{T}}|\in\{5,7\}
, the full-dimensional model solved by ADCG still beats our dimension-reduced method. Curiously, for only three measurement times, our dimension-reduced models mid and high slightly outperform ADCG for high separations, which might be explained by suboptimal parameter choices for ADCG (such as the grid size for the initial guess of a new source point).

Note that the performance of the static reconstruction method slightly improves with increasing
|{\mathcal{T}}|
for each fixed dynamic separation
\Delta_{\mathrm{dyn}}
.
This is explained by the fact that, on average, for the same dynamic separation a larger
|{\mathcal{T}}|
means a higher particle separation at the single time point
0
.

Experiments with noise.

Finally, we intend to investigate the behaviour of our method with noise added to measurements. In this section, we fix the setting with five measurement times,
{\mathcal{T}}=\{-1,-0.5,0,0.5,1\}
. For a given noise level
\delta
, Gaussian noise with standard deviation
\sqrt{2\delta/250}
is added to the vector resulting from truncated Fourier measurements
\mathcal{F}_{\Phi}
with cutoff
\Phi=2
. Since the size of this vector is 250, this is consistent in expectation with the definition of
\delta
in (
18
).

We are especially interested in supporting our convergence analysis from
Section

4
. This analysis suggests the optimal regularization parameter
\alpha
in (
22
) to scale like the square root
\sqrt{\delta}
of the noise level, thus
\alpha=C_{\alpha}\sqrt{\delta}
for some constant
C_{\alpha}>0
. By a coarse parameter search, we have determined the value
C_{\alpha}=0.2
to be roughly optimal, which we will use for the following experiments.

Two new datasets are generated for this setting: A dataset consisting only of a single particle in each configuration (
N=1
), as well as a second dataset consisting of two particles in each configuration which stay far apart (
N=2
with
\Delta_{\mathrm{dyn}}>0.4
) over the five measurement times. Additionally, the dataset
D_{3}
from before (labelled “
4\leq N\leq 20
” in
Fig.

3
) is filtered to contain only configurations which where correctly reconstructed in the noiseless experiments by the respective methods. All datasets are thinned to no less than 50 configurations per noise level.

We compare the dimension reduced method “dimred. mid” from before, but with grid size set to
200\times 200
, to the ADCG algorithm applied to the full-dimensional formulation. The error measure used is the mean of the unbalanced Wasserstein divergence per noise level.

Figure 3
:
Convergence properties for vanishing noise level for our method applied to datasets with one, two and
4-20
particles in each configuration, as well as ADCG on the latter. Slope
\sqrt{\delta}
provided for comparison.

From our analysis in
65
, we expect to see a convergence rate of the Wasserstein divergence of
\sqrt{\delta}
for our dimension-reduced method.
Figure

3
shows this to be approximately true for the datasets with one and two particles per configuration in the regime
\delta\in[1,100]
. It is not surprising that the curves flatten in the regime of very low noise levels, since our grid-based implementation cannot surpass the limit given by the grid resolution.

We notice that the curve “dimred.
4\leq N\leq 20
”, which corresponds to our method applied to the diverse
D_{3}
dataset, never quite reaches the expected slope of
\sqrt{\delta}
. Our hypothesis is that in this case, errors from the discretization start to degrade the reconstruction result already before the noise level gets into the regime in which the expected rate would be observed. Finally, the full-dimensional ADCG method seems to even surpass the rate
\sqrt{\delta}
before flattening for very low noise levels.

Appendix A

A.1
Alternative Formulations

In the dimension-reduced problem

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

, we employed the product space
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}
for the snapshots and the measure space
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
for the position-velocity projections. As discussed in
37
there are, however, formulations alternative to (
7
) that work with either only product spaces or only measure spaces. The purpose of this section is to introduce and analyse these variants, in particular with respect to their equivalence to

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

.

One alternative is to use the product topology both for the temporal dimension of the snapshots and the angular dimension of the position-velocity projections as follows,

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Gamma)^{\Theta}\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)^{\Sigma}\end{subarray}}\!\|\gamma_{\hat{\theta}}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\!+\!\frac{1}{2\alpha}\!\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|\mathrm{Ob}_{t}u_{t}\!-\!f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\quad\text{ such that }\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}\text{ for all }t\!\in\!\Sigma,\,\theta\!\in\!\Theta,

\Pi
-
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)

where
\hat{\theta}\in\Theta
is arbitrary, but fixed.

Another alternative would be to consider both the snapshots as well as the position-velocity projections as Radon measures on a product space. To achieve this, we need to adapt the measurement operators
(\mathrm{Ob}_{t})_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}
such that an evaluation on
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)
is well-defined. For the moment, let
\overline{\mathrm{Ob}}:\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)\rightarrow H^{|{\mathcal{T}}|}
denote such a generalization (which will be specified in
83
below).
Then the second variant of the dimension-reduced problem reads

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)\\
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)\end{subarray}}\!\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\!+\!\frac{1}{2\alpha}\!\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\|(\overline{\mathrm{Ob}}u)_{t}\!-\!f_{t}\|_{H}^{2}\quad\text{ such that }\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\gamma=\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}u.

\mathcal{M}
-
\mathrm{P}_{\alpha}(f)

The main results of this section are the existence of solutions to

M

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

in
84
and to

Π

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

in
86
, where for the former we require that the Radon operator corresponding to
(\Theta,\mathcal{H}_{\Theta})
is injective. The equivalence of

M

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

and

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

will be shown under the same condition in
85
. Finally, the equivalence of

Π

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

and

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

will be shown in
88
, where we require that the move operator corresponding to
(\Sigma,\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma})
is injective.

At first, we elaborate on the relation between

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

and

M

-

⁢

P
α

(
f
)

. To this end, we will need to decompose
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)
as
u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\>\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
. As the following lemma shows, this is always possible when
u
corresponds to the projection of a full-dimensional measure
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega)
.

Lemma 80

(Measure decomposition with full-dimensional lifting)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)
and
\lambda\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that

u=\mathrm{Mv}^{d}\lambda.

Then there is a unique family of measures
(u_{t})_{t\in\Sigma}
in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

u=u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\quad\text{ and }\quad t\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi\>\mathrm{d}u_{t}\text{ is continuous for every }\varphi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

Regarding existence, we can select
u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{d}\lambda
for each
t\in\Sigma
such that the decomposition follows since by definition
\mathrm{Mv}^{d}\lambda=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}^{d}\lambda{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
.
With this choice it then follows from uniform continuity of any
\varphi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that the mapping

t\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi\>\mathrm{d}u_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x+tv)\>\mathrm{d}\lambda(x,v)

is (even uniformly) continuous. Given any other
\tilde{u}_{t}
with
u=\tilde{u}_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
we must have
\tilde{u}_{t}=u_{t}

\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
-almost everywhere. If
\tilde{u}
in addition satisfies the continuity requirement it follows that
\tilde{u}_{t}=u_{t}
for all
t\in\Sigma
.
∎

Now we aim to obtain a similar regularity result for a measure
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)
with
\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}u=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\gamma
for some
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
.
To this end, we first need a lemma about disintegration that is a direct extension of
33
to the setting where
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)
.

Lemma 81

(Measure decomposition with projected lifting on measure spaces)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma)
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
be such that

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}u=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\gamma.

Then
u
and
\gamma
can be decomposed as

u=u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}

with
(u_{t})_{t\in\Sigma}
a family of measures in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and

(\gamma_{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
a family of measures in
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
. Further,
\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and

\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}=\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta},\quad\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma,\quad\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}u=\mathrm{Mv}\gamma_{\theta}

for
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
-almost every
t
and
\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
-almost every
\theta
.

Proof.

At first, note that
\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}u=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\gamma
implies that

\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}u=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}.

Now using the disintegration theorem
[
5
, Thm. 5.3.1]
, we decompose
u=u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\nu_{u}
and
\gamma=\nu_{\gamma}{\otimes}_{\theta}\gamma_{\theta}
with
\nu_{u}=\frac{1}{\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}}{(\pi^{\Sigma})}_{\#}u
,
\nu_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}}{(\pi^{\Theta})}_{\#}\gamma
being probability measures and
\|u_{t}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\|\gamma_{\theta}\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
for almost every
t
and
\theta
. Here,
\pi^{\Sigma}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Sigma\rightarrow\Sigma
and
\pi^{\Theta}:\Theta\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\Theta
denote the canonical projections.
From this decomposition it follows by a direct computation that

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}_{\theta}u=\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\nu_{u}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma=\nu_{\gamma}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}.

Hence we obtain

\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}{\otimes}_{\theta}(\mathrm{Rd}_{\theta}u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\nu_{u})=(\nu_{\gamma}{\otimes}_{\theta}\mathrm{Mv}_{t}\gamma_{\theta}){\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}.

Evaluating the measures on both sides at
\Theta\times\mathbb{R}\times A
with
A\subset\Sigma
an arbitrary Borel set, we obtain
\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}\nu_{u}(A)=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}(A)
. The choice
A=\Sigma
yields
\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}=\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}
and thus
\nu_{u}=\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
. Likewise, evaluation at
\omega\times\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
for
\omega\subset\Theta
an arbitrary Borel set leads to
\|\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}\nu_{\gamma}(\omega)=\|u\|_{\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}(\omega)
and thus
\nu_{\gamma}=\mathcal{H}_{\Theta}
. This proves the decomposition of
u
and
\gamma
, and the claimed equalities follow
similarly as in the proof of
33
.
∎

Now if
(\Theta,\mathcal{H}_{\Theta})
is such that the Radon transform is injective (see
26
), we can obtain a certain uniqueness of the decomposition of
u
in the above
Section
, even for
every
(rather than
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
-almost every)
t
. As our argument requires weak-
\ast
continuity of the Radon transform, we formulate it for the bounded support setting so that
22
can be applied.

Proposition 82

(Unique decomposition with Radon operators)
.

Assume
(\Theta,\mathcal{H}_{\Theta})
to be such that
\mathrm{Rd}:\mathcal{M}(\Omega)\to\mathcal{M}(\Theta\times\Xi)
is injective,
and let
u\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Omega\times\Sigma)
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\Theta\times\Gamma)
satisfy

\overline{\mathrm{Rd}}u=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}\gamma.

Then there is a unique family of measures
(u_{t})_{t\in\Sigma}
such that

u=u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\quad\text{ and }\quad t\mapsto\int_{\Theta\times\Xi}\varphi\>\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}\text{ is continuous for every }\varphi\in C(\Theta\times\Xi).

Proof.

By
81
, there is a family of measures
u
such that

u=u_{t}{\otimes}_{t}\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Rd}u_{t}=\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma\quad\text{for }\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\text{-almost every }t.

Since
\mathrm{Rd}
is injective, this implies
u_{t}=\mathrm{Rd}^{-1}\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma
for
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
-almost every
t
, where
\mathrm{Rd}^{-1}
denotes the left-inverse of
\mathrm{Rd}
.
To show that this formula can be used to specify
u_{t}
for
every

t\in\Sigma
, we show that
\overline{\mathrm{Mv}}_{t}\gamma
is in the range of
\mathrm{Rd}
for every
t\in\Sigma
